### Foreword

For well over a century the Western Reserve Historical Society has been accumulating manuscripts of historical interest. While arranging these in conjunction with publication of a guide, numerous references to John D. Rockefeller were noted. He had, in fact, been a vice president and honorary vice president of the Society for decades. It occurred to us that a study of these documents might shed new light on this one-time Clevelander. Grace Goulder Izant, Ohio historian, was asked to examine this material and prepare a monograph on John D. Rockefeller and the Western Reserve Historical Society. Our concern was not in an in depth presentation of his extraordinary business career, but rather in a portrayal of Mr. Rockefeller as a human being. During her research Mrs. Izant uncovered much hitherto little known information relating to Mr. Rockefeller’s Cleveland years. Her discoveries led us to believe that a work broader in scope was justified. Mrs. Izant had the opportunity to interview people who had known Mr. Rockefeller. From these and other sources she has been able to evaluate many of the myths and misconceptions that surround the man who during his lifetime seldom answered criticism or accusations, and, indeed, shunned publicity of any sort. Thus the monograph expanded into the present book-length study about a premier American businessman and an individual of unusual dimensions. Pe Were | ON COUR, Deke oP RNY PY. PE Pa

> The Western Reserve Historical Society Frederick C. Crawford, president.

## Page XI

### Preface

This is a chronicle that seemingly is unrelated to the contemporary scene—a story about John D. Rockefeller whose record could have unfolded as it did only in the America of the past century. Those years that this life encompassed, however, left the legacy that makes our today. Rockefeller was a protagonist—in certain respects the protagonist —of that bygone era. For decades the setting for his activities was Cleveland. He arrived in the city as a boy, and for more than sixty years called it home. It was in Cleveland’s early industrial heart, the Flats, where he launched his business. In Cleveland he married and reared his children. Here he went about his everyday affairs, moving in and out of the city as his financial and philanthropic interests broadened, returning eagerly to join his family at their secluded domain, Forest Hill. Today’s Cleveland has half forgotten him and his impact on the locale, although it was his wizardry in oil that quickened the city’s early economy, materially furthering the trend toward prosperous metropolis status. While once he was Cleveland’s most widely known citizen, there now is vagueness as to where he lived and carried on his work; a not surprising fact since practically all Cleveland’s Rockefeller landmarks have disappeared. The house that, as a youth, he built for his parents was razed long ago and likewise his own Euclid Avenue town residence. His Forest Hill estate has been broken up. The vast Standard Oil Company complex that burgeoned from his original refinery is dismantled and its site now vacated. Probably few are aware that Rockefeller and his wife as well as many members of their family are buried in Cleveland. Paradoxically, in the city where the oil magnate is becoming a shadowy legend, much Rockefeller lore exists. A rich source, which I have tapped freely, lies in the recollections of Clevelanders and former residents who were acquainted with the Rockefellers and

<!-- page_id: 0006-left | printed: none | viewer_token: none | viewer_sequence: 14/340 -->
## Unnumbered Page

could provide hitherto little known facts about them. Such data, which Allan Nevins labeled oral history, is particularly revealing in the case of the reticent John D, and his wife whose private activities were so little chronicled. In Cleveland one encounters another kind of Rockefellerana— if such a word be permissable—hoary yarns bristling with prejudice, presenting Rockefeller in unrecognizable, out-of-character roles, and always in as bad a light as possible. Undocumented though these are, they reflect the era when he was assailed in every hand, often with special virulence in Cleveland. In later life the Standard head was lauded almost as extravagantly as earlier he had been castigated. Even critics of certain of his practices came to honor him as an innovator on two fronts: as a precursor of modern industrial methods and as the creator of a model system for the distribution of wealth. Long interested in the Rockefeller saga, Frederick C. Crawford, president of The Western Reserve Historical Society, started me on this trail. Meredith B. Colket, Jr., director of the Society, of which, incidentally, Rockefeller was a lifetime vice president, heartily endorsed the project, giving unstintingly of his advice and time. I take this opportunity also to thank Kermit J. Pike, the Society’s librarian, and Mrs, Virginia R. Hawley, chief research librarian, who put at my disposal the Society’s rich resources related to my subject. In addition to those to whom I have expressed my indebtedness in the Notes, I wish to thank members of the Cleveland Public Library staff; in particular Miss Janet Coe Sanborn, Miss Kathleen Prettyman, Miss Emily Lou Alford and Miss Agnes Hanson. Thanks are due, too, to Daniel R. Porter, director, Ohio Historical Society, Columbus, Ohio; Watt P. Marchman, director, the Rutherford B. Hayes Library, Fremont, Ohio; Mrs. Ann Matthews, the Franklin Sylvester Library, Medina, Ohio; Miss Grace D. Parch and John H. Kamp, the Plain Dealer Library; Mrs. Albert D. Levy, Sterling W. Clements; Warren Guthrie, and Mrs. Margaret Mogyordy both of the Standard Oil Company of Ohio. The privilege of working in the Archives, Rockefeller Family

<!-- page_id: 0006-right | printed: none | viewer_token: none | viewer_sequence: 14/340 -->
## Unnumbered Page

and Associates, New York, is deeply appreciated. I am grateful to the archivist, Dr. Joseph W. Ernest, for his unfailing help and cooperation, and for his suggestions and corrections after he kindly read the manuscript. For guidance and encouragement I am under obligation more than I can express, to Lillian C. Zevin. Special thanks are due to my publisher, Howard Allen. Above all, I want to acknowledge the participation of my husband, Robert James Izant in the entire undertaking from its inception to the final chapters of the manuscript. He did not live to see the published volume. It becomes in a very real sense a memorial to him.

Grace Goulder Great Elm, Hudson, Ohio 1972

## Page 1

### 1. Rockefeller Arrives in Cleveland

Mrs. Woodin was expecting new lodgers, two young brothers, John and William Rockefeller. She was given to moving frequently, but on that autumn day of 1853, she had been maintaining her boarding house on Cleveland’s Erie (East 9th) Street.* It was an important thoroughfare in the fast-growing city and ran north almost to Lake Erie. A few days before, the boys’ father, the affable “Doctor” William Avery Rockefeller, had made the arrangements with her, driving in to do so from his farm on the edge of Strongsville, thirteen miles to the southwest. He and his wife and their five children had been living there near Rockefeller relatives since emigrating from their native New York State eight months previously. The father was coming to Cleveland again, bringing his two oldest sons, John, fourteen, and William, twelve, to enroll them in the city’s Central High School. There was no such school in Strongsville, nor for that matter anywhere else in the Western Reserve, as this part of northeastern Ohio was known. Central was one of the first public high schools west of the Alleghenies and had a fine reputation. Doctor Rockefeller was a “botanic physician,” an itinerant dispenser of cure-alls. He and the boys drove into the city behind a pair of spirited horses, for the Doctor never owned any but the finest animals. His enthusiasm for horseflesh was a trait he passed on to his children and his children’s children. The three Rockefellers most likely followed the Medina-Wooster Pike (Pearl Road) into Cleveland, for it was the direct route. As

* In 1906, numbers were substituted for Cleveland street names, particularly in the case of streets running north-south. Erie Street, for example, became East 9th Street. In these pages, in reference to events prior to 1906, the original street name is given, and when first mentioned the later numeral-name follows in parenthesis, as Erie (East 9th) Street. With events after 1906, the current street name is used, as East 9th Street.

<!-- page_id: 0008-left | printed: 2 | viewer_token: 2 | viewer_sequence: 18/340 -->
## Page 2

the road wound down into the Cuyahoga River Valley it connected with Columbus Street. Though the highway dropped sharply its steep grade had been reduced somewhat for the benefit of Pike traffic. The Doctor nonetheless had to use his brakes as his horses headed down the hill. The travelers crossed the river on Columbus Street Bridge, known as “the first fixed” span over the Cuyahoga ever since it replaced the pontoon makeshifts of pioneer days. As the brothers gazed at the crooked stream they could not guess that a few years later its clear waters would be streaked with oily scum from their refineries on its banks. They were now in the “Flats,” that stretch of bottom land where the Ohio Canal basin lay, and the river twisted its way to the lake. This was Cleveland’s industrial heart, with mills, foundries, noisy docks, wholesale houses, shops and shipyards. Along this lively waterfront, John D. Rockefeller soon would stake his future. The experienced elder Rockefeller knew his way through the maze of crowded streets and lost no time in climbing out of the Valley via Vineyard (Columbus Road) Lane. It brought him to upper Superior Street where Cleveland’s early office building, the Atwater Block, stood at the corner. Water (West 9th) Street was directly opposite, running north to the lake. On the same side of Superior, at the corner of Bank (West 6th) Street the Rockefellers could see the elegant Weddell House, “the Astor House of the Lakes.” Superior Street, lined with business establishments, the mercantile street of the thriving city, stretched eastward, invitingly broad and level. With heights and lowlands behind them, the Rockefellers rolled along at a smart clip. They followed the highway on through Public Square where commerce was invading the earlier homesites, straight ahead to Erie Street and Mrs. Woodin’s. The trip into the city, even for a Knight of the Road like the Doctor, called for stout steeds and a sure hand on the reins. Though summer sun had dried the mud on country highways, ruts and chuck holes remained, and the planked sections likewise left much to be desired. Roadways within the city were as bad. Even Superior, though impressively wide as befitted what the founding

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0008-right | printed: 3 | viewer_token: 2 | viewer_sequence: 18/340 -->
## Page 3

fathers intended to be the city’s main artery, was ill-surfaced and rough. Euclid Street was a quagmire when it rained, and Erie Street was no better. On his first visit Doctor Rockefeller had diligently studied the long column of boarding houses published in the city directory. Only one list was longer—that of the saloons. A convincing reason for selection of Mrs. Woodin’s place was its location. It would be convenient, he saw, to Central High School, which was on Euclid Street near the corner of Erie. He was influenced, too, in his choice when he learned the landlady was a Baptist. She and her daughter Martha, a few years older than John and William, attended the Erie Street Baptist Mission Church, which was further along at the corner of Ohio Street (Central Avenue). Doctor Rockefeller himself gave little thought to church, whatever the denomination. On the other hand, the boys’ mother was a devout Baptist, and the good-natured husband was happy to make this concession to his pious Eliza. Mrs. Woodin’s was probably one of the boxy little white houses typical of the modest dwellings in this part of the city, its dining room and kitchen in the basement, its front door opening onto a stoop that hugged a brick sidewalk. If the boarding house head had misgivings about assuming responsibility for two such youthful charges, she soon would be won over by the genial parent, for he could be a persuasive talker. He was a big, hearty man, twinkling-eyed and well dressed, a russet, cheek-to-chin beard fringing his cheerful face. With his sleek horses stomping at her hitching post, the man’s confident manner and fine clothes, Mrs. Woodin would conclude she was dealing with a person of consequence. When she met the sober, quiet John, she must have been reassured completely. He was tall for his age and clearly on his way to becoming taller. The boy was thin and he might have been described as gangling, except for a certain dignity with which he carried himself. His thick thatch of light brown hair was smoothly brushed and every button and crease on his suit in place. William, chubby and merry, probably followed John into Mrs.

<!-- page_id: 0009-left | printed: 4 | viewer_token: 4 | viewer_sequence: 20/340 -->
## Page 4

Woodin’s parlor—he would always follow John. Like his brother, William was neatly, though not expensively, clad. Their outfits, as those of most boys of the era, were homemade, products of their mother’s expert needlecraft. Their appearance belied the frequent assertion that John D. Rockefeller rose to his pinnacle of wealth from a boyhood of poverty. Mystery beclouds the chronicle of William Avery Rockefeller, an enigmatic personality, admittedly erratic as parent and husband and family breadwinner. Yet on the whole he provided generously for his children. This was testified to years later by the youngest daughter, Mary Ann, at that time the wife of William C. Rudd, a founder of the Chandler & Rudd Company, a Cleveland grocery and specialty shop. She declared: “The stories are ridiculous that we were poor. We were not rich, of course, but we had enough to eat and wear and every reasonable comfort. We used to save money always.” Certainly the father had resources at hand when he was setting up his boys for their Cleveland schooling. For one thing he had to meet a substantial board and lodging bill every week. The price may have been one dollar a week for each boy as was stated by John D., reminiscing at age seventy. However, either his memory was at fault, or Mrs. Woodin shortly afterwards raised her price. At any rate, in 1855 when John started. his personal account book, his famous Ledger A, three dollars per person was the amount he entered for weekly board bills. Whatever the figure, the total would have been substantial for two boys over a considerable time. In addition, the Doctor had enough funds to open an account in a Cleveland bank from which John and William were authorized to withdraw for necessities. He would have kept a sharp eye on his sons during his frequent remedyvending expeditions into the area. Without question they were held to a tight budget with nothing frittered away on nonsense and not a penny wasted. But they were supplied with enough for their needs and had none of the worries bred by poverty. In an era when high school was possible for relatively few, William Avery Rockefeller, for all his oddities, made an effort to give

<!-- page_id: 0009-right | printed: 5 | viewer_token: 4 | viewer_sequence: 20/340 -->
## Page 5

his boys the superior educational advantages available at Cleveland’s Central High School, and he spent a considerable sum to do

so. At fourteen John D. Rockefeller put down his roots in Cleveland. Here he would lay the foundation of his fortune and his philanthropy. For the next sixty years—long after New York City became his legal residence—he called Cleveland home.

### 2. City Sights for Country Boys

The city to which young John D. Rockefeller had come was in a boom period of advancing prosperity and expanding population. It had taken full advantage of its strategic location as a port both on Lake Erie and the Ohio Canal. The man-made waterway, accredited with much of Cleveland’s early growth, was giving way to railroads. Yet the canal still functioned as an important carrier of manufactured goods going out from the city and produce coming in from lush farms in Ohio’s countryside. Railroad building was advancing despite many difficulties connected with capitalization, state charters and opposition in rural and canal districts. The city’s first railway, the Cleveland, Columbus & Cincinnati Railroad, had been completed in 1851 two years before the Rockefellers’ arrival. Clevelanders now could travel by rail to the Ohio River at a speed of thirty miles an hour. Tracks of other lines soon would reach the city, forerunners of a national East-West network that would mark the city’s industrial coming of

age. To two young strangers from a Strongsville farm the Cleveland of 1853 was a city of wonderment. At the lakefront, ships with white sails ballooning in the wind headed gracefully for harbor to discharge cargo and passengers. Side-wheelers and screw propellers, numerous now, churned the waves on like missions. Both were creations of flourishing local shipyards. The canal had its patient

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0010-left | printed: 6 | viewer_token: 6 | viewer_sequence: 22/340 -->
## Page 6

mules that plodded along the towpath pulling snub-nosed barges laden with freight and maybe a voyager or two. Docks were bustling, exciting places where husky men loaded and unloaded all kinds of goods. Sturdy ships brought iron ore from recently discovered mines in the Lake Superior region. Coal from Pennsylvania and West Virginia was coming in on the new railroad lines. Wholesale trade centered in the Flats with warehouses and commission merchants’ offices side by side along Merwin and River Streets, thoroughfares that soon were to be John D.’s bailiwick. Sponsors of the 1852-1853 City Directory, a large volume indeed, claimed that “the incoming flow of raw materials gives Cleveland special qualifications for becoming a great manufacturing center.” The anonymous writer was a true prophet, truer than he knew. Amidst the confusion and racket of this “incoming flow,” bewildered newcomers streamed into the city clutching baggage and herding tired children. “News of Cleveland’s wealth and prosperity had ‘leaked out’ and is attracting men . . . of promise from all parts of the country,” the directory’s editor stated boastfully. Families like John’s and William’s had departed the East for a new life in the vigorous “capital” of the Western Reserve. Other types of immigrants were stopping off temporarily on their way to homesteads elsewhere. “An estimated seventy thousand” within the year had passed through the city en route to the New West. Some came and went by stagecoach. Routes fanned in and out of the city. Cleveland on many accounts was a much used terminal and stop-over point. Thus geographical advantages, plus growing transportation facilities, combined to make the city an important exchange point in the national scene for men and materiel. The 1850 census revealed the city’s population at 17,034. By the time the Rockefellers arrived, three years later, the City Directory put the count at 25,670 inhabitants. The figure was boosted the next year by the annexation of the old, and rival community, Ohio City, across the Cuyahoga River. Busy banks, brokerage offices and other commercial establishments made Bank Street a Cleveland Wall Street. The community was fervently religious with practically every faith represented in the many well-attended churches.

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0010-right | printed: 7 | viewer_token: 6 | viewer_sequence: 22/340 -->
## Page 7

Three daily newspapers circulated: the Plain Dealer, Herald, and the Daily True Democrat, shortly renamed the Leader. A weekly, The Aliened American, the city’s first Negro newspaper, began publication that year. It was edited by William Howard Day, who had earned A.B. and M.A. degrees at Oberlin College. The True Democrat, on the editorial staff of which Day had been employed, noted his venture with a warm editorial: “May all success attend him! May the hour speedily arrive when he and all his kith and kin shall be free as the freest among us.” With the steady population growth, Cleveland pushed to the east, and also southeast. Fine homes were going up along Superior, Prospect and Woodland streets, with Euclid Street looked upon as the most fashionable. Churches followed affluent parishioners, building new edifices farther east. Retail business, hotels, and office blocks, heretofore confined to the district west of the Square, were caught up in the eastward trend. The city had a Library Association, an Academy of Natural Science and two medical schools, one of them near the new Marine Hospital at Lake and Erie streets. Among notable intellectuals from the East, Ralph Waldo Emerson spoke at Melodeon Hall. Jenny Lind, the Swedish Nightingale, had received an ovation at her concert, to be followed by artists like Adeline Patti, starring at the Academy of Music. In November of 1853, P. T. Barnum’s New York company presented Uncle Tom’s Cabin at the Atheneum Theater in the Kelley’s Block. Harriet Beecher Stowe’s sensational novel had appeared in book form only the year before with a popular edition “for the Western trade” published simultaneously by Jewett, Proctor and Worthington at 138 Superior Street. The play was also a success. Twelve thousand Clevelanders paid the fifty cents admission fee (half price for rear seats) during a fifteen-night run. Tempted though they might have been by the lurid advertising broadsides, we can safely assume that John and William Rockefeller did not see the performance; their mother had taught them that attending a theater was sinful and a wicked waste of money. The dream Clevelanders cherished of developing a center of

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0011-left | printed: 8 | viewer_token: 8 | viewer_sequence: 24/340 -->
## Page 8

learning seemed assured this year when Cleveland University opened its new building on the South Side. Hopefully, streets in the neighborhood were given names like Library, Professor, and College. Despite the leadership of Dr. Asa Mahan, who had resigned as president of Oberlin College to head the new institution, it soon closed for lack of financial support. Only the streets and their names survived. Newspaper advertisements reflected a wide variety of community enterprises: J. H. Wade’s office for “Telegraph Wires” in the American Hotel Building; a dentist “setting artificial teeth with a continuous gum fused on the plate;” forwarding and commission merchants handling grain, wool, cattle and hogs; every variety of botanic medicine for sale; dealers in peddlers’ goods; ironmongers and engine makers; homeopathic physicians; railroad car and wheel builders; wagon and harness specialists; stoves for kitchen and parlor; tickets sold for cabins and berths on United States Mail Steamers leaving from New York, bound for California. Cleveland was aglow at night with new gas lamps installed by the Cleveland Gas Light and Coke Company. Officials promised one hundred more would be set up soon to light Euclid Street as far out as Erie Street. Gas was piped into many public buildings where signs warned the unwary: “Please don’t blow out the lights.” Candles were still used in most houses. “Burning fluids” like whale and sperm oil or lard oil gave better light, but were higher priced and far from satisfacotry. While local manufacturers were turning out such products, experiments were going forward in their plants for something better in lighting. Many of these firms would rush into petroleum refining when that revolutionary industry entered the field. Mrs. Woodin evidently required her lodgers to furnish their own light, for John D. Rockefeller’s account book showed regular purchases of “burning fluid.” He would have to be content with this illuminant a few years longer.

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0011-right | printed: 9 | viewer_token: 8 | viewer_sequence: 24/340 -->
## Page 9

### 3. No One Ever Called Him Jack

Cleveland’s Central High School had moved to Euclid Street from its original cramped location in the basement of the Universalist Church on Prospect Street. Now it occupied its own building that had cost $1,200. It was only a temporary structure, replaced a few years later by a larger building. The modest quarters, however, seemed a fine schoolhouse to the Rockefeller boys. And indeed the little building in its setting of tall trees, surrounded by a gleaming white picket fence was “a delightfully pleasant place.” The school lot, purchased for $5,000, was a bit to the west of William B. Se(h)ofield’s land at the corner of Euclid and Erie Streets. Once a forest swamp that he had cleared, it became one of the busiest corners in the city. Scofield (he soon dropped the “h” in his name) built his home here, and also a family hotel, Euclid Place, that he and his wife operated. Guests often included missionaries to the Western Indians. The devout proprietor named his son, Levi Tucker Scofield, for one of the gospel workers. In John D.’s high school days, he and Levi became acquainted, an association that lasted through the years. When Rockefeller acquired his Cleveland estate, Forest Hill, Schofield, by then a successful architect, often played golf with his boyhood friend. On the original Schofield property, the architect, in 1901, erected the Schofield (the “h” now restored) Building, an important office block today. Central High School was founded on the advanced idea of providing free educational training for all boys and girls, “even the poorest, if possessed of talent and application,” and it at once attracted youth of every class. To have been educated at Central High School in Cleveland became a mark of distinction. Among the teachers were the scholarly Andrew Freese, principal, who later in 1853 was also appointed superintendent of all Cleveland schools, and E. E. White. Rockefeller appreciated these men and the training he received under them. “Mr, White treated us like gentlemen—and treated all the boys so,” he often said.

<!-- page_id: 0012-left | printed: 10 | viewer_token: 10 | viewer_sequence: 26/340 -->
## Page 10

At the Cleveland school, as in schools he had attended previously, mathematics was John’s best subject. “I always got mathematics first—arithmetic and algebra,” he said. “Mental arithmetic was a great help later in life.” As an illustration, he told how he had saved $30,000 in a pipeline deal by working out a price in his head while the other man was talking. Mental arithmetic had been stressed at Central High. Anecdotes about Rockefeller’s school days portray him as taking little part in playground sports. When the other boys were engaged in spirited games, he was more apt to be looking on from the sidelines, often keeping score with a notched stick as his tally. At one of the school’s traditional Friday afternoon rhetoricals, he delivered a solemn offering that began “I’m pleased although I’m sad.” His schoolmates in youthful indifference to his feelings quickly nicknamed him “Old-I’m-pleased-though-I’m-sad,” delighted with the shy boy’s discomfiture. Another glimpse of him at this time comes from reminiscences of an early settler, O. J. Hodge, a citizen of some note. When John was half way through high school Doctor Rockefeller moved the family from Strongsville to a house he leased from Hodge on Perry (East 22nd) Street. John left Mrs. Woodin’s to join the family. Hodge described his tenant’s oldest son as “an unassuming youth who showed none of the hilarity often seen in boys of that age. Usually he sat quietly in his chair listening to what was being said.” “Never was rent—$200 for the year—paid more promptly,” Hodge declared, “nor never did I have in all respects a better tenant.” However, before the year was out, the restless Doctor took his family off to a new setting in Parma, and John returned to Mrs. Woodin’s. He was just in time to help her move. The house she selected on St. Clair near Water Street was considerably farther from Central High School than the Erie Street location. Nevertheless, her young boarder had no thought of changing landladies. John took his school work seriously. He wrote out his lessons carefully and signed each paper, “John D. Rockefeller,” a signature he carried over into business. His schoolmates dubbed him “John D.” Later, he would be “John D.” to the world.

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0012-right | printed: 11 | viewer_token: 10 | viewer_sequence: 26/340 -->
## Page 11

No one ever called him Jack. Three of his high school contemporaries were to play major roles in Rockefeller’s future: Laura Celestia Spelman, her sister Lucy and a sturdy boy two years his senior, Marcus Alonzo Hanna. “Cettie,” as Rockefeller called Laura Celestia, became his wife eleven years later. In classes at Central High boys sat on one side of the room, girls on the other. It was not easy for John to make chums of the boys and he was so bashful he probably rarely spoke to a girl. Yet it appears he was quietly observant of Cettie Spelman. Since she and her sister were seatmates, he must have noticed Lucy too. At any rate, Lucy, who never married, made her home with the Rockefellers for most of her life. Mark Hanna and Rockefeller were lifelong friends, pursuing strangely parallel careers in both their business and private lives. Mark’s father, Leonard, and the latter’s brother Robert, had come from Lisbon, Ohio, with one Hiram Garretson. They organized the prosperous wholesale grocery and commission house, Hanna, Garretson and Company on Merwin Street in the Flats. At Central High, Rockefeller was a conscientious student, but neither he nor Hanna was an outstanding scholar. They enjoyed participating in class debates and were often on opposing teams. At their frequent meetings in later years, when both were national figures, Hanna liked to recall these debates and used to declare: “You were a born orator, John!” After finishing high school Hanna wanted to go directly into business. His mother, however, persuaded him to enroll at Western Reserve Academy, a preparatory school for Western Reserve College. It was on the college campus in Hudson, Ohio, twenty-five miles from Cleveland. The gay, gregarious lad was bored in the quiet bucolic village and chafed at the strict academic regime. To liven things up he staged an innocent prank during a solemn program of the junior class. As a result, he left the academy abruptly, and most happily on his part. He clerked for a time at his father’s store; and very soon afterwards Rockefeller went to work at a place almost next door. When Rockefeller was courting Cettie, Mark was wooing pretty

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0013-left | printed: 12 | viewer_token: 12 | viewer_sequence: 28/340 -->
## Page 12

Augusta Rhodes, daughter of Daniel P. Rhodes, prominent citizen of Cleveland’s West Side and a pioneer in the iron ore and coal trade. Mark and Augusta were married the same year as Cettie and John. At his father-in-law’s retirement, Mark took over his holdings, out of which the M. A. Hanna Company eventually emerged. At the same time that Rockefeller was pushing his oil interests to world importance, Hanna was likewise expanding his business domain. Both men amassed fortunes from the enterprises they had started in Cleveland at about the same time. Hanna was active in many areas, one of which was Republican party politics. After gaining prominence in municipal political affairs, he rose quickly to leadership in Ohio Republican ranks. Giving up business for politics, he was chosen chairman of the Republican National Committee and raised the unprecedented sum of $3,500,000 for the party. William McKinley won Hanna’s endorsement for the Ohio governorship and later was groomed by him for the Presidency. Becoming United States Senator in 1897, Hanna raised his voice for corporations like the Standard Oil Company when it was popular and politically expedient to condemn them as monopolies. Although linked in the public mind with Big Business, the Senator nevertheless backed labor measures, such as the workers’ right to organize. Shortly before his death in 1904, a ground swell was underway to name him Republican Presidential nomiged

nee. As to Rockefeller, it is claimed that he was always a staunch Republican and regular contributor to the party. But the tastes and temperaments of the two men were very different. The fun-loving, party-going Senator's role in society as a sportsman and a public personality was one Rockefeller sedulously avoided. Their paths, however, repeatedly crossed and their friendship begun in high school lasted as long as Hanna lived. Rockefeller’s loyalty to Central High School never slackened. In 1896 at the fiftieth anniversary of its founding, he invited teachers and alumni to Forest Hill for the observances. The school by then was on Willson Avenue (East 55th Street) occupying a fine

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0013-right | printed: 13 | viewer_token: 12 | viewer_sequence: 28/340 -->
## Page 13

building designed by Architect Schofield. A notable feature of the structure was a tall clock tower, but a clock had not been installed as intended. For this occasion Mrs. Rockefeller supplied a suitable timepiece and had it placed in the space reserved for it. An elaborate Diamond Jubilee celebration was arranged in 1921 to mark the seventy-fifth anniversary of the school. Newton D. Baker, two-term Mayor of Cleveland and Wilson’s Secretary of War, was principal speaker. A purse of five thousand dollars in gold was collected as a scholarship fund in honor of the veteran principal, Edward L. Harris. Five hundred alumni gathered for the event. Missing were two of the most loyal former students, the Rockefellers—Cettie and John D. Mrs. Rockefeller had died six years before. Her husband no longer maintained a home in Cleveland. Nonetheless, his affectionate regard for Central High School was unchanged. From his New York home he sent William Ganson Rose, chairman of the committee on arrangements, a long, nostalgic letter recalling “our delightful meeting of 25 years ago . . . when we met at our home.” Noting sadly how many members of the class of 1855 had passed away, he continued:

I am proud of my connection with the school and most appreciative of the many fine characters among the boys and girls, so many of whom have made their mark and rendered valuable service to the world. I recall with great pleasure the old teachers, Mr. Freese and Mr. White . . . and I want to feel that so long as life lasts that you will count on me as one of you, though it is now 66 years since that summer day in ’55 when I bade farewell to the dear old school to take up the burden of life’s duties.

A vivid picture survives of what John D. was like “66 years before” when he “bade farewell to the dear old school.” Lucy Spelman, interviewed in 1917 about high school days, described him as having been “studious, grave and reserved . . . never noisy or given to boisterous play.” A characteristic gesture, she remembered, was “hugging his slate to his chest as if it were a precious possession. When questioned in mathematics his answer always was ready in-

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0014-left | printed: 14 | viewer_token: 14 | viewer_sequence: 30/340 -->
## Page 14

stantly.” He had a quick sense of humor, she said, but she did not recall his ever laughing loudly. “He was soberly mirthful,” Lucy explained. “His eyes lighted up and dimples showed in his cheeks when he heard anything amusing.” Laura and Lucy completed their course at commencement July 16, 1855. They were among the ten graduates in the first high school class in Cleveland to receive diplomas. Each pupil read an original oration. The title of Laura’s was “I Can Paddle My Own Canoe.” Lucy chose as her subject “The Mind, An Aeolian Harp.” John D. Rockefeller did not wait for commencement. A few weeks before graduation he quit school. But he was a drop-out with a purpose.

> 4. Not a Wasted Moment

The boy who was to become the richest man in the world was in a hurry to get into business. John D. left high school to enroll in E. C. Folsom’s Commercial College. The school’s announcement in local newspapers, which may have aroused his interest in the first place, declared that it had “a large number of scholars . . . with its recent extensive additions affording young men opportunity for self improvement.” It was located in “fine new quarters” on the top floor of the Rouse Block on the northwest corner of Public Square and Superior Street. The four-story structure erected by Benjamin Rouse was Cleveland’s finest office building. Stores and shops occupied the street level with law firms and insurance companies among the other tenants. The Marshall Building is now on this corner. Tuition at Folsom’s was forty dollars, payable in advance. In Rockefeller’s case the fee presumably was furnished by his father. Principles of banking, methods of business transactions, and general commercial subjects were taught. Bookkeeping, both single and double entry systems, was emphasized. Rockefeller excelled in this drill—he would be in essence a bookkeeper all his days.

<!-- page_id: 0014-right | printed: 15 | viewer_token: 14 | viewer_sequence: 30/340 -->
## Page 15

Penmanship received special attention. And well it might, since all business letters and documents were written by hand in the day when the typewriter was years away and the computer not even a dream. The instruction in penmanship paid off for Rockefeller. All his life he wrote a pleasing, completely legible hand, the letters full formed, uncluttered with curlicues or flourishes. There was no “horsing around,” at Folsom’s, as a former student said in talking about the school. Not one wasted a moment. Occasionally, the young men did allow themselves a little time off on a hot summer day for a swim in Lake Erie. When they could persuade Rockefeller to join them, he would go along reluctantly at the start with little to say. But as soon as they reached the lake, he was the first to jump in, played tag in the water with the rest of them, and seemed to enjoy himself. He was an excellent swimmer. By midsummer of 1855 he completed the Folsom course. According to some accounts he returned to Central High School for commencement and received his diploma with the others. But this apparently is an error—Rockefeller’s name is not on Central High School's list of the ten to whom those first diplomas were awarded. There had been talk of sending him to college, probably to Western Reserve College in Hudson, or to Oberlin. His mother favored the plan, for she dreamed of his becoming a minister—a Baptist minister, of course. Doctor Rockefeller, not drawn to religion anyway, felt that John had had enough schooling. It was time he stepped out into the world and learned to support himself. Armed with a certificate from an accredited business school, he had acquired all the formal academic training he ever was to enjoy. Having focused his attention largely on mathematics and rudimentary sciences, he had been exposed to a singularly limited body of knowledge. Except for appreciation of music, awakened in his early years by his father, there had been little if any stimulus to explore the arts, literature, and the like. Nonetheless, what he absorbed seemed to have been uniquely right for him. With a mind uncluttered by a scattering of interests, his genius for organization could function freely. He would create a new pattern for industry, a new system for distribution of wealth and be acclaimed a bil-

<!-- page_id: 0015-left | printed: 16 | viewer_token: 16 | viewer_sequence: 32/340 -->
## Page 16

lionaire. All that was in the future—though some of it not too many years in the future. When he picked up his business school certificate and shook hands with Mr. Folsom in farewell, John D. was sixteen. Though not quite through with growing, his boyhood was behind him. As a youngster of ten or eleven, before coming with his parents to Ohio, he and a playmate, according to Rockefeller lore, were passing the home of the town’s “rich man.” His companion asked the childish question: “What are you going to be, John, when you grow up?” He is supposed to have answered at once: “A rich man,” pointing to the local man’s house. “As rich as he is—worth a hundred thousand dollars.” Though this may be only a likely yarn, some such objective, even if not fully articulated in his mind, seems to have motivated this prematurely grave boy on the threshold of his lifework. He had determined to stay in Cleveland and find his work here. The brisk tempo appealed to him. He was ready to begin. But before job hunting, he wanted to visit his family. His father could be capricious, and later, Frank, his youngest brother, a trial. Nonetheless Rockefeller’s attitude always was, “my family, right or wrong.” After their short sojourn on Perry Street in Cleveland, his parents had left, probably late in 1854, for Parma. Their house, which was good sized, was on a plot of ten acres watered by Stony Creek. Doubtless the Doctor had spotted it on his trips to and from Strongsville since the Pike passed near the place. Frank, and probably Mary Ann, attended the district school near York Road, not far away. This section now is part of Parma Heights and the municipality’s town hall is on the former school site. John D. had not seen this home and he was eager to do so. There were sure to be chores with which he could lend a hand. . . . A network of stagecoach lines linked Cleveland with outlying districts. John boarded the stage for Parma. The fare was fifty cents.

<!-- page_id: 0015-right | printed: 17 | viewer_token: 16 | viewer_sequence: 32/340 -->
## Page 17

### 5. Prelude to Cleveland

The Parma home was the sixth John had known. The first was a snug, plain dwelling on a fifty-acre farm near the village of Richford, in Tioga County, New York, about twenty-five miles from the Pennsylvania border. Here John Davison Rockefeller was born July 8, 1839, to Eliza Davison and William Avery Rockefeller. On hand to greet the new baby was an eighteen-month old sister, Lucy, named for her grandmother, Lucy Avery Rockefeller. Two years after John’s birth, another boy arrived. He was called William for his father, but without the Avery middle name. Eliza named her first son for her father, John Davison, a well-to-do farmer who lived twenty miles to the north in Niles township. He was an austere, thrifty man and a strict Baptist. Godfrey and Lucy Rockefeller, the paternal grandparents, as well as others of the numerous Rockefeller clan, had homes in the Richford neighborhood. The remote, hilly region was more noteworthy for its scenery than for fertile farm land. Young John lived there until he was four, long enough to imbibe something of the landscape’s charm. He would remember certain details such as the brook that trickled down Michigan Hill and through the dooryard. His father probably had been attracted to the site for its stand of timber, and he also purchased an adjoining one hundred and twenty wooded acres. Lumbering was one of his “enterprises.” He followed no regular trade. He was away from home for long periods, saying little about where he went or what he did. If Eliza knew, she was not one to reveal the knowledge. On the surface, the marriage of William and Eliza appeared an odd match, truly an attraction of opposites: she, a prim slight girl, strictly reared, drawn to lively, ebullient Rockefeller. Or, was Eliza in young womanhood gay and lighthearted, ready for a laugh or two? One tale about the courtship, though it may be apocryphal, would fit such a mood on Eliza’s part and is in keeping with the impish streak in her bridegroom-to-be.

<!-- page_id: 0016-left | printed: 18 | viewer_token: 18 | viewer_sequence: 34/340 -->
## Page 18

A favorite practical joke of William Avery Rockefeller was to pretend he was deaf and speechless. In such a guise when going about as a peddler, he drove up to Farmer Davison’s door. His knock was answered by Eliza’s sister, Mary Ann, so the story goes, while Eliza peered over her shoulder. The stranger, well dressed and good looking, handed them a schoolroom slate on which he had listed his wares and set forth his simulated afflictions. The girls read the messages and Eliza exclaimed something like: “Oh! how handsome he is! If he weren't deaf and dumb I'd marry him!” Much pleased with himself, the young man slapped the reins on his horse and clattered off. In a few days he returned full of laughing explanations of his ruse. He claimed Eliza and she capitulated. Whether she was won by the bizarre improvisation, or wooed in proper fashion in her family’s parlor, Eliza married him in the face of her parent’s stern disapproval. As time went on and the little household on the Richford farm included three small children, the young father was not steadying noticeably. His absences continued, often stretching into weeks, sometimes into months. We can imagine Eliza, a young mother, worried, exasperated, lonely. Yet it appears her love was unshaken for the strange, fascinating man who had chosen her. He would return triumphantly, sure of his wife’s welcome, wearing good clothes, driving a splendid new team, and displaying a fat roll of bills. If where he had been or what he had done went undisclosed, no matter, Obviously, it had been a lucrative experience. Her husband had his good points, undoubtedly appreciated by his wife. He took care of his own, if in an unorthodox manner. For all his irregularities, he paid his bills, was punctual to the minute in appointments, and never touched liquor. Gradually it became known that he was a patent medicine vendor, an herbal doctor. In days when medical schools were few and physicians scantily educated and scarce, there was a place for such a “doctor.” “Doctor” Rockefeller had other arrows in his quiver, such as buying and selling horses. Since reputedly he was an expert trader few persons are likely to have got the best of him. In any case, he always drove or rode thoroughbred stock. If the animals

<!-- page_id: 0016-right | printed: 19 | viewer_token: 18 | viewer_sequence: 34/340 -->
## Page 19

in the Rockefeller barns changed frequently, the latest were certain to be better than those they replaced. In his wanderings William Avery Rockefeller came upon a farm near Moravia at the southern tip of Lake Owasco, one of the loveliest in New York State’s Finger Lakes district. He installed his family there in 1843. This was only a few miles from the Davison farm. Eliza was coming home, as it were, coming with three small children and another on the way. Mary Ann, named for Eliza’s sister, was born in August. Two years later, 1845, the family was complete with the birth of twins, a boy Franklin (Frank) and a girl, whose name in the family Bible was spelled “Francis” and not as usual, “Frances.” The house was improved and enlarged at once by the father, indicating an especially favorable state of affairs. Like the other house, the Moravia home was in the midst of streams and forests, Lake Owasco adding to the scenery. John Rockefeller’s boyhood introduction to the beauties of nature left a permanent impression on him. As he himself indicated, the memory inspired him in later years in development of his country estates, Forest Hill in Cleveland and Pocantico Hills in Tarrytown, New York. And as a little boy of seven or eight, another lifelong trait was implanted—a respect for money. His life confirmed both the educational theory that the earliest years are the formative years, and the worn truism that the boy is father of the man. Eliza was frugal by nature and her husband’s habits made her more so. Once home from his jaunts, the Doctor paid the accounts accumulated in the village store during his absence. But his wife never knew when to expect his return. In the meantime she hoarded the pennies, and she taught her oldest son to do so. She provided him with a blue bowl. It was the depository of any money he earned, including what she paid him for household tasks. The blue bow! was kept on a convenient shelf in the kitchen. John credited his mother with helping him on his first business venture. She turned over to his care an errant hen turkey, telling her son that the chicks when hatched would be his. The boy watched patiently for the shell-breaking, corralled the flighty baby

<!-- page_id: 0017-left | printed: 20 | viewer_token: 20 | viewer_sequence: 36/340 -->
## Page 20

birds, fed them regularly and eventually managed to get the grown fowl to market. Coins from the sale clinked pleasantly as he dropped them one by one into the blue bowl. “To this day I enjoy the sight of a flock of turkeys and I never miss the opportunity of studying them,” he said decades later. He repeated what his family called his “turkey story” many times. He recalled, too, how for three days he had dug potatoes for a neighbor. The sun was blistering hot. The potatoes were put into baskets and lugged to wagons. It was hard work—perhaps too hard for a little boy. The pay was thirty-seven and a half cents a day. One morning a farmer acquaintance of the Rockefellers stopped by to ask if he could borrow fifty dollars. Eliza took John aside and pointed to the blue bowl. The boy emptied it and counted out its contents; fifty dollars and a little more. He agreed to let the farmer have the money—with interest. The man returned it in due time with three dollars and a half besides. A person could make money work for him, John reflected. It was far easier and more profitable than digging potatoes. He soon discovered you could get others to take over what you disliked doing. He hated milking chores. “I can hear Mother now calling ‘John, time to milk the cow!” he said when he was an old man. He inveigled Mary Ann in to doing the work for him, paying her one cent a milking. After a bit she struck for two cents and he willingly met the higher wage. Some thought all this indicated a lazy tendency. Anything but lazy, he was saving his energy for more important matters—a principle from which he never deviated. Eliza passed on to her oldest son a great deal of herself; strong will, unfailing self control, an obsession for order, and an abhorrence of waste. “Waste not, want not,” she reiterated, and the son heeded her. Each had great capacity for patience, an unwavering variety akin to tenacity. The two likewise resembled each other in looks. Photographs of the aging Eliza and of John as he grew older are so much alike as to be startling. John learned early that his mother, far from being a permissive parent, “could be a good deal of a disciplinarian.” Once when she was laying on her birch switch, John tried to explain he was innocent of the misdeed for which she was punishing him. “Never

<!-- page_id: 0017-right | printed: 21 | viewer_token: 20 | viewer_sequence: 36/340 -->
## Page 21

mind,” Eliza retorted, “this will do for the next time,” and she finished the undertaking. One time John and William skated at night on a nearby stream though forbidden to do so after dark. The ice gave way. The two scrambled out and at the same time by means of a long pole managed to save a companion from drowning. Hurrying home, dripping wet and shivering, they proudly reported their heroic performance. Eliza listened unimpressed and applied her switch on both for disobeying her in the first place. Nonetheless, mother and son developed a close, mutually devoted relationship. Serious beyond his years, John tended to shoulder family responsibility to offset his father’s vagaries. Eliza came to lean on him. In fact, the whole family depended on him increasingly with the years. John D. never seemed too busy to respond. Later when Eliza’s father, John Davison, died and left her a small bequest in trust, her oldest son handled the modest inheritance with all the attention he gave his million dollar deals. Toward the end of Eliza’s life, when staying at Forest Hill, she had a sick spell. She began fretting for John one day, keeping it up hour after hour. The family became alarmed and dispatched a carriage downtown for Rockefeller. He left a business meeting, hurried to Eliza’s bedside, and took her hand. “There, there, Mother,” he said. “It’s all right.” Eliza gave a deep, contented sigh, turned over and went to sleep. John D. was equally appreciative of his father, repeatedly declaring he owed him a great deal. The astute parent sensed the opportunities in an economy emerging from the pioneer era. He wanted his boys to benefit, to be “sharp,” especially his oldest son whose ability he recognized. Something of this parent’s aggressive imagination seems to have been passed on to John. Despite his trying ways, the father contributed a joie de vivre that undoubtedly was a pleasant antidote to his wife’s strait-laced regime. His homecomings were hailed with glee by his children who had a deep affection for him. As adults they greeted him warmly whenever his wanderings brought him to their homes. Likewise he captivated his grandchildren on his rare appearances. The vigorous, buoyant man loved the out-of-doors. He taught

<!-- page_id: 0018-left | printed: 22 | viewer_token: 22 | viewer_sequence: 38/340 -->
## Page 22

his boys to swim, to manage a boat, and to skate. John developed real skill in these sports, and followed them until well advanced in years. He retained fond boyhood memories of cutting over the ice on his skates, a sharp wind in his face, and in his pocket a wonderful treat—bread covered with scrapings of maple sugar. Some of his earliest improvements at Forest Hill were directed to the estate’s two lakes, making them suitable for swimming and boating, and in winter seeing that the ice was kept smooth for skating. John and his brothers were good marksmen, drilled by their versatile parent who was an expert with the gun. It was a useful accomplishment in the open country where game could add savory variety to the home menu. The Doctor also trained his sons in horsemanship. He admonished them, whether in the saddle or the drivers seat, to “make the beast know you are the master.” John, all his life passionately devoted to horses, adopted the advice when he had his own stables. The Doctor enjoyed music and seems to have had a natural talent. Once, after an especially long trip, he brought home a melodeon. One can see him reining his horses in the dooryard, his brood trooping out to meet him while he pointed with a knowing grin to the mysterious bulk in the back of the wagon. His fingers were deft on the keys and he encouraged his children to play the instrument. He liked to sing. It would have been a lusty voice that boomed from that robust frame. John acquired a lifetime love of music, traceable without doubt to those early years. He has stated that at one point in boyhood he practiced on the piano for six hours a day. But it was not clear when or where this could have been. Never outgrowing his practical joking, the father played tricks on his children, and admitted it. Something of this teasing streak was passed on to the otherwise serious-minded John. His sister, Mary Ann, ruefully described being a victim more than once in her childhood of her brother's pranks. In Moravia John had his first experience with regular attendance at Sabbath School. Eliza prevailed upon a broad-minded Presbyterian neighbor to let her and her children drive into town with

<!-- page_id: 0018-right | printed: 23 | viewer_token: 22 | viewer_sequence: 38/340 -->
## Page 23

![0018-right image](assets/photos/0018-right-01.png)

him, leaving them off at the Baptist Church while he went on to his house of worship. She required the children to contribute something, if only a penny or two, as the collection plate was passed. John, the monied one, was expected to dip into the blue bowl for his donation. No matter how little one had, it must be shared, their mother contended and she was adamant about it. The comfortable, prosperous interval in Moravia began to change. William Avery Rockefeller had a brush with the law on a serious charge—maybe more than one. Records are vague and rumors unsubstantiated. The little girl twin, delicate from birth, sickened and died. Father Davison, whose regard for his son-in-law had not increased, sued him for a past-due note on a loan of $1,500. Paying it shattered the Rockefeller resources. Gossip about “Big Bill” was bandied about the neighborhood. Life in Moravia could no longer be endured. Eliza and her husband gathered their offspring and their belongings and drove off to Owego in Tioga County, the county where their three oldest children were born. For the first time they were not on a farm, but on the edge of a town. Yet their setting as usual was one of natural loveliness. When John was eleven, he and William entered the highly rated Owego Academy. John thought the lessons difficult and the teachers “harsh.” The frequent uprootings had put him behind children of his own age, but he appears to have had little trouble keeping up in mathematics. His father’s affairs for the time being were at low ebb. Nonetheless, he contrived to pay his sons’ tuition. He was giving his children the best “book learning” he could. They, in turn, walked the three miles to and from school. In warm weather, like the other pupils, they arrived barefoot with their shoes slung over their shoulders to save wear. Doctor Rockefeller’s roamings had led him over the mountains into Ohio, maybe more than once, and possibly farther. He caught the western fever. The uncrowded lands beckoned, promising him, as they had countless others greater scope for himself and family.

<!-- page_id: 0019-left | printed: 24 | viewer_token: 24 | viewer_sequence: 40/340 -->
## Page 24

The Rockefellers left Owego late in 1852 or early 1853. There are conflicting statements as to whether they traveled by train or via the Doctor's usual means of transportation, horse and wagon. Ostensibly they were heading for Cleveland, a city whose fame was spreading. However, they went first to Strongsville, a few miles from Cleveland, in the same county—Cuyahoga. They boarded with the Doctor’s sister Sara Ann, wife of Willis Humiston. According to Strongsville tradition, the Humiston residence was on Wooster Pike near Whitney Road. How Eliza fared amidst her in-laws is not known. On the other hand, her husband soon found cronies who took him to turkey shoots and on hunting forays. They admired his prowess with a rifle for they were good shots themselves. Many could remember the Great Hinckley Hunt of 1818 in neighboring Medina County. Fathers of some had joined men of other settlements in that bloody orgy when hundreds of defenseless wild animals were ambushed and exterminated. After a bit the Rockefellers set up housekeeping on a small farm on the outskirts of Strongsville. Big Bill’s new friends, impressed with his ready cash, were curious as to its source. The only answers they received from him were sly winks and hearty guffaws. One of them, on an errand in Richfield, a nearby village, stumbled on the explanation. He was attracted by a small crowd loitering around a spring wagon. A sign propped against the wheels proclaimed that “Dr. William Avery Rockefeller” was in town selling his “Celebrated Cancer Treatment for $25.” And there, sure enough, was Big Bill himself in a frock coat and silk hat, holding his bottle aloft, discoursing eloquently on its efficacy. The man’s report lost nothing in the recounting once he was back in Strongsville. The Doctor hawked other nostrums (or, were they the same formula as the Cancer Cure?)—guaranteed remedies for a series of maladies, all probably equally harmless and ineffective. One wonders if his assortment included vials of Seneca Oil widely used for the aches and pains of man and beast. It was a dark, greasy substance made from petroleum, better known as “rock oil.” After what probably were none too comfortable quarters in Pn

<!-- page_id: 0019-right | printed: 25 | viewer_token: 24 | viewer_sequence: 40/340 -->
## Page 25

Strongsville, it is likely Eliza enjoyed the months spent in the house they had rented from O. J. Hodge on Cleveland’s Perry Street. There, however, her husband’s wanderlust surfaced again. She well may have resented leaving the house for another move to an unfamiliar place. But she packed up for Parma, and went along. It was this Parma house that John went to visit after finishing his business school training. The Cleveland-Parma stagecoach that John had taken was routed along the Pike and put him down practically at his parents’ front door. It would be a safe conjecture that his mother had been listening for hours for the stage driver's long horn heralding its coming. John spent a month in Parma. As he prepared to leave for the Great Adventure, his search for work, his father observing the youthful confidence, tried to cushion him against possible disappointment. If no employment was forthcoming in the city, he told his son, he need have no fear about making a living—he always would be able to find work in the country.

## Page 26

little church, taking part in the young people’s meetings and Bible classes. John admired him and they saw a good deal of each other. All in all, getting reestablished at Mrs. Woodin’s was like a homecoming for the sixteen-year old youth. His compelling objective was to locate a commercial concern in need of what he had to offer: a knowledge of bookkeeping and good penmanship. He had attained man’s height, but not his girth. As he set out on his quest, his suit, a bit worn perhaps, was somber but well-pressed. A black cravat was wound around his neck in the current mode, shoes polished, hair in place, and on his head, doubtlessly, a narrow-brimmed derby hat. He did not carry a letter of introduction to a company head, for he knew no Cleveland businessmen. And certainly none had ever heard of John D. Rockefeller. He turned to the City Directory and from it copied a list of the important firms. It was like him to consider only those of high rating and to ask to see their chief officials. Day after day the earnest, slender youth tramped the city streets in the humid summer heat. No one wanted a young man who could keep books and who wrote a good hand. Cleveland was feeling the effects of “hard times.” Doggedly he returned to business places to re-canvass merchants on the chance that an opening had developed since his first inquiry. When his courage dropped to its lowest point it was restored by the memory of his father’s well meant parting words about farming. Anything but that! And he would try another door. The perseverance and determination that were to mark all his later dealings won out. After a month of turndowns, as Rockefeller wrote nearly seventy years later, “one man on the docks told me that I might come back after the noon meal.” John departed in dignified manner, but once out of sight of the office, he skipped along the street in boyish exultation. He rushed to Mrs. Woodin’s to tell her the good news. “I was elated but in a fever of anxiety lest I should lose this opportunity,” he said years later. After what seemed to him the proper time, he presented himself again and heard the thrilling words: “We will give you a chance.”

<!-- page_id: 0020-right | printed: 27 | viewer_token: 26 | viewer_sequence: 42/340 -->
## Page 27

Nothing was said about wages. The man who hired him was Henry B. Tuttle, of Hewitt & Tuttle, a wholesale commission and produce company. Their headquarters was beside the Cuyahoga River on Merwin Street. The street had been laid out by Noble B. Merwin, pioneer landowner, whose handsome home and tavern, the Mansion House, had been a popular hostel in Canal days. Its site when the two Rockefeller boys arrived in Cleveland was occupied by the Atwater Block. Tuttle and the senior partner, Isaac L. Hewitt, were well known in Cleveland, especially Hewitt. Both had business interests in addition to their commission house. Hewitt owned downtown real estate, including the Hewitt Block which was occupied by shops and small industries. He was a founder of the Cleveland Iron Mining Company which had been organized five years before, one of the first operations to bring out ore from the Michigan wilderness. Tuttle later joined the mining company and became its secretary. Hewitt, a pleasant, friendly man, introduced John to the office routine, explaining he was to assist Tuttle with the bookkeeping. The boy took off his coat, hung it on a peg, carefully, to avoid wrin- Kes, and topped it with his hat. He slipped on a pair of black cotton sleeve guards as he had learned to do at Folsom’s. His employer pointed to the high bookkeeper stool. The new clerk climbed up, hooked his long legs in the rungs, and felt like a king on a throne! Hewitt pushed the letter press toward him, and John went to work. That memorable day was September 26, 1855. Rockefeller observed the date every year for the rest of his life. He considered it as significant as family birthdays and Christmas. On each anniversary of that September day the American flag was flown at Forest Hill. It was only a few miles from Merwin Street. Young Rockefeller gave all his energy to the demands of his first job. The only diversion he permitted himself was his church. All his social as well as his religious activities centered there. The Baptist faith was to become the keystone of his life. J

The Erie Street Baptist Church was the outgrowth of a mission organized in 1850 by the wealthy First Baptist Church, which was located on Seneca (West 3rd) at Champlain Street (present sit of the Terminal Tower). The parent congregation hoped thus “to meet the religious destitution” accompanying the increase in population in the Erie Street neighborhood. As a first step, a lot at Erie and Ohio Streets was acquired. A small, quaint building, once home of the Second Presbyterian Church on Rockwell Street, was purchased and moved to the new property. The cost, $2200, included the moving and restoration expenses. Within a year, under a dynamic young leader, Rev. J. Hyatt Smith, the congregation felt strong enough to function as a full-fledged church, independent of the mother institution. Among the parishioners of the modest house of worship, there soon would be a lad named John D. Rockefeller. The young man was a faithful attender at all sessions, the three Sunday services, midweek prayer meetings, and the socials. He often was accompanied to church by Eyears, a more frequent visitor at Mrs. Woodin’s as the day for his marriage to Martha approached. When John Rockefeller decided to take the step of formal baptism, Alfred Eyears joined him in the rite, the two being immersed in the baptismal basin by Deacon Alexander Sked. A Scotsman and a florist, the deacon gave the same tender care to the young men in his church as to his flowers. While John was in high school he had enrolled in Deacon Sked’s Bible class, and before he left school, when about fifteen, he succeeded him as its teacher. Among Rockefeller’s first friends at the church was Henry Chisholm, some years older than he and recently come from Scotland. At first engaged in a profitable business building piers and docks, he was soon to establish the Chisholm and Jones Company, iron manufacturers, in the proud little village on the edge of Cleveland called Newburgh. Rapidly expanding, before long the mill was em-

<!-- page_id: 0021-right | printed: 29 | viewer_token: 28 | viewer_sequence: 44/340 -->
## Page 29

ploying more than one hundred and fifty men, and the first blast furnace, in this area was erected. In 1863 Chisholm organized the Cleveland Rolling Mill (later the American Steel and Wire Company). He and his wife, like the Rockefellers, continued as dedicated members of the church, the two families carrying on their friendship through the years. John’s brother William soon joined the little mission. Another member was Benjamin Rouse, whose block on Public Square housed Folsom’s business school. Rouse and his wife Rebecca, one of Cleveland’s notable women, had come to the city as agents for the American Sunday School Union. They lent energetic support to early religious temperance and charity movements and helped found the Cleveland Orphan Asylum. This institution provided the first dependable care for the city’s indigent homeless children. Communicants of the Erie Street outpost were largely in modest to poor circumstances. They included clerks, shopkeepers’ assistants, artisans and the like. The denomination’s more prosperous adherents assembled at the First Baptist Church, the first house of worship in Cleveland to have a steeple. Such pretentiousness did not attract the young assistant bookkeeper at Hewitt & Tuttle. In the humble edifice, the boy had a satisfying sense of belonging. Every Sunday saw him settled in one of the straight-backed pews on the men’s side of the austere meeting room. How limited a budget the Erie Street Baptists operated on is suggested by excerpts from trustees’ records for the year 1855:

So great was the difficulty in raising the necessary operating funds that “the young people were required to defray the expenses of heating and lighting the lecture room for their weekly prayer meetings.” So, John D. ## Page 30

in the corner. Without doubt it was a bare, grim little room, uncarpeted, not brightly lighted for candles cost money, nor overly warm since fuel was expensive, too. His fellow churchmen, recognizing the newcomer’s devotion and dependability, shortly appointed him clerk. Before he was eighteen they elevated him to trustee. The reticent lad took his place beside church elders, all considerably older than he. They were ready to give respectful ear to his suggestions, always offered with deference and few words. A heavy financial burden hung over the parish, a mortgage of $2,000, long past due. Keeping abreast of interest payments alone was a struggle. The debt had worried Rockefeller, and soon a crisis was at hand. The mortgage holder, A. A. Stafford, was pressing for his money. One Sunday moming the minister announced from his pulpit that the creditor threatened foreclosure and this, notwithstanding the fact that he was a deacon of this church. The young trustee, as he himself has stated, stepped into the emergency, assuming responsibility for discharging the obligation. He stationed himself at the church door after service and buttonholed worshippers as they left. Pleading with each to give something, he gathered contributions then and there. Amounts for the most part were ten, twenty-five, fifty cents, rarely as much as a dollar, with promises of sums not much larger. He cajoled, and, as he admitted, “almost threatened.” He followed up his campaign with thoroughness. Equipping himself with a notebook in which he entered each pledge, he then went about collecting them. It was a tedious task that lasted for weeks and was carried on after a long day at his office post. Collections finally added up to the desired $2,000. The church was saved. He looked back on the accomplishment as training in what he termed “begging.” It was useful, he liked to say, for it helped him when he had to beg at banks for loans in the years ahead. “I contributed what I could,” he stated, but he regretted it could not have been more. “My first ambition to earn more money,” he added, “was aroused by this and similar undertakings in which I was constantly engaged.”

## Page 31

### 8. Ledger A

For John D. Rockefeller, landing his first job did not mean earning money. He acted as assistant bookkeeper at Hewitt & Tuttle’s from September 26, 1855 until the end of the year with no word about salary between his employers and John himself. “When January arrived,” Rockefeller related, “Mr. Tuttle presented me with fifty dollars, which no doubt was all I was worth, and it was entirely satisfactory.” At the same time Tuttle told the boy that from then on his wage would be twenty-five dollars a month. It was obvious, however, that he had not been without funds before receiving the fifty dollars. William Avery Rockefeller was the probable source, or was there also a residue from some sort of a blue bowl cache? At any rate, he did have some money and it had to be accounted for, every penny of it. Shortly after going to work at Hewitt & Tuttle’s he bought an inexpensive notebook, ruled it according to the best bookkeeping principles and lettered on its cover Ledger A. That journal, not much larger than an ordinary pocket notebook, and unpretentious as to paper and binding, is a treasured item in the Rockefeller family archives at Rockefeller Plaza, New York. In Ledger A the fledgling businessman recorded the money he received and spent and saved. The little book provides a picture as unstudied and intimate as a candid photograph of John Davison Rockefeller at sixteen, on his own and unknown in the big city of Cleveland. He was about to step into a career that would make him the most famous person ever to live in that city. This journal was the first of a succession of similar personal account books he maintained throughout his life. Before he began tallying his dollars and cents, he set aside a section labeled “Records.” Under the important date of September 26, 1855, with what must have been a triumphant glow, he penned the first item: “Commenced business as bookkeeper for Hewitt and Tut-

tle, Merwin Street, Cleveland, Ohio, after graduation from E. C. Folsom’s Mercantile College, Cleveland, Ohio.” Next, dated December 27, he wrote: “Miss Susan Sked married to Mr. David Austen at Father Sked’s house.” Was Rockefeller a guest at the wedding? One wonders. His wardrobe required attention. It probably was fairly well worn and, since he was still growing, some of it must have been too small for him. As he pointed out, he kept his attire within his means in the early years, but his dress was important to him. Throughout his life, as photographs attest, he was fastidious about his appearance. His first expenditures, as specified in his new account book, were headed “Clothing.” On November 24th he purchased:

> I pare (sic) drawers 1 silk scarf 2 wollen (sic) undershirts 1 pare fur gloves

> $1.50 1,50 2.25 2.50

It is clear from the above that he had cash to spend and that his various schools had been woefully lax in teaching him spelling! The silk scarf, and particularly the fur gloves, seem an extravagance and so were viewed by Rockefeller himself as he puzzled over the entries years later. “I thought I used to wear mittens,” he mused. Actually, there was the notation of a small sum spent for mittens. Those fur gloves could have been a welcome protection for a bookkeeper’s fingers against Lake Erie’s winter winds. They might have functioned, also, as a kind of status symbol for a littlenoticed youth struggling ‘to get on in a new job. To have is to share. The adage had been ingrained indelibly on John’s conscience by his mother. The day following his indulgence in outfitting himself he ruled up another column, “Donations,” a reckoning of what he allocated to his church:

He contributed “to the poor in the church” ten cents every week. Like amounts frequently were given “to a poor man in the church” and to “a poor woman in the church,” evidently currency he handed personally to the recipients. His largest single donation was one dollar paid out several times for slip rent. Continuing this careful accounting from December to April, he added up his “Donations”: } $5.88. He greeted the new year, 1856, by sallying forth to Cleveland’s emporiums for some further cautious investments: January 2 2% yards cloth for pants $3.13 January 5 making same 2.25 January 5 1 pare rubbers 1.25 In February he needed only “1 pare shoes” for $2.25. He selected a “Shirt Collar” for thirteen cents and a “Coat Button” for eight cents. Total clothing costs for the four months came to $9.09. Except for the interesting purchase of the fur gloves, he had given away almost as much as he had spent on necessary clothes in the same period. True, beginning in January 1856, he received a salary of twentyfive dollars a month. However, he had to juggle his entire budget against the fact that practically half of his income went to Mrs. Woodin for lodging, entered in his account book week after week at three dollars. He was completely self-supporting now—and he even managed to save a few dollars, here and there, duly noted in his book. He indulged himself in no new wearing apparel until October came with the threat of winter, when he purchased an overcoat for ten dollars. Vanity now conquered the otherwise staid lad: he bought “1 pare cassimere (cashmere) pants at $6.50.”

This fruit remained a favorite with him. It is said he kept a sack of apples on his bedroom window sill at Forest Hill and at his Fifth Avenue home. A couple of times he bought ten cents worth of candy. Twice he loaned “A. Pinney,” the underpaid minister, two dollars, and after a bit, A. Pinney’s name duly appeared on the credit side. The preacher paid his debts! Mrs. Woodin took care of her boarder’s “washing.” He put down in detail the kind and number of “pieces” with her charge for each. His landlady handled a good many garments for him, and her bill for six months of tubbing was $4.22. Payments of from five to ten dollars were credited from time to time to “H & T,” indicating, it would seem, that the monthly stipend was not forthcoming in a lump sum.
