## Page 35

could count on. Consequently, he increased his gifts for Sabbath School, Foreign Missions, Mite Society and the like, from five and ten cents to double the sums. Sixty cents went for expenses of a “Young People’s Meeting.” As church clerk, he had supplied stationery as well as postage. Systematically he noted each week on the debit side: paper, 15, stamp, 05, etc.” After a few weeks he decided not to hold the church longer for the obligations, and he wrote across the entries, “Let it go!” Young Rockefeller keyed his philanthropy to his financial condition. He continued the policy as the years transformed pennies into dollars and dollars into millions in his business and benefactions. In no sense did he scale his charity to tithing, for such a practice, he made it clear, had no appeal for him: “that one-tenth of a man’s income must be devoted to good works . . . is but a rough yardstick to go by. To give a tenth of one’s income is well nigh an impossibility for some, while to others it means a miserable pittance.” | His philosophy was expressed in a much quoted statement in a speech he made before a young men’s class in New York: “I believe it every man’s duty to get all he honestly can, and to give all he can.” Though pay at Hewitt and Tuttle might have been somewhat irregular, Rockefeller in his Reminiscences wrote of his first job: “I thoroughly enjoyed the work. My father’s training, as I have said, was practical, the course at the commercial college had taught me the rudiments of business and I had a ground work to build upon.” His employers evidently were not unaware of such qualifications in their assistant bookkeeper. At the end of 1856 Hewitt promised him a promotion and a pay raise for the coming year.

## Page 36

### 9. “Mr. Rockefeller”

Tuttle quit the firm of Hewitt & Tuttle, and the assistant bookkeeper, John D. Rockefeller, was given his job without the title of partner, or the income. For some time he had been carrying more and more of Tuttle’s work, so no breaking-in period was necessary. Tuttle, though a partner, had preferred a regular wage to depending on company profits and he was paid $2,000 a year. Salary of his successor was $500, shortly raised to $600. Activities of the commission house were diversified, providing Rockefeller with valuable opportunities for varied experiences. He came to know jobbers, commission merchants and producers. Shipments handled by lake, canal, and railroad gave him insight into transportation routines and put him in touch with vessel owners, lake captains, canal and railroad operators. Complications often arose, as for example, determining a carrier’s proper share in cases of loss or damage. “It taxed all the ingenuity of a boy of seventeen to work out this problem to the satisfaction of all concerned,” said Rockefeller looking back on this period. Railroads were still more or less of a novelty and they appealed to him particularly. Young as he was he was keen enough to sense a significance for the future in the steadily increasing number of lines coming to Cleveland. With the company and Hewitt himself controlling a number of rental properties, rent collection was a regular item of business. When payments were slow, Rockefeller was assigned to go after them. He also took on produce bills long overdue. Calling on each tardy debtor he pressed determinedly for settlement. If the offending party was located at a distance, he would drive out in the little cart Hewitt put at his disposal, tie up his horse, and settle himself in the individual's office obviously prepared to stay until the money was in his hand. He made his points briefly, keeping his voice low,

<!-- page_id: 0025-right | printed: 37 | viewer_token: 36 | viewer_sequence: 52/340 -->
## Page 37

his poise and his patience intact. When he turned his unfaltering blue-grey eyes on his debtor and said firmly: “Mr. Hewitt has waited long enough,” the man might grumble a bit, but finally gave in. The bookkeeper scrutinized all statements submitted to the commission house, checking every item before paying out “my employer’s money from the till.” He was shocked at the careless practices that he observed in a neighboring business house. He happened to be there one day when a plumber presented a bill “about a yard long,” as he described it afterwards. “This neighbor was one of those busy men,” Rockefeller said. “He merely glanced at the tiresome bill and ordered his bookkeeper to pay it.” By coincidence, Hewitt & Tuttle employed the same plumber. The man presented a statement a few days later for work on their premises. Rockefeller was ready for him. He went over every bit of the bill and discovered some errors. Though amounting to only a few cents, he insisted on correction and considered it “greatly to my firm’s interest to do so.” Cleveland in 1857, like most of the nation, was caught in an economic depression that saw banks fail and mercantile establishments close. The recession was to continue until an upswing came with the Civil War’s demand for goods and services. William Avery Rockefeller seemed unaffected by the financial slump. Actually, it was a period of stability and prosperity for him. He gave up his place in Parma and brought his wife and children to Cleveland, settling them in a comfortable house at 35 Cedar Street. He rented it from William Gabriel, a blacksmith, later a carriage maker. The house was large enough to accommodate them all, including John. And it had the great distinction for that day of an inside toilet and bathroom. John had stayed with Mrs. Woodin as she shifted her boardinghouse from Erie to St. Clair Street, to Hamilton and finally to Miami (East 6th) Street near the new Market House. Now that he lived at home, he paid board at the same rate as at Mrs. Woodin’s. The Rockefeller brood was growing up: Lucy, almost nineteen, preparing to marry Pierson Briggs; William, soon to graduate from

Central High School; Mary Ann, a plump fourteen; and Frank, at eleven, already impatient under discipline, but an attractive youngster. Good likenesses of the family were made at this time. Photography was coming into vogue. A dozen studios, including some still making daguerrerotypes, were opening up along Ontario and lower Superior Streets. One proprietor, James F. Ryder, was to win international fame through the work of his protégé, Archibald M. Willard, and his painting, “The Spirit of ’76.” Parents and all five Rockefeller children sat several times before the cameras of these early operators. The young people, stiffly posed, with solemn, tense expressions, seem older than they were. John sits very straight in his chair, a hand on each knee, no hint of a smile or boyish merriment on his sensitive face. They were well dressed, but in a style seemingly more suitable for middle age than for youth. The senior Rockefeller was listed in the City Directory for 1857 at the Cedar Street address as “Dr. Rockefeller.” In the classified section under Physicians the word “botanic” was added in parenthesis after his name. Doctor Rockefeller, now openly a dispenser of herbal medicines, dabbled in other gainful projects. His absences from home, though less frequent, were still of long duration. Doubtlessly he continued his buying and selling of horses. He also was a money lender, a respectable pursuit in that era and a profitable one. John was accustomed to arrange loans for his father. One was to Hewitt for $1,000. The date for its repayment came and went. Though the father importuned his son to ask Hewitt for the cash, the boy, understandably, felt at a disadvantage in approaching his employer on the matter. The father, feeling no such hesitancy, came brusquely into the office one day. He faced Hewitt and demanded his money. Hewitt forthwith paid the debt. “When Father looked at a man like that, the man was apt to do what he told him,” Rockefeller said of this incident which he had witnessed. He confided in Mrs. Woodin details of these transactions, She deplored the high rate of interest involved—ten percent. “We,” Rockefeller related, “had frequent and earnest arguments on the

<!-- page_id: 0026-right | printed: 39 | viewer_token: 38 | viewer_sequence: 54/340 -->
## Page 39

subject . . . But all the arguments in the world did not change the rate and it came down only when the supply of money grew more plentiful.” It would be interesting to know what profits came to the shrewd son from the loan deals he handled for his equally shrewd father. Such arrangements would be in keeping with the practices of both. Young Rockefeller was engaging in some ventures of his own on the side, such as buying and selling consignments of flour, grain, and pork. He kept close track of price fluctuations, methodically recording costs and profits in his account book. Hewitt’s bookkeeper was becoming known up and down the busy riverside. The tall, still very thin lad was at his place by six-thirty every morning, often staying after the warehouse was closed for regular business. In winter’s pre-dawn and early twilight, a passerby could see him on his high stool beside the window, bending over the blotter and the letter press in the light of a whale oil lamp, or maybe a candle. Through the day he was frequently in and out of the office on Hewitt’s affairs, but he had no time to loiter or visit with other young clerks. He did, however, strike up an acquaintance with Charles Farrar Browne, a Plain Dealer reporter, five years older than John D. and often on the docks to pick up financial news. Soon adopting the pseudonym Artemus Ward, Browne began writing daily whimsies to the delight of his newspaper’s readers, including the young bookkeeper. To the latter, Browne was “always funny,” even when gathering produce reports. Somehow he broke through Rockefeller’s reserve. Artemus Ward, to be called America’s first humorist, left Cleveland for New York, where his books of quips and drolleries won him ardent fans, among them President Lincoln and John D. Rockefeller. Rockefeller was an equally enthusiastic follower of the one-time reporter's books and often spoke of their Cleveland association. When he was with Hewitt & Tuttle, Rockefeller came upon the biography of Amos Lawrence, a wealthy New England textile manufacturer who made a practice of giving away crisp new bills in one to ten-dollar denominations. While in no sense a bookish person,

## Page 40

Rockefeller read the volume avidly. The teen-age clerk was greatly impressed with Lawrence’s largesses and, according to tradition, resolved to do something similar when he was rich, as he fully expected to be. It has been said that the Lawrence story prompted Rockefeller’s habit years later of handing out shiny nickels and dimes. At the time this seemed a harmless diversion, but in retrospect it was unfortunate. The public too often now associates Rockefeller with disbursement of those small coins rather than the many millions he distributed for the betterment of human kind. At the Merwin Street commission house, the hard-working youth became a man without tarrying in boyhood. He gave few an opportunity to know him well, but he soon was regarded with marked respect. Seasoned merchants on the waterfront found themselves addressing the eighteen-year old bookkeeper as “Mr. Rockefeller.” His sagacious father, aware of his son’s capability, approached him at this time with a proposition that he build a house for the family. The Doctor explained that he had bought a lot on Cheshire (East 19th) Street and assured him adequate funds were on hand. Whether John drew up the plans or not is uncertain, but he was in full charge of construction. Gathering estimates from eight contractors, as he indicated in his ledger, he selected the lowest bidder. A fine brick residence, substantial in every detail, went up. Mary Ann, in an interview years later, declared the builder lost money in the transaction, and this troubled her. It was the best house the family had lived in, and they made it their home for many years. The William Avery Rockefellers had attained respectability and security. Coming into quiet waters at long last after twenty tempestuous years, Eliza Rockefeller basked in the serenity, especially appreciative of the fine home her John had built for them. She attended services at the Erie Street Church and participated in various programs there. The Society for Women elected her vice president. Except for her Baptist associations, she probably had few contacts outside her home. There is no evidence that she was drawn into

<!-- page_id: 0027-right | printed: 41 | viewer_token: 40 | viewer_sequence: 56/340 -->
## Page 41

civic causes like the abolition movement then surfacing in Cleveland, and supported by many churches. The city was the final “station” on the “Underground Railroad” from which fugitive Negroes could embark on Canadian-bound vessels. Trembling blacks climbed the steep belfry stairway of St. John’s Episcopal Church on the West Side to await signals for their freedom voyage. Many others were secreted in Cleveland homes. The bell on the Stone (Old Stone) Church on Public Square tolled to sound alarm whenever slave hunters, frequently in the city, were sighted. As evidenced by editorial viewpoints of two Cleveland newspapers, sentiment in the city was divided over emancipation. The Leader, vociferously anti-slavery, stood for immediate abolition. On the other hand, the Plain Dealer advocated “gradual extermination” of slavery and favored Negro colonization. It opposed as unconstitutional what it labeled “pre-emptory abolition.” The Plain Dealer supported Stephen A. Douglas, father of the controversial Kansas-Nebraska Act. “The Little Giant,” as he was called, had friends in the city, where he stopped off occasionally to visit his cousin Daniel P. Rhodes, a leading Democrat. John D. Rockefeller, engrossed though he was in his job, could | not have escaped the growing tension, and he may have attended some of the spirited anti-slavery meetings. His sympathies, it seems clear, were with the blacks for this year he widened his range of donations beyond his own Baptist Church to include a Negro congregation in the city, as well as a struggling German mission. His contributions rose from $19.31 at the close of 1856 to $28.37 by December 1857. His own interests broadened. He paid a dollar to join the newly launched Young Men’s Christian Association, which met in the Second Presbyterian Church. The YMCA’s president, Dan P. Eells, a prominent banker, also was active in the Ragged School, a center for poor children on lower Champlain Street. Rockefeller, possibly through Eells’s influence, earmarked a small sum for this project. One of Cleveland’s first charities, it was reorganized as the Al

eee

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:65.69 -->

<!-- page_id: 0028-left | printed: 42 | viewer_token: 42 | viewer_sequence: 58/340 -->
## Page 42

Cleveland Industrial School and ultimately became the Children’s Aid Society. The Ragged School’s first president was Truman P. Handy, another banker. Rockefeller soon was to know both men —and they him. In 1858 Hewitt raised his bookkeeper’s salary to $700. Rockefeller thought he was worth $800. Hewitt promised to negotiate about the additional hundred dollars at a later date. By year’s end John’s Ledger A, under “Donations,” showed a gift total of $43.85, one and a half times that of 1857. Yet 1858 had not brought the expected salary increase. With the city’s rapid expansion eastward, it was decided at this time to extend Prospect Street beyond its terminus at Sterling (East 30th) Street. The proposed thoroughfare would pass through land owned by George Merwin directly in line with his handsome brick home. To save it, the house was moved about seventy-five feet south to a lot on the new segment of Prospect Street. Apparently the first brick structure of such proportions moved in Cleveland, the difficult undertaking would have interested Rockefeller for the old house was a symbol of Merwin Street. Called the Mansion House, it had been built long ago as home and inn by the early settler in the Flats, Noble Merwin. When its site at Superior Street and Vineyard Lane was sold to make way for the Atwater Block, Noble’s son George salvaged the house. Brick by brick he reconstructed it on his acreage “out in the country,” where it was his home for many years. After its relocation, the historic house began a new life and had a succession of occupants and owners until 1895. Then it was purchased by the Rowfant Club, a men’s literary club. Ever since, as Number 3028 Prospect Avenue it has been headquarters for this lively group of bibliophiles. As the reincarnated Mansion House was settling on its new foundation, Rockefeller was about to sever his connection with familiar Merwin Street. Hewitt had not mentioned the extra hundred dollars. Early in 1859 the youth posted the firm’s books for the last time—and resigned. Disturbing as Hewitt’s silence was to the ambitious nineteenyear-old lad, it proved most fortunate for his future. As Rockefeller

<!-- page_id: 0028-right | printed: 43 | viewer_token: 42 | viewer_sequence: 58/340 -->
## Page 43

himself commented later, had the additional pay been forthcoming, he would have stayed with Hewitt, thereby missing, in his words, “a favorable opportunity” that was shaping up for him.

### 10. He Became His Own Employer

The year 1859 was memorable historically, and no less so for Rockefeller. For one thing, “Colonel” Edwin L. Drake in August of that year discovered oil in Pennsylvania. Two months later, the fanatic abolitionist, John Brown, attacked the United States arsenal at Harper’s Ferry. Equally noteworthy, as it turned out, John D. Rockefeller, in the spring, took a courageous step: he gave up his job as an employee and went into business for himself. The “favorable opportunity” presented itself in the person of Maurice B. Clark. One of the numerous, ambitious young Englishmen who had been attracted to Cleveland, he was working for the commission house of Otis, Brownell & Company on River Street. By utmost frugality Clark had saved the tidy sum of $2,000. He wanted to set up a commission house of his own. He was looking for a partner who could invest a like amount. So it was, that after careful deliberation, he picked young Rockefeller to be that partner. Clark was twenty-eight years old, Rockefeller not yet twenty. The Englishman had reached his plateau of success after years of hardship. While working as a gardener in Wiltshire, where he was born, he had scuffled with an overbearing employer. In the fray he knocked the man to the ground. To escape possible trouble with the law, he surreptitiously sailed for America, landing in Boston, a stranger and penniless. A bitter struggle lay ahead for the immigrant. Taking any job offered him, he gradually moved west until he reached Cleveland. He liked the town. He would make it his permanent home. With such a background, Clark could appreciate the thrifty, no-

mo———isi‘iéaRS

<!-- page_id: 0029-left | printed: 44 | viewer_token: 44 | viewer_sequence: 60/340 -->
## Page 44

nonsense habits of the young bookkeeper in the neighboring commission house on Merwin Street. Actually he had more than a passing acquaintance with Rockefeller. The two met first at Folsom’s Business School where Clark had enrolled although he was older than most of the students. Also, Clark and his wife lived on Cheshire Street only a few doors from the Rockefellers and they saw something of each other as neighbors. To John D., Clark’s proposal looked alluring. But where was he to get the necessary money to match what Clark could provide? He had saved between seven and eight hundred dollars from his salary and from his various independent transactions. He thought he might be able to gather together another hundred or two, but that would make only half of what Clark stipulated. “I talked the matter over with my father,” John D. recounted. “He told me he always had intended to give each of his children $1,000 when they reached the age of twenty-one.” William Avery Rockefeller offered to advance John’s one thousand dollars at once. However, the young man’s joy was cut short as the wily botanic physician added: “But John .. . the rate is ten.” The son wrote about this episode in 1909, just a half a century later. In case he was presenting his father in too mercenary a light, he hastened to exonerate his canny parent, and explained what he well knew from his own experience: “At that time 10 per cent a year was a very common rate of interest for such loans . . . I gladly accepted my father’s offer . . .” In March a sign “Clark and Rockefeller” went up on a warehouse at 32 River Street, in those days a busy trade artery running north and south beside the Cuyahoga River. The rear of the company’s headquarters faced the Cuyahoga, a convenient arrangement for the firm. The buildings, long since razed, were between Main and Front Streets. A four-story brick building, for a time occupied by Upson-Walton Company, ship chandlers, stands on the site. On the thoroughfare, now known as West 11th Street, only a few concerns survive. These are surrounded for the most part by ramshackle, abandoned structures and a few eating places like Pickle Bill’s and Mrs. Minever’s Saloon, geared hopefully to attract adventuresome

<!-- page_id: 0029-right | printed: 45 | viewer_token: 44 | viewer_sequence: 60/340 -->
## Page 45

patrons from uptown. As this is written, there is evidence of interest in updating the district. Nearby, Merwin Street winds southward beside the river, tangling with railroad tracks and shadowed by the colossal arch of the Superior-Detroit high level bridge. Directly under it, is pointed out the one-time location of Hewitt & Tuttle's commission house where John D. first went to work. The street which retains its original name, was a main street in the Flats. On it, at the corner of Superior Street, the Sexton Block was erected. In this building, Rockefeller had his first office a few years later. On West 11th Street, at the foot of St. Clair, a handsome sign indicates the spot where Moses Cleaveland landed in 1796. Maybe some day another plaque will mark the place on the river front where John D. Rockefeller got his start. The partnership of Maurice Clark and John D. Rockefeller was noted by the Leader, which gave their undertaking its blessing:

Clark and Rockefeller is the name of a new firm just commencing a general commission business at 32 River Street. As experienced, responsible and prompt businessmen, we recommend their house to the favorable consideration of our readers.

We have no record of Maurice Clark’s attitude, but we know his young associate “swelled with pride at being a partner in a firm with $4,000 capital.” He—John Davison Rockefeller—was his own employer! Characteristically, Rockefeller checked his enthusiasm. Remembering his Bible’s warnings about pride and its dangers, he declared he seldom put his head “upon the pillow at night without speaking a few words to myself.” What he termed “these intimate conversations with myself” came to be more or less habitual throughout his life. He thus was enabled “to stand prosperity” and was saved from getting “puffed up,” as he put it. His religion was a practical religion as befitted a practical man, He used his Bible freely like a tool. It is easy to imagine him at bedtime, in his room in the Cheshire Street house, extracting what he could from the Scriptures. His

<!-- page_id: 0030-left | printed: 46 | viewer_token: 46 | viewer_sequence: 62/340 -->
## Page 46

brother Will shared the bedroom with him. He was working, too. Like John, he had started as a bookkeeper in the Flats, employed by Hughes and Davis, where later his name would be added as a partner. No self-examination plagued the carefree Will. When the hour came for sleeping, he slept. Clark & Rockefeller Company “prospered from the first . . . business came in upon us so fast we hardly knew how to take care of it,” the junior partner revealed. He took full charge of the office and the books, giving the same scrupulous attention to details as he had at Hewitt & Tuttle’s. The other member of the firm, a sociable man who enjoyed meeting people, worked on the outside, buying and selling produce. Volume increased. A freight car filled with grain for the firm might be waiting on a side track for unloading while a schooner lay at anchor, its hold heavy with a consignment for Clark and Rockefeller. Early in their venture an unseasonable frost in Central Ohio and Indiana reduced harvests and consequently slowed deals. The more temperamental Clark was discouraged. His partner, never losing his confidence, plunged into work all the more intently and righted the temporary setback. It soon was apparent that the $4,000, which had seemed so substantial a capital base, was inadequate. To meet increasing invoices and the expansion forced upon them, they needed more money. William Avery Rockefeller willingly provided several loans. After all, at ten percent what better investment could he find? His son, at first gratefully, and later with more and more misgiving, accepted such help. The big Doctor had a disturbing way of looming up unexpectedly in the office some days before a loan was due, to boom out cheerfully that he must have his money. To comply put John D. invariably in a vise financially. Yet, with no change of expression, he always agreed to have the amount ready the next day. The young merchant was doubly annoyed when his parent afterwards would confess with a grin that he had not needed the funds —he was only testing his son’s ability to meet emergencies! The tolerant John D. conceded that perhaps this sort of treatment was good discipline for him. Nonetheless the son determined to try a

<!-- page_id: 0030-right | printed: 47 | viewer_token: 46 | viewer_sequence: 62/340 -->
## Page 47

more stable kind of borrowing as the new firm’s growth continued to require additional capital. The junior partner, well groomed as usual, set out to call on a banker, Truman P. Handy at the Commercial Branch Bank located in the Atwater Block. Handy was president of the bank and its principal stockholder with Dan P. Eells the cashier. An aristocrat and an astute businessman, Handy was recognized as the foremost banker in the city. That year, among the many offices he held, he was elected president of the newly formed Cleveland Clearing House Association, an innovation in inter-bank check handling of the greatest convenience to commercial houses. He had advanced steadily since arrival in Cleveland in 1832 from Buffalo where he was cashier of a bank. Accompanied by his bride he had made the hazardous journey by stagecoach. The historian and statesman, George Bancroft, of Worcester, Massachusetts, whose varied interests included state banking, had induced him to revive the Commercial State Bank on the charter of Cleveland’s first financial institution, the defunct Commercial Bank of Lake Erie. Bancroft supplied $200,000 raised in the East for the purpose. Handy, aged twenty-five, was made cashier, soon succeeding Leonard Case as president. Cleveland at the time was a hamlet of only several thousand souls. At the front door of the new bank on Superior Street hill stood a town pump, its drippings frequently attracting a grateful pig or a couple of geese straying from the pastures on the Public Square. From the first, Handy became a leader in practically every important cultural and civic movement in the developing city. The pillared mansion he built in 1842 on Euclid Street west of Erie (Hippodrome site) was a triumph of pioneer architecture, evidence of its owner’s taste and standing in the city. As a member of the Board of School Managers (Board of Education), Handy was an ardent advocate of a free public high school. When Central High was built a few doors from his home, he enjoyed visiting there to observe the progress of its handful of pupils—John D. Rockefeller among them. Handy and Eells, both active in the Young Men’s Christian As- Ary

socation, had seen Rockefeller, now a Y member, at Association meetings. Nor did the bankers overlook the fact that the young man had been coming to the bank for the past couple of years to make deposits in his savings account. “In those days in Cleveland,” as Rockefeller observed, “everybody knew almost everybody else.” The young commission merchant from River Street was no stranger to Handy and Eells. They greeted their visitor cordially and listened attentively as he presented in forthright fashion particulars about his new business and his need for a loan. Years later Rockefeller could recall every word of the final conversation:

> “How much do you want?” Mr. Handy asked. “Two thousand dollars.” “All right, Mr. Rockefeller, you can have it. Just give me your warehouse receipts; they’re good enough for me.”

The borrower added: “As I left the bank my elation can hardly be imagined. I held up my head—think of it, a bank had trusted me for $2,000! I felt I was a man of importance in the community.” In his quest for a loan Rockefeller instinctively had selected the most prominent banker in the city, and had won his support. An endorsement by Truman P. Handy in Cleveland in those years was a passport to success. The shrewd banker, on his part, sensing Rockefeller’s ability, saw him as a valuable future customer of his institution. And so it turned out! “For long years after,” Rockefeller said of this first loan, “the head of this bank was a friend indeed; he loaned me money when I needed it, and I needed it almost all the time.” Later Rockefeller was able to express his gratitude in a way that proved most lucrative for Handy. He had recommended to the banker a certain block of Standard Oil stock as an investment. Handy knew it was a good offer, but at the time he lacked ready resources to take advantage of it. Turning banker, the oil man loaned him the necessary amount. He liked to remember that Handy “took out his principal with a very handsome profit.” Rockefeller’s contacts with the highly respected Handy and Eells went beyond financial matters. He was drawn more and more into

<!-- page_id: 0031-right | printed: 49 | viewer_token: 48 | viewer_sequence: 64/340 -->
## Page 49

local projects with them, such as the Ragged School, the Cleveland Bible Society, of which Eells was a leader, and the Sunday School Union. The latter vigorous organization had been fathered by Handy who for years was superintendent of the Sunday School at the Second Presbyterian Church. Shortly, Rockefeller was appointed to this office at his church, a position he held for most of his life, and he became a faithful attender at the Union’s gatherings. With these wider connections, Rockefellers gifts increased. While his church still was paramount in his giving program, he was going further afield. In that first year in his own business, Rockefeller’s ledger showed money sent to a Cincinnati Negro “to buy his wife.” It was the start of a lifetime concern for the American black, probably inspired by the anti-slavery agitation now growing more vociferous in Cleveland. In April, shortly after Clark & Rockefeller opened their doors, a mob of irate abolitionists descended on Public Square. A protest march was staged around the jail where distinguished citizens of Oberlin and Wellington were incarcerated. They had seized a fugitive slave, John Price, from a United States marshal in Wellington, secreted him in Cleveland and helped him onto a ship bound for Canada. The participants were found guilty under the Fugitive Slave Law, and the incident made history as the Oberlin-Wellington Rescue. On Independence Day, four days before John D.’s twentieth birthday, John Brown secretly bivouacked in a Maryland farmhouse with his “army” of twenty-one men. They were training for the thrust that would “rally Negroes everywhere and end slavery for-

ever. Almost due north of them in Western Pennsylvania at Titusville, Colonel Drake, a former railroad conductor, was drilling a well for petroleum. He had been at the task a long time, frustrated by all kinds of difficulties. No one was paying much attention to him, and certainly no one suspected what John Brown was planning, By a coincidence, in that part of Crawford County, near where Drake was occupied, John Brown had homesteaded thirty years before and there had buried his first wife, Dianthe. Before Drake’s attempt, no one had thought of going deep into

<!-- page_id: 0032-left | printed: 50 | viewer_token: 50 | viewer_sequence: 66/340 -->
## Page 50

the earth for oil, as in drilling for water. People were content to skim it off like cream on milk where it rose to the surface of certain streams. They had gathered it, too, as it seeped from rocks. Hence it came to be called “petroleum” from two Greek words meaning “rock oil.” Petroleum had been known since ancient times. John D., had he realized it, heard of it first in his Bible. Noah, building the Ark, was commanded to “pitch it within and without with pitch,” which has been interpreted as meaning petroleum. DeSoto calked his boats with “pitch;” Greeks and Egyptians used petroleum to lubricate chariot wheels. It was burned in primitive religious rites as it floated on streams. American Indians valued it as medicine. In his will, Washington noted “burning springs” on his lands in Pennsylvania and (West) Virginia that were “luminous and inflammable by nature.” American pioneers found petroleum a nuisance; it invaded fields, killed vegetation, contaminated streams where cattle drank, and spoiled salt and water wells. One Samuel Kier of Pittsburgh, who sold oil in little vials as a general and expensive remedy, used as a trade mark a derrick contraption like those erected at water and salt wells. This illustration, some claim, was the inspiration for the revolutionary idea of drilling for petroleum. Kier peddled his oily cures about the country from the rear of a gaudily-painted wagon. It is a Cleveland tradition that when John D. was at Central High, the Pittsburgher stopped off in the city and parked his caravan in the Public Square for a sales effort. Speculation is futile but irresistible as to whether curiosity might have lured the high school boy, en route to or from classes, to Kier’s caravan for a look at his bottles of oil, or to compare them with his father’s wares. Whoever deserves credit for the untried method of digging for oil, Drake and his New Haven backers were becoming discouraged with its difficulties, but they persevered. Suddenly their luck changed. Drilling at last went ahead smoothly, deeper and deeper, three feet a day. Workmen stopped for a rest Saturday, August 27. The next morning one of their boys strolled over to the shaft, cupped

hands to eyes and peered down. Thick, dark liquid, greasy and illsmelling, had risen almost to the top of the tubing. | “Oil! Oil!” he shouted. Men came running. Crowds swarmed about the rigging. They _ came mostly from the near country. Men looked and hurried to buy | adjacent land, made plans to dig wells. But it was a mild and limited flurry, at first, nothing like the wild stampede that broke out later. Contrary to the way the story often is told reverberations did not fan out immediately from Titusville. If echoes reached John D., chances are he was indifferent. He was fully occupied with his new business. In writing about this period in his Reminiscences, it is significant that he does not mention Drake’s find. Similarly, Cleveland newspapers ignored the excitement in Pennsylvania, if indeed any hints of it came their way. Editors were preoccupied with other items such as lake shipping, the prospects of trans-Atlantic trade for local vessel owners, and the like. Before the public and the press had assessed Drake’s achievement, it was relegated to the background. Seven weeks to the day after the triumphant cry of “Oil,” John Brown and his men rocked the country to its depths with their attack at Harper’s Ferry. News- | papers devoted columns to the raid, the “Old Man’s” trial, and his composure as he faced the gallows. Harper’s Ferry had rallied no | Negroes: it had hastened the coming of the Civil War. All the while, efforts of Cleveland manufacturers to improve illuminants were matters of great interest to editors and readers. S. S. Barrie, for one, was advertising coal oil that “notwithstanding failures elsewhere is a brilliant success . . . non-explosive, guaranteed not to smoke if used in proper lamps,” and cheaper than lard or sperm oil or gas. The Cleveland Oil Company in new quarters at Water and St. Clair Streets was offering “a fine grade of lard oil and sterino candles.” An Englishman, C. E. Dean, who had been refining lard oil and manufacturing coal oil from cannel coal, acquired ten barrels of crude oil from the Oil Regions. He and his assistants, John Alexander and Samuel Andrews, also Britishers, experimented with this * lot of oil. Finally they produced kerosene. It is said their success, |

<!-- page_id: 0037-left | printed: 52 | viewer_token: 52 | viewer_sequence: 76/340 -->
## Page 52

to a large extent, was due to Andrews. It was the first kerosene made in Cleveland. In the meantime, details about the Titusville oil flow did not appear in a Cleveland newspaper until November 18, when the Leader got around to noticing the event that had occurred nearly three months before. Anything but a sensational presentation, the low-keyed report was published on an inside page: The Oil Springs of western Pennsylvania are attracting considerable attention and there is quite a speculative rush for the oleaginous locations. According to the Mercer Dispatch, some of these are better than gold mines for it says that until the recent fire which burned up the works of Mr. Drake at Oil Creek the well yielded from 25 to 35 barrels a day at a cost of ten cents per barrel! New Works are to be erected when it is supposed double the quantity will be obtained. We suppose 10 cents per gallon should be substituted for 10 cents per barrel. Another paper puts Mr. Drake’s profit at 90 cents per gallon clear, or over $400 per day. The reporter stated that two of the citizens of Conneaut (Ohio) visited the newly discovered deposits of oil in Titusville, Crawford County, Pa. last week and brought back samples of it. The Reporter says: Our readers will recollect that this oil was found issuing from a fissure on a rock on the bank of a small creek and early in the past season a shaft was sunk and at a distance of sixty-nine feet oil was found in apparently inexhaustible quantities. About forty barrels of oil is pumped daily from this well, and which, it is said, finds a ready market. More recently another shaft was sunk and oil at the depth of seventy feet found. Arrangements are now making (sic) for the sinking of other shafts. Indeed we learn that the land in the vicinity is eagerly sought for by companies and corner lots and oil springs are at a high figure. Geologists tell us that underneath the rock where the oil is found are large deposits of coal and that for ages this oil has been collecting from it. The doings in Pennsylvania seem to have won the attention of few Clevelanders. An exception was J. G. Hussey, of Hussey & Mc-

<!-- page_id: 0037-right | printed: 53 | viewer_token: 52 | viewer_sequence: 76/340 -->
## Page 53

Bride, a produce company in the Flats. Rumors got about that Hussey had made a trip to the Oil Regions and had acquired well rights on a large farm. On his return to Cleveland, he dodged questions and would say little about it. | Rockefeller and Clark, of course, knew him and had heard the | riverside gossip about his plunge into the new industry. The partners, however, were concentrating on their River Street business. Commission Merchant Rockefeller had reason to be pleased with his first year’s experience as his own employer.

> 11. Oil as a Sideline

When Clark & Rockefeller closed company books in March 1860, at the end of their first year, John D. stated “our sales amounted to half a million dollars.” Their net profit was $4,400. Divided between them, this meant that the junior partner, during his first year in business for himself, had earned three times what he received the previous year from Hewitt. The Oil Regions of Western Pennsylvania were now fully in the grip of a boom and an orgy of drilling. Land prices skyrocketed. Forests of oil derricks crowded the landscape. At some wells the greasy liquid flowed out beyond control; at other sites nothing surfaced. Rockefeller soberly weighed such reports now relayed daily to Cleveland. He concluded oil was speculative and hazardous. He would keep his mind on grain and meat consignments. Within a year a couple of small refineries were operating along the Cuyahoga, the tell-tale residue from them dirtying the river. Clark & Rockefeller began to handle heavy, often leaky barrels of crude petroleum shipped out of Pennsylvania. George W. Gardner was taken into the commission firm. He had worked with Clark at Otis, Brownell Company and was welcomed

eee Fp

<!-- page_id: 0038-left | printed: 54 | viewer_token: 54 | viewer_sequence: 78/340 -->
## Page 54

by the partners, for he had cash to contribute to the company’s capital. His connection was of short duration. Gardner appears to have been something of a playboy, at least in Rockefeller’s evaluation. The new partner owned a handsome boat that he loved and kept moored in sight of the office. The graceful craft floating gently at anchor tempted him. On a summer day, with soft breezes stirring, he was known to slam shut the company ledger, hurry off and set sail over Lake Erie’s waters. More than once he tried to persuade John D. to leave his desk and join him. But the youngest partner could neither comprehend nor tolerate such indifference to the work at hand. Before long the sign over the commission house at 32 River Street once again carried only the names of Clark and Rockefeller. Gardner, a member of a prominent Cleveland family, remained an ardent sailor, later becoming Commodore of the Cleveland Yacht Club. He was active in local business and politics. He served as president of the board of trade in 1868 and as mayor of the city in 1885-1887, and again in 1889-1891. Rockefeller was well into his second year as partner of a growing business before he reached the age of twenty-one. Eligible to vote for the first time in the November elections of 1860 that put Lincoln in the White House, presumably he cast his ballot for that candidate. The choice of the majority of Clevelanders, Lincoln was staunchly backed by the Leader. By contrast, the Plain Dealer, under Editor J. W. Gray, represented an articulate minority who championed Stephen A. Douglas. Pre-election tensions had been siphoned off temporarily on September 10th when crowds turned out for dedication of a statue honoring Commodore Oliver Hazard Perry. It was placed in the center of Public Square, which from then on was officially designated as Monumental Park. The new name, however, was never widely accepted. The unveiling took place on the anniversary of Perry’s victory on Lake Erie. Banker Handy persuaded his friend George Bancroft to be orator of the day. It was an appropriate choice, for Bancroft, famous for his work on American history, also had served as Secretary of

<!-- page_id: 0038-right | printed: 55 | viewer_token: 54 | viewer_sequence: 78/340 -->
## Page 55

the Navy, and had had a leading part in establishing the Naval Academy at Annapolis. Men streamed from offices and shops to hear the distinguished visitor. Since the speaker was his bankerhero’s friend, Rockefeller, it is likely, joined the crowd. After taking charge in the early days of Clark & Rockefeller’s office details, the junior partner decided to try his hand at customer development. It was an imaginative departure from commercial practices of the day: I began to go out and solicit customers—a branch of work I never before attempted. I undertook to visit every person in our part of the country who was in any way connected with the kind of business that we were engaged in, and went pretty well over the states of Ohio and Indiana. I made up my mind that I could do this best by simply introducing our firm, and not pressing for immediate consignments. I told them that I represented Clark and Rockefeller, commission merchants, that I had no wish to interfere with any connection they had at present, but, if the opportunity offered, we should be glad to serve them, etc., etc.

In this passage Rockefeller revealed a good deal about himself and his business philosophy, in essence, a pattern he followed in the big deals that lay ahead. Trimly clad in conservative but good clothes appropriate for the role of a well-established merchant, he presented his proposition simply, with a quiet voice and few words. No bombast, no high-pressure salesmanship. His hearers concluded this earnest young man was one they could trust and they gave him their business. “After I stayed in the country a few weeks, I returned home,” he said, “and the consignments came in.” For the distant calls he went by train, but when he could he drove, always happy to have a horse at his command. His travels carried him into the rich agricultural districts of the two states. He enjoyed these forays for at such times he was reminded of the bucolic countryside of his New York State boyhood. On these expeditions he met Henry M. Flagler in Bellevue, Sandusky County, Ohio, heart of lush corn and wheat country. Flagler was related to Steven V. Harkness, an influential citizen in that part

of Ohio. The Clark & Rockefeller firm was handling consignments from Flagler regularly and it may be that it had similar contacts with Harkness, who was also a grain dealer. On February 15, 1861 Abraham Lincoln stopped off in Cleveland on the way to his inaugural. The sad-faced, gaunt man, stovepipe hat in hand, rode down Euclid Street in an open carriage as a cold rain fell. Church bells pealed, to be drowned out by thundering cannon in the noisy welcome. Throngs gathered at the Weddel House as the president-elect addressed them from the hotel balcony. The thin man towering above the hundreds massed before the hotel plead for the Union and the Constitution, although secession already had been proclaimed by six states. A month after Lincoln’s visit, the partners at 32 River Street finished their second year of business. Profits had soared from $4,400 in 1860 to $17,000 in 1861. Rockefeller’s charitable program expanded accordingly. The previous year his ledger showed he had given away $107.35. This year his donations came to $250.97. On April 13th Fort Sumter fell to the Confederates. Two days later Lincoln asked for seventy-five thousand three-month volunteers. An overflow audience jammed Cleveland’s Melodeon Hall to hear Judge Rufus P. Spaulding’s ringing appeal for citizens to forget politics and rally to the Union cause. Torchlight processions and street-corner exhorters boosted recruiting. The Cleveland Grays, a military organization of elite young men who had formed an honor guard for Lincoln on his stop-over two months before, was the first group from the city to leave for the front. Other young men, about Rockefeller’s age, employed in the Flats were giving up their jobs to join the forces. Older men in executive positions departed with them. Rockefeller watched them go. Many of them he saw every day. He was troubled. He knew that he, not Clark, was the company’s mainstay. He realized his mother and the Cheshire Street household were largely his responsibility. While his father was better off financially, he was as mercurial as ever, his treks away from home becoming more extended. Young William was not enlisting, but anyway John D. felt he could not shift his obligations to him. The

<!-- page_id: 0039-right | printed: 57 | viewer_token: 56 | viewer_sequence: 80/340 -->
## Page 57

upshot was John D. Rockefeller did not go to war. His conscience } obviously bothered him, for he believed in the Union cause. Stories/ persist about his gifts of large sums to families of soldiers, to local war relief funds, and for equipment given to individuals who had, enlisted. . One morning Levi Scofield, now a captain in the Union Army, led thirty recruits into Rockefeller’s office, for what purpose we do not know. According to the captain, who recounted the incident years later, the commission merchant went to his office safe, lifted out a heavy bag and from it gave each of the men ten dollars. “God! how rich he must be,” exclaimed one soldier-to-be under his breath. “Yes,” agreed a companion, “maybe worth as much as $10,000!” It was Captain Scofield who afterwards designed Cleveland’s Civil War Memorial, the Soldiers and Sailors Monument, a landmark on the Public Square that cost $280,000 and was dedicated on July 4, 1894. One member of the Rockefeller family, young Frank, did join the Union ranks. Not yet sixteen and fired by the impassioned drives for recruits, the youth tried to sign up. Rejected at first because of his age, he refused to be thwarted. Trying a second time he managed to be accepted through a boyish trick. Before returning to the recruiting office he chalked the numerals “18” on the soles of his shoes. When the officer in charge, looking at him quizzically, asked his age, he replied quite truthfully: “’'m over eighteen, sir.” Frank disclosed none of this to his family, but alert Doctor Rockefeller somehow got wind of what was up. As the boy was stealthily leaving the house, his father’s voice boomed out ordering him to come back. If he must go to war, he said to the boy, “say goodbye like a man!” John D. was distraught, but stood by his impetuous brother. He supplied him with money and his needs, including clothes and a rifle. Frank served in the army for almost three years. He was wounded in 1862 at Cedar Mountain and again the next year at Chancellorsville. After the latter engagement he was given an honorable discharge. Cleveland newspapers filled their pages with news from the bat-

## Page 58

tlefields. Names like Bull Run, Fredericksburg, Antietam, Stone River and Chickamauga were on every tongue. As in the case of many businesses, war orders stimulated trade for Clark & Rockefeller. The firm was forced to acquire three additional offices adjacent to the original headquarters. Congressional investigation disclosed widespread profiteering, by merchants and manufacturers supplying war materiels, as well as corruption in government contracts. No shadow of irregularity fell on Rockefeller, as even those otherwise hostile to him readily conceded. One of his biographers, John T. Flynn, author of God’s Gold, often critical of him, writes on this point: “As for Rockefeller, his firm made a great deal of money during the war so that when it was over he was a rich man. But there is no evidence that he engaged in any questionable transactions or dealt in any improper way with the government.” He had benefited from the rising prices. But he was honest. As the casualty lists lengthened and Americans fought Americans in a seemingly interminable bloody struggle, war took precedence over every topic save one—oil. While battles raged, oil wells were going down in Pennsylvania, yielding steady flows of the liquid treasure—or nothing. Fortunes were made, and vanished. A Cleveland tale, persistent but never confirmed, is that a group of local businessmen, impressed with the diligence and reliability of young Rockefeller, sent him on an early inspection trip to the Oil Regions. Whether or not he went at this time, he did go later. In any case, he could not ignore the subject of oil. The steady increase in crude oil sales was apparent in shipments handled by his firm. Refineries were functioning in several parts of the country, including Cleveland. Getting the petroleum from its source to the refineries became the great problem. In a few situations, rivers and creeks provided transportation of a sort, but for the most part, draying was relied on. Roads, totally inadequate to begin with, soon were churned to ribbons of mud. Overloaded, creaking wagons crawled from the wells in unbroken lines, Horses and mules were strained to exhaustion, often forced on by cruel beatings.

## Page 59

The year 1863 was ushered in with Lincoln’s Emancipation Proclamation. The Plain Dealer consistently castigated the President for exceeding his authority in the conduct of war. The Leader endorsed his assumption of power as essential for ending the conflict. _ Against the much-publicized objections of residents in the fine houses on lower Euclid Street, a horse-car trolley was put into operation from Public Square to Erie Street. This year John D. Rockefeller went into oil. Among the many men discussing this newly revealed bounty of nature, none was more eloquent about it than the Englishman, Samuel Andrews. He could think of little else since that achievement of distilling kerosene from those barrels of petroleum at Dean’s refinery. He had come to Cleveland in 1857, a self-taught chemist who was expert in tallows, candles and the like. Now he was fascinated at the possibilities he saw in this new substance—petroleum. Convinced that kerosene, properly made from it, surpassed all other illuminants, and shortly would supplant them entirely, he wanted to organize a company to manufacture the product. Enthusiastically he presented his idea to Rockefeller and Clark, both of whom he knew. Rockefeller saw him frequently at the Erie Street Baptist Church where he and Mrs. Andrews were members. Andrews was a native of Wiltshire, like Clark, and the latter had befriended him when he arrived in Cleveland, helping him to locate his first job. Rockefeller and Clark listened to what he had to say. The subject was not new to them. They, too, had been discussing oil intently. From the numerous sidelights on the family as contributed by Rockefeller’s sister, Mary Ann, we have a glimpse of him at this time, facing a crucial decision. He was thinking about oil continually, his sister disclosed. In the middle of the night he was known to waken William, his bedfellow, to go over some new angle that had occurred to him. She related how Clark and Andrews soon were coming early every morning to the side door of the Rockefeller home on Cheshire Street to sit beside John in the dining room while he ate his breakfast. “They all talked oil, oil, oil until I was sick of the word!”

## Page 60

she declared. “They always waited until John finished eating, for,” she added, “they did not seem to want to go without him. Then they would walk off to work together, still talking about oil.” John was at breakfast by 6:30 or before, Mary Ann explained. He was carrying out their father’s admonition: “If you want your business to succeed, be there early and start it yourself.” John D. approved of Andrews’s thrifty ways. His wife took in sewing to plump the family budget. The couple saved money, always a measure of merit with Rockefeller. The infant oil industry, Rockefeller realized, was anything but stable. Wells were dug indiscriminately. Too many refineries were springing up. Already more than a dozen operated in Cleveland. The previous year crude oil brought from thirty-five to fifty cents a barrel at the pumps and, when refined, the same price per gallon. Profits, Rockefeller saw, were in the refined oil rather than in the crude. Despite the threat of over-production, he finally reached the conclusion that with good management money could be made in oil, and he invested several thousand dollars. In 1863 a new firm emerged—Andrews, Clark & Company. Maurice brought in his two brothers, James and Richard, who had followed him to Cleveland. Rockefeller’s name did not appear as a member of the organization. If he was a silent partner, he soon was the one in command. Greatly to the new firm’s advantage, the Cleveland, or western end of the Atlantic and Great Western Railroad was completed early in July. Final spikes were pounded into place the day after Lee retreated at Gettysburg. Financed largely by English capital, the road had been planned before Drake's strike at Titusville. The line penetrated the very heart of the Oil Regions, supplying for the first time an efficient means of transportation of crude petroleum from wells to refineries. Through a connection with the Erie Railroad, it shortly provided a direct route to New York City. The A&GW, with its broad, six-gauge tracks, gave Cleveland not only access to oil sources, but linked the city with the eastern seaboard markets and exporting facilities as well. This railroad became the nation’s greatest carrier of oil, offering stiff competition to other lines. Later, it was absorbed by the Erie

<!-- page_id: 0041-right | printed: 61 | viewer_token: 60 | viewer_sequence: 84/340 -->
## Page 61

in Jay Gould’s take-over of that line. The A&GW’s importance to John D. Rockefeller and to Cleveland was enormous. On November 3rd the railroad’s first train, its shiny locomotive patriotically flaunting Union colors, steamed into the city at forty miles an hour. On November 19th Lincoln delivered his immortal address at Gettysburg National Cemetery in memory of the 7,058 Americans who had killed each other on the battlefield. The Plain Dealer sent a reporter, whose brief dispatch the following day informed readers that “the weather being fine, the program was carried out successfully.” Not a word of Lincoln’s speech. The local press was more impressed with the glittering banquet staged about this time by the influential Board of Trade in recognition of the new railroad and its titled English backer, Sir Morton Peto. The menu featured nine varieties of fish, as many of meat and game, dozens of pastries and “liberal servings of sparkling champagne.” Guests included the popular General James A. Garfield, returned from the war and lionized for his service; Amasa Stone, bridge builder and railroad director, and wealthy H. B. Payne, sonin-law of the respected pioneer, Nathan Perry. John D. Rockefeller, whose oil shipments would constitute the road’s heaviest freight, did not attend. He probably was not invited. Not one to court association with the prominent or to indulge in the pleasures of the table, he likely gave no thought to the affair. The young oil executive instead was busy clinching an option he had taken in the name of Andrews, Clark & Company on a threeacre piece of land abutting on the new railroad. The property lay on the south bank of Kingsbury Run, where the stream cut its channel to the Cuyahoga (and today is hidden in a culvert as it empties into the river). Providing water as well as rail transportation, the plot was the nucleus of the one hundred-acre site occupied later by the Stan- _ dard Oil Company. From this high point there was a good view of Cleveland and its Public Square less than two miles distant. Tall trees, remnants of a virgin forest, shaded the banks of the Run. Photographs show a cow grazing placidly beside the busy plant's crude buildings. Rockefeller stated that in this period he regarded oil as a side-

line. Nevertheless, he gave serious attention to the newly launched refinery on Kingsbury Run. “It was my first direct contact with the oil trade,” he said. Meanwhile he kept his hand firmly on the throttle of the commission business. It was a flourishing enterprise as shown by public notices like the following:

A consignment of 1300 barrels of salt from the new Michigan Salt Works was received yesterday by Clark & Rockefeller. 500 bushels of clover seed, 800 bushels of timothy seed and 200 barrels of mess (army) pork now in Clark & Rockefeller warehouse ready for immediate shipping. But Rockefeller had another interest more compelling than refining oil or selling produce.

> 12. Cettie

Perhaps it was at anti-slavery meetings that Rockefeller renewed his acquaintance with Laura Celestia Spelman—“Cettie” to her classmates, and “Cettie” to John. The Spelmans were ardent abolitionists. Rockefeller, too, it would seem, was drawn early into the crusade, judging from his gifts to Negro causes. Chances are he had not forgotten the chestnut-haired Cettie who sat on the girls’ side of the classroom at Central High School. In those days Cleveland was a small community. Consequently, John had no difficulty keeping informed of Cettie’s whereabouts, although her parents changed residence frequently. The Spelman family, coming to Cleveland from Akron in 1851, settled first on “The Heights,” on the southwest side of the crooked Cuyahoga near the site of the short-lived Cleveland University. John and his brother Will, who had acquired saddle horses as soon as they could afford to buy them, found it a pleasant expedition to canter across the river valley for a call on the Spelmans. Before long, only John’s mount was seen tethered in the Spelman dooryard. Tale t

<!-- page_id: 0042-right | printed: 63 | viewer_token: 62 | viewer_sequence: 86/340 -->
## Page 63

Cettie’s family moved into the city proper, taking a house on Perry Street near Euclid Street, and finally to a permanent home at 58 Huron Street. John followed their peregrinations. The Huron Street address was within walking distance of the Rockefeller residence on Cheshire Street. Sometimes John stopped in at the Spelmans’ on his way home from his refinery. He might be in working clothes, his shoes muddied. He was known to take his place beside his employees when they were pressed for help, and he was not above trundling a barrel or two of oil in a wheelbarrow. Most of the time, however, he presented himself in his usual, immaculate attire. Harvey B. Spelman, Cettie’s father, was a prosperous merchant. As a child he had come from Massachusetts to Rootstown, Portage County, Ohio, where the family became prominent citizens. Spelman pioneered in several northern Ohio towns, opening a store in Wadsworth, Medina County in 1833. Two years later, in Middlebury, now part of Akron, he married Lucy Henry, also of New England lineage. She was a teacher in a one-room log school. They lived for a short time in Le Roy, Medina County, where, in 1837 Lucy, eldest of their two daughters, was born. Returning to Wadsworth Spelman operated a general store on Main Street. The building housing it seems to have served as both store and home, for it was the birthplace of Laura Celestia on September 9th, 1839. John D. Rockefeller was born two months before. Following a short business connection in Kent, Spelman moved his family to Akron where he managed a store at the corner of Hower and Market Streets, and also a branch in the nearby village of River Styx. He became a member of Akron’s first Board of Education in 1847, and in 1849 was elected as representative to the Ohio Legislature. He was an active supporter of the so-called “Akron Plan,” a system of free graded schools, then an innovation but later widely adopted. Wherever the Spelmans settled they participated in community affairs and were early won to the anti-slavery cause. In Cleveland their home, probably the one on Huron Street, was a “station” on the Underground Railroad. Mrs. Spelman used to say that the only

times she broke the Sabbath was when she cooked hot meals for the fugitive slaves sheltered in her home on their way to Canada. About this time Spelman went to Burlington, lowa where he opened a store, and in 1856 the family lived there for a time while continuing to maintain their Cleveland residence. The Spelmans were Congregationalists, adhering as firmly to their doctrines as Rockefeller to his Baptist faith. In Cleveland, and in several other Ohio communities, Lucy and Harvey Spelman helped organize churches of their denomination. They continued to support the Congregational Church in Rootstown where their relatives worshipped. Cettie also kept in touch with Rootstown, and after her marriage made numerous gifts to the village church, including, on one occasion, a large pipe organ. Laura Spelman had been teaching four years in the “lower forms” at Hudson Street School in Cleveland when Rockefeller’s visits turned into serious courtship. According to Lucy, it was in March 1864 when her sister said the magic yes and the two were formally engaged. They did not spread the news; few knew about it, Lucy added. Harvey Spelman and his wife were proud of their daughters and had them photographed often. We see Cettie as a very attractive young woman, her dark and luxuriant hair combed into a knot from a straight center part, her fine eyes with a happy gleam. Her dress followed the current style with full sleeves and billowy skirts, along with prim lace mittens and a bonnet tied under her chin. Cettie had more advantages in schooling and travel than her fiancé. After she and her sister graduated from high school, their parents sent them to Oread Collegiate Institute in Worcester, Massachusetts. Established in 1849, the school was among the first institutions of higher learning open to women. Emphasis was on the classics, Christian living and Negro welfare. Abolition was a burning issue. The school scheduled such visiting lecturers as Emerson, Thackeray, Wendell Phillips, and Henry Ward Beecher. John Brown came to exhort the students who listened to him as to a prophet. Religion, a strict uncompromising variety, was stressed. Cettie,

<!-- page_id: 0043-right | printed: 65 | viewer_token: 64 | viewer_sequence: 88/340 -->
## Page 65

given to letter writing, had been in the habit of writing home each Sunday until the school declared such activity a desecration of the Sabbath. The product of a devout upbringing herself, she was dismayed and selected another day for her weekly epistle. Despite the rigid regime, she entered into the Oread schedule, her zest, albeit, somewhat dimmed by a five-thirty rising bell for prayers at dawn and recitations that began at seven. She was “Editress” of the school paper, Vox Oreadum, “issued weekly by the Oread Euphemia and devoted to literature and general intelligence (politics excluded).” It was handwritten by Cettie whose penmanship was excellent. She found the New England winter severe, and so expressed herself in a long essay entitled “The Winter King Has Triumphed.” Under the by-line, Cettie L. Spelman, her contributions to the Oreadum included a many-versed poem which began:

> Are there not studies on each leafy tree? Did God not write his glorious sermons there? Are there not lessons in the deep-tossed sea? Are there not whispers in the wild, free air?

In addition to the advantages offered at Oread, she and Lucy had had a better than average musical education. When the Spelmans were in Iowa, their daughters studied piano and voice under a much-loved teacher who made creditable amateur musicians of both young women. With all this to offer, the sisters presented their credentials to the Cleveland Board of Education, applied for positions and were engaged. Lucy as well as Laura taught at Hudson Street School. For one interlude Lucy was a member of the faculty of Cleveland Institute, a successful coeducational boarding and day school. It had taken over the campus of the former Cleveland University on the South Side near where the Spelmans lived for a time. The director was Ransom F. Humiston, once a teacher in the public schools. One of Cettie’s able pupils was John Green, a Negro whose late father had purchased John’s freedom in the South. The boy and his mother, also a freed slave, came to Cleveland but had a difficult time in the unfamiliar setting. John, however, managed to re-

Nevertheless, she was his devoted partner, maintaining their home as a quiet harbor where he could be sure of surcease from outside pressures. Both were deeply religious and neither cared for formal social affairs. The two shared a love of music which became an important element in their family life. Another common bond was concern for community welfare. As appeals for help mounted, Rockefeller’s giving reflected his wife’s point of view as much as his own.

## Page 67

### 13. “The Boss Is In Love”

Rockefeller pursued his marriage plans systematically. He maintained a financial diary covering his courtship, wedding, and honeymoon. The old account book, Ledger A, was superseded in 1864 by Ledger B. In it under the heading “Sundry Expenses” he recorded romance-related expenditures as well as purely personal items. The new volume was a sturdier, thicker journal as befitted one whose community and business interests were expanding rapidly. Discerning Cleveland leaders saw the twenty-five year-old “Mr. Rockefeller” as a citizen to be recognized, already a man of some wealth, well launched toward more. Despite his easier financial condition, he continued his early habit of accounting in detail for income and outgo. The practice, he explained, kept him up to date on his financial cireumstances. No matter how small the sum it was noted in his ledger. Entries of five cents occur frequently for such expenditures as tolls on bridges over the Cuyahoga, as well as on certain roads; and for “car” rides, which meant trips on horsecars then widely in use. The small size cars were the bane of women wearing hoop skirts, until the invention of the “tilting” or collapsible device. If Rockefeller bought a postage stamp, its cost, five cents or less, was jotted down, along with the five cents and one cent that one time he gave to his sisters, Lucy and Mary Ann. He apparently was fond of “sodas” (maybe ice cream soda?), and each time he indulged himself in the treat, the price, ten cents, went into the book. In February of this year there were notations of from five cents to one dollar and twenty cents under “Fair.” In other words, Rockefeller had been attending the Northern Ohio Sanitary Fair which opened that month on the Square with great ado. General Garfield, who had resigned from the army and was now a United States Congressman, gave the principal address at the dedication of the vast new building. It was arranged in the form of a Greek cross

expenses. The date selected for his own wedding was September 8th, one day before his bride’s twenty-fifth birthday. True to his principles, he meticulously entered each one in his ledger. He bought “Bouquettes” at from fifty cents to a dollar and a half. He paid three dollars for a “Viynette” [sic]—his spelling still troublesome. Another, spelled correctly this time, cost five dollars and seventy-five cents. On the “Fourth of July,” he spent three dollars and forty-eight cents. With the steadily mounting count of Cleveland men killed or wounded in Civil War battles in the South, the city’s mood was a sober one on this year’s holiday. The event of the day was a procession of wounded veterans who were cheered and wept over by crowds of patriotic citizens along the line of march. The culmination was a public banquet for the heroes. Rockefeller’s entry may have covered the price of tickets and transportation for himself and Cettie. The journal mentioned numerous “drives,” outings, probably in rigs hired from a livery stable. One drive was as far away as Rocky River at a cost of a dollar and seventy-five cents—in all a ten mile round trip! The account book disclosed other expenses, some sizable:

> dentistry Mary Ann History of Rebellion blister wine [surprising! ] runaway accident

> $ 50.00 101.50 (Lake Superior trip) 4.00 BS) 50 2.60

Rockefeller bought a wedding ring for Cettie for $15.75 and had it engraved. He began getting himself ready for the wedding and the tour he had mapped out:

umbrella, bag repaired $ 75 new bag [valise?] 7.00 opera glasses 16.50 covering trunks 8.00 In those days of late summer Rockefeller was busier than he ever had been. The Andrews, Clark refinery was going very well, better every day. In 1863, when they first took over their threeacre site, they had leased the land, but within a few months they bought it outright. They gave the operation a name: The Excelsior Works. The real head of the venture, as well as of the commission house, was John D. Rockefeller. Despite these responsibilities, his ledger shows that he took plenty of time out for his romance. He was an ardent bridegroom.

> $ .75 7.00 16.50 8.00

## Page 70

On the day of the wedding he arranged a special dinner for his refinery employees—there were twenty-six of them now. Naturally reticent, he had divulged nothing about his marriage. The men surely would have suspected something of what was up from his rare joviality. As he prepared to leave the works, he called gaily to the superintendent, admonishing him to keep everyone busy. “But don’t ask anybody to work for nothing,” he added as he hurried off with a light step, a smile on his usually serious face. “The boss is in love!” A month-long honeymoon had been planned. Rockefeller recorded the details, beginning with a succinct item: “September 8, married at 2 o'clock p.m. to Miss L. C. Spelman by Reverend D. Wolcott assisted by Reverend Page at the residence of her parents, 55 Huron Street.” (Actually the number was 58. ) Wolcott was not “D” but Samuel Wolcott, pastor of Plymouth Congregational Church, Prospect and Erie Streets, where the Spelmans worshipped. The other clergyman was S. B. Page of Erie Street Baptist Church. A notation in Ledger B, “Marriage, $20” presumably indicated what he paid the two ministers. No notice of the wedding appeared in any Cleveland newspaper. No description has come to light of what the bride wore. Judging from earlier photographs of her, doubtless she was a trim and stylish bride. A photograph of the two on Mount Washington, New Hampshire, during their wedding trip, showed the bridegroom, distinguished looking in a long frock coat and high silk hat, the attire of the well-dressed man. Six days before the wedding, Sherman had entered Atlanta. The war was tapering off, but far from over. Every train into Cleveland brought wounded, crippled veterans, who became a common sight on city streets. Ledger B reveals that Rockefeller at this time allotted monthly stipends of from fifteen to twenty dollars to substitutes and “Families of Soldiers from the 4th Ward.” There were also fifty dollar amounts to the Ward’s War Fund, as well as contributions for purchase of revolvers and the like for enlisted men. Following the wedding ceremony, a shiny brougham drew up in front of the Spelman home. The driver climbed down from his

<!-- page_id: 0046-right | printed: 71 | viewer_token: 70 | viewer_sequence: 94/340 -->
## Page 71

high seat to stand at his horse’s head, waiting for the happy couple to appear. Rockefeller had hired the carriage for two dollars from the livery stable around the corner to take him and the new Mrs. Rockefeller to the railroad station. It would have been the old Union Depot at the foot of Bank and Water Streets. The resplendent new Union Depot, then being planned, was not to be built for another two years. The Rockefellers’ first stop was Buffalo. The fare was ten dollars. After a few days there, they made the traditional honeymoon expedition to Niagara Falls. With stopovers along the way, the couple traveled by rail to Montreal and on to Quebec. Always committed to go to Sunday morning and evening church services wherever the honeymoon schedule took them, in Quebec, Rockefeller noted, they attended a Scotch Methodist Church. After a voyage on the St. Lawrence “by steamer” and a train journey to New England, they visited for a week in Boston “with wife’s friends.” The stay included a side trip to “Wooster” (Rockefeller’s spelling). Cettie wanted her husband to see the castle-like eminence surmounting its hilly campus that was the Oread Collegiate Institute. Or, maybe she wanted Oread to see the tall handsome husband she had acquired! Also on this call they met for the first time Sophia B. Packard and Harriet E. Giles, who only a few weeks before had joined the Institute faculty. Miss Packard was executive head and the other a teacher. These remarkable women, both Baptists, left Oread in 1867, and after an interval devoted largely to Baptist home missions, founded a school for Negro women in Atlanta, Georgia. Years later, with Rockefeller support, it developed as Spelman College. The bride and groom, however, had no intimation of such a development on this carefree stopover at Oread. They went on to Fairfield, Connecticut, where William Rockefeller and his bride of a few months were visiting her parents. All of the Rockefeller family liked William’s friendly, talkative wife, Mira. John and Cettie had a few days in New York City, perhaps the first time either had seen the metropolis. Heading homeward, they booked passage on a Hudson River boat as far as Albany. At Buf-

falo they could not resist one more look at Niagara Falls before boarding the train for Cleveland. Like tourists everywhere, they spent money for souvenirs and keepsakes, as well as for necessities—all carefully accounted for, including: looking glass $ .75 silver cups 20.00 broom [two of these, probably whiskbrooms] .60 view of Niagara 15 cushion 1.57 pictures 1.50 hair restorer [a grooming aid] 1.00 Davis & Company, livery 8.00 Rockefeller indeed must have been enjoying himself. For once he failed to make daily entries of expenditures. It also was less than Ledger B’s allocation that year for charity, a total of $671.86. One month was what Rockefeller had allotted for his honeymoon, and it was concluded within one month to the day. The couple returned to Cleveland on October 8th to make their home with the Doctor and Eliza at 33 Cheshire Street. They reached Cleveland on Saturday. (No traveling on the Sabbath.) Next day, so the ledger reported, both were at Sabbath School and church. It was the Erie Street Baptist and not Plymouth Congregational Church. Cettie was deferring to John. Most people did. The following day, Monday, Rockefeller was back in harness, writing the one word “Business” as a new heading in his ledger. Tuesday he was off to Chicago, returning on Saturday to resume on Sunday the routine of church and Sabbath School. By now, Rockefeller was undisputed lay leader of the congregation, working in a Baptist mission branch, teaching Sunday School and helping wherever he could. Mrs. Rockefeller soon was drawn in, beginning as teacher of an infant class.
