## Page 110

came to be a world-renowned painting, “The Spirit of ’76.” The artist, Archibald Willard, a friend of Devereux, painted it in Cleveland and then exhibited it in cities across the country. One of several “originals” created by Willard was purchased by Devereux and given to his birthplace, Marblehead, Massachusetts. What may be the earliest extant “original” is owned by The Western Reserve Historical Society. Another hangs in Cleveland’s City Hall. While paintings were not among Rockefeller’s interests, railroads were, and the General and he had numerous contacts thrugh the years. Faced with storms that in 1871 still beset “the oil trade,” Rockefeller also was giving much attention to home affairs. Laura was expecting their third child, Alta, who was born in April. Another girl! It was said that the father had hoped this time the baby would be a boy, an heir apparent for the kingdom he was building. But a girl was God’s will, and the child was welcome. Alta inherited certain of her father’s traits, like his organizing ability. She was a “doer.” Father and daughter grew increasingly companionable. She was born partially deaf, a handicap that won Rockefeller’s sympathy. The affliction was overcome later through treatments in Vienna. Instability in the oil business persisted. Companies were foundering, threatening to draw others with them into the depths. After lengthy conferences with his partners, Rockefeller evolved a way to save the industry. It was not a wholly altruistic conception. The Standard would benefit as well, if order could be established within the oil ranks. What he termed “Our Plan” followed these lines: Cleveland refineries, and eventually those in other parts of the country, would be asked to combine in partnership with the Standard Oil Company as the nucleus. It was a philosophy of “united we survive, divided we perish.” Overhead costs thus would be lessened, duplication eliminated, and production volume increased. The result would be lowered prices for the consumer and higher profits for the refiner. With his genius for looking ahead, Rockefeller saw that industrial combination was to be the way of the future. Indeed, though

<!-- page_id: 0070-right | printed: 111 | viewer_token: 110 | viewer_sequence: 142/340 -->
## Page 111

little realized, it already had arrived in areas of the economy. The idea, however, was new and viewed with suspicion by citizens who yet had to hear of a supermarket or a national chain of retail outlets. While Rockefeller was formulating his plan, another was being nurtured with utmost secrecy by a handful of Pittsburgh and Philadelphia refiners inspired by Thomas A. Scott, vice president of the Pennsylvania Railroad. In essence the scheme was to be a union of refiners and railroads for mutual gain: the refiners guaranteed rebates on oil haulage and the railroads assured the oil shipments. A nefarious provision was added, namely, “drawbacks.” Outside refiners were to pay top freight rates, half of which would go back to a pool to be divided among the member refiners. A more ruthless device scarcely could be imagined for squeezing out independent oil manufacturers. Early in 1872 the organizers acquired the charter of a nonexistent corporation known as the South Improvement Company. The name had no relation to the intended activities. Rockefeller and Flagler, testifying afterward at a federal hearing, insisted they had no part in framing the scheme; they knew nothing about it until much later. “It was not our idea,” Rockefeller declared to his biographer, Inglis in 1917. Ida M. Tarbell, brilliant high priestess of the muckrakers who were to excoriate Rockefeller in coming decades, claimed otherwise: “Rockefeller and Flagler,” she wrote, “went in at once and began to work up the company—secretly.” Nevins disagreed. After careful study he concluded both men were telling the truth. Another biographer, Flynn, agreed with him in part: “It is almost certain,” he wrote, “it did not originate with Rockefeller.” But he was not so certain about the role of Flagler, who, he believed, was in it from the start. Unfortunately for Rockefeller’s standing, the Standard Oil Company did ally itself with the South Improvement Company later. However, before that step was taken, Rockefeller had set about putting into operation his own plan, which he always insisted was a much sounder system for strengthening the industry.

As deliberate as an expert chess player, Rockefeller made the first move in “Our Plan” to bring other Cleveland refineries within the orbit of the Standard Oil Company. One late December morning of 1871, he dispatched a message from his office in the Cushing Block on Public Square to the Second National Bank in its handsome new quarters at Superior and Water Streets. There his friend Stillman Witt was a leading director. The president of the Standard Oil Company wished to have a conference with Colonel Oliver H. Payne that afternoon at three o'clock in the bank’s rooms. Would someone kindly see that the Colonel got the word? The abrupt summoning of Payne was a characteristic Rockefeller technique; once a decision was made, he acted immediately. A few minutes before three o'clock the tall figure of John D. Rockefeller could be seen proceeding with a purposeful gait, but not hurrying, across the Square. Snow was falling, powdering his tall hat and well-cut black overcoat. As he reached the bank doorway and was shaking off the flakes, a clock somewhere began to strike the hour. The Colonel was waiting for him. Colonel Payne’s works, Clark, Payne & Company on Walworth Run, was the Standard’s closest competitor. The Clark part of the firm consisted of Maurice and James Clark, Rockefeller’s onetime partners. John D.’s early associates had a strange way of appearing and reappearing in his saga like actors coming and going through scenes of a play. Holding the greatest share of stock, Payne was the actual head of the company and it was with him Rockefeller wished to deal. He came directly to the point, offering the Colonel the opportunity to merge with Standard Oil on a partnership basis. There would be a management role for Payne, but Rockefeller would have nothing to do with the Clarks. Payne had suspected the object of this meeting before Rockefeller disclosed it. The Walworth Run plant, substantial as it was,

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number, image_text_overlap:0.179 -->

<!-- page_id: 0071-right | printed: 113 | viewer_token: 112 | viewer_sequence: 144/340 -->
## Page 113

had been losing money of late, and Payne knew the oil industry was in bad shape generally—with the exception of the Standard. The Colonel was not long in acceding to the combination idea, confident he could convince his stockholders likewise. In the settlement that followed, Payne’s company received $400,000 worth of Standard Oil Company stock, or 4,000 shares. It represented payment for the plant and other assets as well as for good will and Payne’s brains. One of Rockefeller’s aims was to bring able men into the Standard Oil Company. Payne was one of them and before long he was put on the board of directors. James Clark, who acquired 1,000 shares as his portion, evidently was as much of a problem to Payne as he had been to Rockefeller whom he never ceased to abuse. Tired of Clark’s tirades, Payne bought his stock at a price above market value. Clark was jubilant. He had extracted a profit from Rockefeller’s Standard Oil Company! Later, as the stock soared he assumed what became a familiar stance: cursing the Standard’s president for the lost wealth. Rockefeller, on his part, could understand why men might prefer United States currency to the then unstable oil industry's stock, but he never could comprehend why they later invariably blamed him for their decision. Rockefeller’s basic strategy was to approach the strongest refineries first. Success with them would influence the lesser firms to follow, he reasoned. Making his initial overture to Payne, a carefully thought out step it is certain, was a master stroke. Payne was a member of the wealthy Perry-Payne family. The Perrys were connected with Commodore Oliver Hazard Perry, hence young Payne’s name, Oliver Hazard. The Colonel was an aristocrat and fully aware of the fact. He and Rockefeller were born the same year, but they had little else in common, save a respect for each other's business sagacity. One can think of no greater contrast in lineage than between the two young men. Rockefeller, with a father listed in the Cleveland city directory as a botanic physician, was descended on his mother’s side from an obscure farmer in New York State. Cultured Henry B. Payne, the Colonel's father, had made his money in law

eee Flip

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:67.5 -->

<!-- page_id: 0072-left | printed: 114 | viewer_token: 114 | viewer_sequence: 146/340 -->
## Page 114

and railroads. He was prominent in the national Democratic party, was a United States Congressman and later a Senator. Oliver Payne's mother was the daughter of Nathan Perry, Jr., whose father, Nathan Perry, Sr., had come to Cleveland the first time with General Moses Cleaveland. On his second trip here from Connecticut he bought tracts of land in several sections of the Western Reserve, including 1,000 acres in the young city General Cleaveland had founded. The Payne mansion at this time stood next to the Perry homestead amidst extensive grounds on Euclid Avenue at the corner of Perry Street. However, the Paynes when first married had lived some distance away on St. Clair Street. Every morning the bride’s father saddled a horse before breakfast to canter over the fields to see his daughter. All the way he was riding through his own property, crossing what was to become Payne Avenue and a wide stretch that later was known as “Payne’s Pastures.” When John D. was attending Central High School in Cleveland, Oliver Payne was a student at Phillips Exeter Academy in the East. From there he went to Yale University. He interrupted his college studies to enlist in the army at the outbreak of the Civil War, serving with distinction. He never married and mixed with only a few select friends. Flagler referred to him as “the kin of God.” Diffident though Payne might be with Rockefeller socially, the two got on well together in business. The Colonel was made treasurer of the Standard and invested heavily in the company’s stock as well as in other enterprises. After retiring he moved to New York where he developed an estate in Ulster County. He also made trips to Europe in his yacht, the Aphrodite. He was the uncle of Cleveland’s veteran Congresswoman, Frances Payne (Bingham) Bolton and her sister, the late Mrs. Dudley S. Blossom. With the prestigious Payne in his camp, Rockefeller turned to other refiners. Next he won the sturdy Yorkshireman, John Stanley, who received 200 shares for his small but well established plant. Operation of the firm had been an uphill struggle of late and Stanley did not disguise his relief at coming under the wing of the Standard Oil Company.

/ ] On January 1, 1872, the Standard increased its capitalization to $2,500,000. Witt and Stone each bought 500 shares and Handy, 400. The Long Island refiner, Jabez Bostwick, an admirer of Rockefeller and his company, came into the fold receiving 700 shares for Z a desirable plant that included a terminal of importance to the Standard. There were other new shareholders, one of them Peter H. Watson, freight agent for the Lake Shore Railroad. He also was one of the men working to form the South Improvement Company, _ _ but this was known to only a few. ] Rockefeller now presided over a much larger group than in the ¢= beginning days when he and his four partners were in charge. He - was no novice in the role of chairman, for he had learned the art as a teen-ager when appointed head of the Erie Street Church’s { _ board of trustees, all older than he. Except for Colonel Payne, the Standard’s new directors were also his seniors. . As chairman he was fully in command and thoroughly informed ar # on every detail of company operations. Yet Rockefeller’s mild manin ner deceived the uninitiated. Director Stone differed with him one ut day in an encounter as reported by Rockefeller. Stone suggested 1 _ the controversy be settled by two directors whom he would name. § §©6>-_ Rockefeller agreed and Stone selected Witt and Colonel Payne. On the way home that night Flagler was nervous about the outcome, but not Rockefeller. “I am right,” he said succinctly. When the meeting took up the following day, Stone reminded f | the two arbiters that it was he who had appointed them. NeverthegE less, their verdict was in favor of Rockefeller. Stone stormed out of ‘ | = the room. | Contemporaries found Stone gentle and pleasant in his home, Ma) but in public the thick set, heavily built man was domineering, al- “MN ways insisting on being “top of the heap.” He resented the rise of j Rockefeller, who was nearly twenty years his junior, a poorly paid 2 clerk when Stone was already a man of affairs. His dislike of Rockefeller flared again over an option he held for a the purchase of additional shares of Standard Oil stock. He failed ( to present it until after expiration of the option date. Rockefeller refused to recognize the right because of the time element. Stone

## Page 116

was so furious that Flagler advised letting him have the shares. Rockefeller would not and the disgruntled man sold all the stock he held. Several times during January 1872, Rockefeller was in New York for more or less extended stays. From references in letters to his wife, it is clear that he was then working with the men formulating the South Improvement Company. He wrote to Laura about “a new plan which grows on me.” And again: “we got our contract from Scot... and I feel well over it.” He obviously was much occupied with these negotiations, but, also, he had his wife and home in his heart and mind. Another baby was on the way. Bessie was four, Alta, eight months. Their mother, it appeared, felt burdened by this pregnancy and the prospect of a third child so soon. Her husband tried to cheer her. Addressing her as “My Beloved Wife,” he told her about attending a Baptist church service where he met a Mrs. Gribble (evidently known to both). “she remarked how much comfort Harriet (her daughter) had been to her,” Rockefeller wrote, “and she didn’t want her when she came . . . how do we know that in old age we may be left to lean on who we hoped would delay coming for some time” (the italics are Rockefeller’s). “Don’t allow any misgivings,” he continued. “We together will make the best of our experiences and try for all the good and happiness that can come from our mutual life.” He added a P. S. “I dreamed of the girl Cettie Spelman and woke to realize she was my Laura.” Letters went from Laura to him, often long letters. She had driven in the carriage—there was not enough snow for the sleigh—to call on Mrs. Eyears and had stopped to see Mrs. Chisholm. The latter had been hurt in an accident, possibly a runaway. At the door she met the injured woman’s physician, Dr. Henry K. Cushing (whose son, Harvey W., became a celebrated neuro-surgeon). It was from Dr. Henry Cushing that Rockefeller rented his office space. The physician razed the family homestead and on its site, in 1870, erected the Cushing Block (eastern part of the May Company building). Sending news of the children, Mrs. Rockefeller described “how

<!-- page_id: 0077-right | printed: 117 | viewer_token: 116 | viewer_sequence: 156/340 -->
## Page 117

prettily Baby has learned to kiss . . . but somehow she has taken a fresh cold . . . Bessie sends thanks to her Papa for her music box ... Mr. and Mrs. Sam Andrews spent the evening with me .. . The Flaglers dropped in. . . .” John D. wrote his wife he had arranged to have her parents visit her. They would be company for her while he was away. He was paying their expenses. Apparently only her mother came, and when she left, Laura was more lonesome than ever. She missed her husband. He, in turn, longed for “wings to reach you tonight.” He had been staying at William’s house. “Tell Bessie that last night I slept with Emma (William’s baby daughter) and O! did she kick! I feel more than ever thankful for a true and loving wife. Why shouldn't a man be stimulated to effort! the world is full of Sham, Flattery and Deception, our home is a haven of rest and freedom.” Between trips Rockefeller resumed his program of persuading “the important Cleveland refineries” to join with Standard. Giving them a choice of cash or stock in the company, he always counselled taking shares. The oilman needed to conserve all the cash possible. Also, Rockefeller had absolute faith in the Standard Oil Company. | He believed in it as he believed in God and the Baptist church. Those who accepted his injunction made fortunes. Many of the others became his enemies. Rockefeller invariably tried to discharge debts for plant improvements by urging contractors to take payment in stock. Two brothers, John and Hugh Huntington, had installed fireproof roofing under a sizeable contract. John reluctantly settled for stock. Hugh held out for cash. John became a multi-millionaire, leaving many bequests to Cleveland, including an endowment to help found the Museum of Art. Hugh, on the other hand, remained a man of modest means. For one refiner, Alexander, Scofield & Company, losses had forced the firm to suspend operation for a time, and the partners sold out. They wanted no stock, for they heartily disliked John D. Both of these Englishmen had pioneered in illuminant manufacture in the Flats, and they found it hard to accept the rapid rise of the young upstart Rockefeller.

| ee Fp

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:58.16 -->

<!-- page_id: 0078-left | printed: 118 | viewer_token: 118 | viewer_sequence: 158/340 -->
## Page 118

John Alexander nursed a particular grudge because his early assistant, and fellow countryman, Sam Andrews, had left him to go with Rockefeller—and had prospered! The firm’s relations with Rockefeller were not improved in the case of William Scofield (not to be confused with William Sc(h)ofield) when his daughter Helen married Frank Rockefeller whose contacts with his brother were growing strained. The fact that Frank had also had an interest in another refinery bought out by the Standard did nothing to sweeten his attitude toward John. Alexander and Scofield later denounced Rockefeller as having cheated them in the price paid for their plant. Similar charges were leveled at the Standard by other refiners who had sold. Rockefeller countered such criticism as due often to an owner's exaggerated notion of his property’s worth, plus disregard for the inroads of age and obsolescence in a plant. Isaac Hewitt, Rockefeller’s old employer, the only man he ever worked for, was a partner in the Alexander & Hewitt refinery. An evaluation of $20,000 was put on his holdings when the company was planning to sell out to the Standard. Hewitt consulted his former bookkeeper as to whether he should take it in cash or stock. Rockefeller argued for the latter. Hewitt, however, disregarded the advice. Before long it was plain to him—and to his daughters—that he had thrown away an opportunity to become a millionaire. A few years later he testified at a government hearing that he had been “threatened” by Rockefeller and had sold under duress, all of which was vehemently denied by Rockefeller. The muckrakers preferred to believe Hewitt and quoted him often. Frank A. Arter, another Clevelander, left a different story for posterity. Arter had been in difficulties with his refinery and was ready to unload to the Standard. Rockefeller’s appraiser, Ambrose McGregor, a redheaded, conscientious Scot, went to work with his customary thoroughness. He never overlooked a thing, not even a hammer or a screwdriver, in his effort to compile a fair list. This time he came up with a salvage value of $3,000 for a plant that had cost Arter $12,000. Also, the oilman was shouldering a $25,000 debt, and he felt the money settlement was the only alternative for

<!-- page_id: 0078-right | printed: 119 | viewer_token: 118 | viewer_sequence: 158/340 -->
## Page 119

him. Rockefeller and Flagler, whom Arter knew, counselled him to take the stock if he possibly could manage. He did, and after a struggle Arter emerged a very wealthy man. His son, Charles K. Arter, often made a point of telling how much the family felt “obliged to Mr. Rockefeller—a very great obligation, indeed.” In February 1872, the Plain Dealer broke the story of the South Improvement Company. A fury like hot and searing lava erupted against the program. Crowds of angry men in the Oil Regions staged mass meetings that were near-riots, labeling the “company” a “monster,” an “octopus,” and its members “thieves and robbers.” As soon as it was discovered that the Standard Oil Company had joined, imprecations of the most violent sort were directed at Rockefeller. Rockefeller was the Standard Oil Company to the public. Neither he nor Flagler was prepared for the virulence of the uproar. Even Rockefeller’s most laudatory biographers criticize him for subscribing to the South Improvement Company. The questionable feature was not so much in the rebate provision since reduced freight cost for big shipments was a recognized commercial practice. The evil lay in the drawbacks arrangement. The South Improvement Company cannot be ignored in any study of Rockefeller, for, short-lived as the “company” was, it contributed to the cumulative, intense enmity focused on the Standard Oil head until well into the early nineteen hundreds. Rockefeller explained many years later his reasons for the Standard’s participation. The oil industry situation was desperate. Though he thought the proposition not as good as “Our Plan,” it was a ready-made scheme backed by powerful refiners and railroads. He could not afford to antagonize them, for if the proposed setup failed, he would need their cooperation to put across his own conception of a united oil industry. The opposition proved too much for the South Improvement Company. Within a matter of weeks it fell apart without ever starting to function. Not a barrel of oil was carried under the arrangement, Rockefeller emphasized when discussing it later. While the furor was raging he would make no comment. He barred his doors to reporters and refused to give out any statement.

| es Fp

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:58.77 -->

<!-- page_id: 0079-left | printed: 120 | viewer_token: 120 | viewer_sequence: 160/340 -->
## Page 120

He explained his stand to his wife, writing to her from New York in March. Newspapers were full of the excitement and of accusations against him. Laura was greatly disturbed as she indicated in her letters to him. “It is easy to write newspaper articles,” her husband told her. “We will do right and not be nervous or troubled by what the papers say .. . by and by [sic] when we are through we may briefly respond (though it is not our policy). But leave future events to demonstrate our intentions and plans were warranted. I want to act perfectly conscientiously and fearlessly in the matter and feel confident of good results.” In refusing to talk, Rockefeller made a mistake, as he realized later. The public interpreted his silence as an admission of guilt. With these events swirling about him, his church was never neglected. He took his place as superintendent of the Sunday School at the Euclid Avenue Baptist Church and made notes about the sermon for Laura if she was unable to attend. He gave much attention to the new church building being erected at this time at Euclid and Huntington Street. He and Henry Chisholm were the heavy contributors to the building fund. Chisholm tithed, but not Rockefeller. Instead, he matched members’ pledges dollar for dollar. No reliable record of his gifts to this church is available. In what must be regarded as incomplete total, the sum is put at $755,466 over this period and a little beyond. The Rockefellers’ new baby arrived in August and was named Edith. Three small children—Bessie, Alta and Edith—to liven up the Euclid Avenue home! If they were all girls, their father loved them, every one, for he was a doting parent. Cleveland’s Union Club, an exclusive town club for men, that had been incorporated at the beginning of the year, was organized in September 1872. The club purchased the Handy mansion on lower Euclid Avenue. Officers were elected with William Bingham, wholesale hardware merchant, named president, and Henry B. Payne, vice president. The committee on admissions consisted of Amasa Stone, Henry Chisholm, J. H. Wade and Oliver H. Payne. Writing about Rockefeller, authors like to picture him and Flagler at luncheon in the rarified atmosphere of the Club, deep in discussion of business problems. Actually, neither Rockefeller nor

<!-- page_id: 0079-right | printed: 121 | viewer_token: 120 | viewer_sequence: 160/340 -->
## Page 121

Flagler was a member of the Union Club, nor was William Rockefeller. The only Rockefeller who ever belonged was Frank, who was a member from 1890 until his death. The Club, today located on Euclid Avenue at East 12th Street, purchased this property in 1901 from Mrs. William B. Castle, whose son-in-law, D. Z. Norton, was an early member. The Castle home was razed, and the present club house completed in 1905. By midsummer of 1872, all but a half dozen of the many oil refineries in Cleveland either had united with Standard Oil Company or had been purchased outright. Rockefeller at thirty-three was the head of the largest oil company in the country. And Cleveland was the nation’s oil center. Ida Tarbell contended that Cleveland oil plants had been crushed by the Standard head. And, paralyzed, they collapsed into the Standard’s net, she declared. Nonsense, Rockefeller retorted. They already had collapsed and were near bankruptcy. “Most of the refiners were very desirous to get out of the oil business,” he wrote in his Reminiscences. Within a few years, several of the important oil men who had been loudest in their castigation of Rockefeller for his part in the South Improvement Company joined the Standard. Notable among them was the boyish Titusville refiner, the able John D. Archbold, who ultimately succeeded Rockefeller as president of the Standard.

> 20. A Son and A Summer Home

With a laggard January dawn breaking over the sky, a short, plump woman carrying a doctor's satchel hurried up the steps of the Rockefeller home on Euclid Avenue. At her approach, the big front door swung open promptly, for Dr. Myra K. Merrick was expected. A familiar figure in this house, she went directly to Mrs. Rockefeller’s bedroom upstairs, radiating good cheer and self-assurance with every step. Before many hours had passed, she came briskly out of the room

— Ee Fp

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:76.25 -->

<!-- page_id: 0080-left | printed: 122 | viewer_token: 122 | viewer_sequence: 162/340 -->
## Page 122

to confront Rockefeller patiently waiting nearby. “You have a fine son, Mr. Rockefeller,” she announced triumphantly. And shortly she put in his arms a blanketed mite of humanity who, it was understood, would be named John D. Rockefeller, Jr. Later that day the elated father burst into his office with the news. Never had his associates seen him so excited. He had dropped his mask of formality and, for the first time, his emotions were in view. With an onrush of affection, the men crowded around him to shake his hand. Even Payne unbent. He and Flagler stood beside him, their arms around his shoulders. Tears glistened in Rockefeller’s eyes. It was January 29, 1874. The great day was described in a letter written by Mrs. Rockefeller to her son on his birthday years later:

Twenty-two years ago today I remember almost as well as though it were yesterday. The scene was laid in our upstairs bedroom at 997 Euclid Avenue—the hour was ten o'clock in the morning, the day has slipped my memory (actually it was Saturday). The persons were Dr. Merrick, Sr. (her niece Eliza was Dr. Merrick, Jr.) and Mrs. Lobb (a nurse). Father was across the hall. How glad we were the baby was a boy for there had been four girls and that he was perfectly formed. Though the first three years of his life were filled with anxious care for his health, he now stands a man of strength and vi- CONS: ze

Dr. Merrick, Cleveland’s pioneer woman physician, was widely recognized as a skilled obstetrician. Male practioners, it is said, often secretly consulted her in difficult cases. Having overcome the roadblocks against entry of women in the medical profession, she now numbered prominent Clevelanders among her many patients, including practically all the wives of Standard Oil officials. A homeopath and a woman of great charm, she was regarded as a family friend by the Rockefellers. They never forgot that it was she who brought John D. Jr. into the world, and they were generous | supporters of her free clinic for women and her work to establish ‘Huron Road Hospital. The arrival of this long-awaited son was accepted by his parents as answer to their prayers. Consequently they determined to mold

<!-- page_id: 0080-right | printed: 123 | viewer_token: 122 | viewer_sequence: 162/340 -->
## Page 123

him after their image of righteousness and duty. No details survive of the delicate babyhood mentioned by his mother in her letter. His Aunt Lucy referred to a stay in Colorado when he was a year old, and this possibly was for the child’s health. Then, too, his parents may have been over-solicitous in the guarding of this only boy. Nevertheless, John and Laura Rockefeller seemed more concerned with their children’s spiritual than with their physical wellbeing. Shortly before young John’s birth, his father acquired Forest Hill, a tract of a little over seventy-nine acres bordering Euclid Avenue in East Cleveland, part of the extensive lands of the Doans who were early settlers. The site was four miles east of the Rockefeller Euclid Avenue home and seven miles from the Standard Block on Euclid Avenue facing Sheriff Street, new headquarters of the Standard Oil Company. While Forest Hill was to become the family’s beloved summer home, originally Rockefeller had no such plan in mind. He bought the property as an investment. Perhaps he was following the lead of Stephen Harkness, who believed in the future of Cleveland and was buying real estate particularly along Euclid Avenue east from Willson Street. Rockefeller sold his acreage in December 1875 to the Euclid Avenue Forest Hill Association and then bought shares in the company. Its purpose was to establish a “water-cure and place of public resort.” The company was incorporated at a capitalization of $250,000 with 2,500 shares issued at $100 each. Incorporators were: Marcus W. Montgomery, C. B. Dutton, W. F. Walworth and John D. Rockefeller. As part of this enterprise, Rockefeller and Montgomery with several others earlier had organized the Lake View & Collamer Railroad, described in its prospectus as a “deluxe standard gauge dummy steamline.” It was routed east from Lockyear Avenue and today’s East 71st Street, near where the Superior Street horsecar line ended and, keeping to the north of Superior Street, went to Chardon Road, or Collamer. High wooden trestles carried the tracks over the valley of Doan Brook where later Rockefeller Park was laid out, and also over Nine Mile Creek. Harkness was in this venture, too, for he and Rockefeller do-

## Page 124

nated land near Lake View Avenue for a roundhouse and repair shops, which gave employment to a number of men. Rockefeller personally interviewed each worker, warning them that indulgence in intoxicating liquor on or off the job would cost them their positions. A notice in the Leader in September 1875 stated that the Lake View & Collamer Railroad expected delivery in a day or two from the Globe Iron Works in Pittsburgh of a locomotive and coaches to accommodate one hundred and fifty passengers. Stockholders had voted to lease the road for fifteen years to L. M. Tantum at a rental beginning at $3,240 for the first year. Capitalization of the railroad was $250,000 with shares of stock to be sold at $50 a share. Value of the equipment was put at $90,000. The directors, in addition to Rockefeller and Montgomery were: William West, president, John H. Ammon, Isaac H. Page, James McCloskey and Hezekiah Avery, most of them East Cleveland residents. On October 7th the undersize engine, glittering in brass trim, pulling small, steam-heated cars, clattered over the tracks on the first run. The road was well patronized. The community of Collamer, thrived with an influx of settlers, new stores, and churches, all dependent on the little railroad. The road’s terminus at the Nickel Plate right of way in East Cleveland was in close proximity to the water-cure resort getting under way at Forest Hill. This, and the fact that two men, Rockefeller and Montgomery, were the leading promoters of both the railroad and the Forest Hill resort, show the close tie-up between the two undertakings—each would stimulate patronage of the other. But the country was hit by a depression. Both enterprises failed. It is difficult to determine whether the little railroad’s collapse brought about that of the water-cure resort or vice versa. Backers of both lost money—including Rockefeller. A most unusual circumstance as far as he was concerned. Today nothing remains to mark the route of the Lake View & Collamer Railroad. The only reminders of its existence are stock certificates, now collectors’ items. Forest Hill fared better. Rockefeller was not slow in buying it back. He probably had explored the slopes and deep valleys sev-

<!-- page_id: 0081-right | printed: 125 | viewer_token: 124 | viewer_sequence: 164/340 -->

eral times on horseback, both when he first acquired it and also when plans were afoot for the water-cure development. Laura Rockefeller liked Forest Hill from the moment she saw it. The big, rambling building intended to house the sanitarium was half finished when work was abandoned. ## Page 127

### 21. Crowded Years

Rockefeller’s excursion into operation of a dummy railroad and a water-cure resort is all the more amazing when one considers that at this time he was in the midst of one of the most eventful and grueling periods of his career. The Standard’s absorption of Cleveland refineries was largely completed with amalgamation or purchase of more than twenty (the figure sometimes put at over thirty) local plants. In the wake of this accomplishment Rockefeller and Flagler along with Payne, now in the company’s upper echelon, were faced with many decisions: which plants should be scrapped; which updated and retained; who of the inherited personnel to add to the Standard family; who to pension off. . . . In the end six big refineries emerged under the Standard banner. Number 1 and Number 2 plants were original Standard units. The other four were reconditioned and remodelled from those taken over. As vigilant as in the days of a more limited operation, Rockefeller kept in touch with the sweeping changes. He appeared in the yards to check outgoing freight. In the office a bookkeeper might find himself displaced from his high stool while “Mr. Rockefeller” looked over a ledger. Inspecting a lineup of barrels one day, he wondered if the overlap in the iron bands around them could be shortened with safety, and he assigned the thorough going Mc- Gregor to find out. The Scot in due time reported that several inches might be eliminated with no risk to reinforcement. The resultant decrease in the required metal saved the company several thousands of dollars—“a small fortune,” the president commented with great satisfaction. Despite his heavy schedule, Rockefeller often interviewed former employees of disbanded plants as well as other job seekers. On the lookout for promising individuals to add to Standard’s staffs, he was known to spend as much time in patient examination of an inexperienced teen-ager as with a skilled older applicant. O09

## Page 128

The oil industry was caught up again—or still—in the twin bogey of too much crude and an overcrowded refinery field. It was affected, too, by the country’s financial troubles. Panic had gripped the nation with the sudden collapse in 1873 of the banking house of Jay Cooke, the man who had been such a factor in financing the Union cause in the Civil War. Attempting to bring order out of confusion, certain manufacturers formed the National Refiners’ Association. Producers also united. But both soon broke up. Although Rockefeller was elected president of the refiners’ group, he had little faith in either one, calling them “ropes of sand.” Instead, he turned more vigorously than ever to his bold design of strengthening the petroleum business generally—and benefiting the Standard—by merging refineries throughout the country with the company as he had done in the case of Cleveland plants. The West required little attention since the Standard’s geographic location provided natural ties. In the South he extended his hold by purchasing half interest in a Louisville company whose distributing outlets opened the way soon for Standard’s departure into retail selling. However, the bulk of oil manufacturing and some of the strongest refineries in the United States were to be found within a roughly triangular area embracing parts of Pennsylvania, New York and New England. Rockefeller and Flagler in the early 1870s were concentrating in this section. Representatives of hard-to-win, out-of-town oil companies sometimes were invited to Cleveland where they might be met at the railroad station by the Rockefeller carriage with its trimly liveried coachman and sleek horses. After a tour of the Standard’s superbly functioning works and an examination of company books at the busy office in the Standard Block, the visitors, returning home, invariably recommended affiliating. Important refiners, responding to the proposals, were coming into the union one by one. Jabez A. Bostwick’s Long Island Oil Company with a valuable refinery and terminal at Hunter's Point joining in 1872, was followed by two other Long Island companies. Leading plants in the Philadelphia and Pittsburgh districts also were acquired. The Standard now dominated the main oil manufacturing sections of the country but was not yet a monopoly.

<!-- page_id: 0083-right | printed: 129 | viewer_token: 128 | viewer_sequence: 168/340 -->
## Page 129

At a meeting in Cleveland in 1875, the company raised its capital structure to $3,500,000. The board of directors was enlarged to include officials of the newly acquired refineries. Among them was Charles Pratt, an able New York oilman and a dedicated Baptist whom Rockefeller admired. With him came H. H. Rogers, who was to have a prominent part in the Standard’s subsequent chronicle, but who did not gain Rockefeller’s complete confidence. Clevelanders were still in the majority with former independent local refiners like Huntington, Stanley and Arter elevated to the board. New stockholders included city leaders like Eells, Handy, and H. B. Payne. As the Standard’s power extended, despair and animosity seized bypassed independent refiners as well as producers. There was mistrust not alone of the Standard, but even more of the railroads. Investigations were started in Ohio and Pennsylvania. In Ohio the proceedings dwindled to nothing. In Pennsylvania, on the other hand, indictments were lodged against Rockefeller and certain other Standard officials. While these charges were never carried out, unfavorable attention had been turned on the Standard, damaging it greatly. A stiffer enquiry got under way in New York State, where the legislature appointed a committee headed by an energetic young man, one Alonzo B, Hepburn, who dug ferociously into the matter. Since discriminatory practices on the part of railroads implied cooperation with a second party, the New York committee shifted to big shippers, the millers, meat packers, salt makers—and the Standard Oil Company. After two years of hearings, a long report was issued in January 1880 that selected the Standard for special indictment as a monopoly, and the railroads the for connivance that made this possible. Chastisement of the railroads was pointless, for by this time pipelines had largely taken their place in transportation of oil. While other industries were found to have risen to monopoly proportions, the committee turned its biggest guns against the Standard. Its officials declared the findings unfair to the company, a conclusion generally substantiated as viewed through the perspective of passing years. Contemporary opinion, however, accepted

## Page 130

the judgment, condemned the Standard and even more bitterly denounced its symbol, John D. Rockefeller. With these proceedings still fresh in the public’s mind, Rockefeller’s reputation was injured still further by two episodes that took place in his home city. One concerned a firm made up of William Scofield, Daniel Shurmer and John Teagle. The other was precipitated by Mrs. Fred M. Backus, whose late husband had manufactured lubricants. The Scofield, Shurmer & Teagle Company was a consolidation of two firms that had sold out to the Standard. They had made the usual pledge not to engage in refining operations for a ten-year period. Yet, in 1875, less than three years later, the new partners had built a plant and were refining oil. Rockefeller met the situation by setting up a profit-sharing arrangement whereby the Scofield firm was guaranteed a minimum yearly profit and was given the privilege of buying crude oil and shipping its product under the aegis of the Standard’s special rates. In return, the Scofield Company agreed to a limitation in output as specified by Rockefeller. All went well for three or four years. The firm enjoyed profits that even the biased Ida Tarbell declared were “enormous.” Scofield and partners began to exceed the limitation agreement and refused to curtail the quantity. Rockefeller sued and lost. The case went to higher courts. The Standard, which meant Rockefeller to the public, was found guilty of attempted restraint of trade. Scofield and partners came out the winners and were hailed as heroes. But theirs was a Pyrrhic victory. Without the Standard’s underpinning in buying power and freight costs, the once prosperous company was forced to shut down its operation. As a footnote to the bitter controversy, it is interesting to record that Teagle, who later closed his breach with Rockefeller, married the daughter of Maurice Clark, Rockefeller’s first partner. Years later the couple’s son, Walter Clark Teagle, became president of the Standard Oil Company of New Jersey. Rockéfeller’s standing with fellow Clevelanders, as well as his own feelings, were deeply hurt by the encounter with Mrs. Backus. Whatever interpretations may be put on his actions in other in-

<!-- page_id: 0084-right | printed: 131 | viewer_token: 130 | viewer_sequence: 170/340 -->
## Page 131

stances, in the Backus case it would seem that the verdict must be entirely in his favor. Mrs. Backus, who had been running her husband’s plant since his death, approached Rockefeller stating she wished to sell, estimating the property as worth $150,000. At the outset she was displeased when Rockefeller stated that such matters were not handled by him, as she wished, but by specially trained company personnel. Nevertheless, he followed the proceedings carefully because he had known Mr. Backus. When the appraiser's report came through he saw to it that $10,000 was added to the payment price for good will. Mrs. Backus received $79,000 in cash. She also retained assets she valued at $60,000 and later sold elsewhere at this price. Thus, she netted $139,000. Rockefeller urged her to keep some of the Backus stock that soon would be converted into Standard Oil stock, but she did not wish to do so. She seemed completely satisfied with the transaction, as Rockefeller thought. After some time, Mrs. Backus changed her mind and wanted some of the settlement in stock. Rockefeller explained this was legally impossible, as the papers had been filed, and the plant taken over. In short, negotiations were closed. To his “profound astonishment,” as he wrote in his Reminiscences he received “a most unkind letter” from the widow. Worse was to | follow. Testifying on behalf of the Scofield Company at a hearing of their suit, she claimed in an emotional outburst that Rockefeller had forced her to sell a plant worth $200,000 (she had raised its value by now) for $79,000, had refused to sell stock in the Standard to her, and altogether he had cheated “a poor widow who was the sole support of three fatherless children.” When Rockefeller brought out his memoirs more than twentyfive years later, he told his side of the tale for the first time. He also included statements by the brother of Mr. Backus, Mrs. Backus’s own attorney and some former employees of the Backus plant. All contended that in their judgment Mrs. Backus had been fairly, even generously, paid for the plant that they pointed out was beginning to deteriorate. Until this late date Rockefeller had made no move to vindicate himself. Many people, therefore, put full credence in

## Page 132

Mrs. Backus’s projection of him as a heartless rich man who had taken advantage of a lone woman with three little children clinging to her skirts. Clevelanders talked about the Backus case for years, always sympathetic with the poor widow. Incidentally, when the “poor widow” died, she left an estate of $350,000. Rockefeller never forgot the encounter with Mrs. Backus. In his Reminiscences, he devoted eleven pages of the short book to the “Backus Affair.” Still smarting over the memory, he coneluded: “It is probable that its wide circulation and its acceptance as true by those who know nothing of the facts has awakened more hostility against the Standard Oil Company and against me personally than any other charge which has been made.” In the midst of these disturbing happenings in Cleveland Rockefeller was continuing his effort at unification of the oil industry, winning refineries in many places including the Oil Regions. As corollaries of the annexations, the Standard was gaining fleets of tank cars and barges, segments of pipelines which were welded into a facility of great value, increased shipping assets and the like. A complex and widely spread organization was emerging under the genius Rockefeller. Yet, he and other officials by 1879-1880 were troubled over certain legal matters. The large combination of companies they were creating was functioning under the Standard Oil of Ohio. Its charter, however, restricted it to operation within the state. To meet the problem it was decided to deputize a Standard official as trustee. Flagler, secretary of the company, was chosen. He held the stocks of the merged companies for the stockholders of the Standard, but not for the Standard Oil Company itself. Shortly, his duties were taken over by three men in the headquarters office in Cleveland, namely Myron P. Keith and George F. Chester of the Standard’s legal staff, and George H. Vilas, company auditor. The Standard and its thirty-seven stockholders transferred to them all stocks in the new companies. Thus the Standard Oil of Ohio avoided ownership in these out-of-state businesses. As expansion continued, nine more trustees were added: John D. and William Rockefeller, Oliver Payne, Bostwick, Flagler, Pratt,

<!-- page_id: 0085-right | printed: 133 | viewer_token: 132 | viewer_sequence: 172/340 -->
## Page 133

Benjamin Brewster, Archbold and William G. Warden, an important Philadelphia oilman. The “Cleveland Crowd”—the two Rockefellers, Flagler, Payne and Harkness (though not a trustee)—were in control, owning nearly four-sevenths of the holdings. The new arrangement also called for setting up separate Standard Oil companies in each state, under one management and a common executive board. The headquarters, shared by all, was in New York, the logical location. Certain Cleveland personnel moved to that city. Key men like McGregor, and also Payne for a time, remained in Cleveland. All properties of the Standard and its subsidiaries were placed in the hands of the trustees whose duty was to issue certificates of interest in the holdings. They were also given the broad authority of “supervision over the affairs of the several Standard Oil companies” now being formed. “In effect, but not in law, one great company,” the Standard Oil Trust was formed in January 1882. Few outside the principals knew it for intentionally the agreement was not publicized. The word “trust” as yet had no evil connotation, but carried its true meaning, custodial care. By no means all the country’s oil firms joined the combination. Rockefeller repeatedly pointed out that the Standard always had plenty of competitors. But the stabilization he sought had come to the industry. The Standard Oil Trust has been called the first in American economic history, opening the door on a new industrial epoch. This trust in the 1880s, it has been stated, was the largest and richest manufacturing organization in the world. John D. Rockefeller ruled it, not as a lone dictator, but as the master mind in conjunction with the able men he drew around him. The efficient, well-knit Trust entered the retail trade field. Its wide-flung network of interlocking companies provided a readymade channel for reaching practically every part of the country. Retail sales had been handled in a disorganized, wasteful fashion, a condition soon remedied by the Rockefeller touch. Shortly, neat Standard Oil Company wagons, always pulled by good horses, became familiar sights on city streets and country lanes. Kerosene for light, and after a bit, for cookstoves, was trundled to the door of

every householder, or left by the tankful at corner grocery stores to be dispensed in handy little cans. Proud of this service to the public, Rockefeller asserted: “The Standard was one of the first, if not the first, industry” to promote direct-to-consumer selling. He contended the quick success and and popularity of this innovation bred antagonism among envious competitors and was responsible for much of the criticism of the Standard. He pointed out that these “selling methods, far in advance of any that then existed,” were emulated by “many lines of trade.” Constantly searching for ways to increase volume of sales, Rockefeller early sensed the opportunities for marketing oil in foreign lands. Acquisition of the well-established Long Island firm strengthened the overseas program William Rockefeller was cultivating so assiduously. John D. frequently pointed out that sales of the American oil curtailed competition from Russian and other foreign oil, and returned millions of dollars in gold to the United States. The Standard’s kerosene in increasing quantities moved from Cleveland “to be carried,” as John D. said, “by camels or on the backs of runners in the most remote portions of the earth; we adapted the trade to the needs of strange folk... . In many countries we had to teach the people—the Chinese, for example—to burn oil by making lamps for them.” Burners for these lamps were produced in Cleveland at the Buckeye Foundry on Platt Avenue. One of the partners there, Francis E. Drury, was also experimenting in the manufacture of oil burning stoves that later netted him a fortune. Out of the pioneer foundry, following a succession of consolidations through the years, the Perfection Stove Company was formed. “Perfection” oil burning stoves were put on the market at the turn of the century. The new company continued the traditional association with the Standard Oil Company, but within the United States and with stoves instead of lamp parts. Perfection’s oil stoves were distributed by the various state-based Standard Oil companies throughout their territories. No formal business agreement was signed between Standard and Perfection. It was enough that the product of

<!-- page_id: 0086-right | printed: 135 | viewer_token: 134 | viewer_sequence: 174/340 -->
## Page 135

an oil company stimulated demand for the product of a stove company. The mutually profitable relationship lasted for years. Long after the Standard’s first emissaries converted the Chinese to kerosene lamps, the Rockefeller Foundation through its China Medical Board established the Peking Union Medical College. It consisted of fifty-nine buildings, situated on a twenty-five acre compound. The culmination of long study and planning, the complex was completed in 1921 at a cost of over $8,000,000. When the Foundation’s final grant was made in 1947 it had expended almost $45,000,000 for the unit and for the stimulation of a broad medical program and medical training in China. The China Medical Board of New York is no longer connected with the Rockefeller Foundation. The Peking Union Medical College, however, is still functioning. Its name was changed to The Anti-Imperialist Hospital, and recently changed again, this time to the Capital Hospital. Rockefeller consistently plowed earnings back into the capital structure of the Standard Oil Company even though this tended to lower dividends. Samuel Andrews, one of the Standard’s “founding fathers,” grew increasingly irked at the practice. His pioneering and enthusiasm had aroused John D.’s initial interest in oil. The burly Englishman, from the beginning days in Cleveland was the Standard’s superintendent, a practical chemist, able in his job and hard working. However, he had not kept abreast of management trends. At board meetings when the Standard was launching its outof-state mergers, he was airing his discontent, finally threatening to sell his stock and pull out. One day in the spring of 1874 Rockefeller, tired of listening to his grumblings, asked him what he would take for his holdings. “A million dollars,” he answered without hesitation. Rockefeller asked for a twenty-four hour option, saying that they would discuss the matter further the next day. On the following morning Andrews came into the office in the Standard Block to find the president had a check for a million dollars ready for him. The former superintendent accepted it gladly, thinking he had the best of the bargain. Soon the same old story was re-enacted. Siar

## Page 136

The stock climbed to new heights. Disgruntled at the riches he had lost, Andrews went about Cleveland berating Rockefeller. The latter sent for his old friend and proposed turning back the stock to him at no increase in price so that he could thus benefit by its growth in value. Andrews declined the overture. The Englishman, grown to a portly likeness of John Bull himself, watched Ambrose McGregor succeed to superintendency of all Cleveland plants, and nursed his grievances. Later, during the Hepburn investigation of the Standard, Andrews demonstrated his pique by testifying against Rockefeller. In what seemed a gesture of bravado, Andrews built an enormous and costly mansion on Euclid Avenue at the corner of Sterling Street. The house had one hundred rooms and its owner's vain boast and dream was to entertain Queen Victoria in it. Her Majesty was invited, but did not come! Another who probably never stepped foot in the big house was Rockefeller, although he passed it every time he went downtown from his home. No proper staff of servants could be found for the mansion, often referred to as “Andrews Folly.” It was boarded up and eventually leveled by Andrews’s son Horace. Although later moving to New York, the younger Andrews was active for a time in Cleveland traction affairs. He was a vigorous opponent of Mayor Tom L. Johnson in the latter’s fight for three cent street car fare. Sam Andrews was not the only Standard chemist to make a name for himself locally. Another was the German-born Herman Frasch, a brilliant technician, but tempermental and hard to get along with. Rockefeller purchased his patent for processing paraffin, an item which became one of the Standard’s hundreds of profitable byproducts. The wax was so pure that it was used by British candlemakers and by Cleveland’s “Yucatan King,” William J. White, in his novel concoction, chewing gum. Pennsylvania deposits of crude were showing signs of dwindling. The internal combustion engine was in the offing and the world generally was on the eve of vastly accelerated needs for oil. At this crucial time oil was found in the vicinity of Lima, Ohio and nearby in Indiana. The quantity promised to be tremendous, but the qual-

<!-- page_id: 0087-right | printed: 137 | viewer_token: 136 | viewer_sequence: 176/340 -->
## Page 137

ity was “sour,” due to a high content of sulphur. With uncanny sense of the oil consumption that was to come, Rockefeller insisted on the Trust investing heavily in the Lima area, although fellow directors opposed him, seeing no possible use for the ill-smelling, tainted oil that coated lamp chimneys and befouled machinery. Rockefeller had been impressed with Frasch who was now in Canada. He persuaded him to come to Cleveland, where he installed him at Plant Number 5. Putting a well-equipped laboratory at his disposal, Rockefeller set the peppery little man to work figuring out a way to decontaminate the Lima-Indiana oil. John D.’s faith in-the German was justified. It was not an easy task but at length Frasch met with success. A young Cleveland chemist, William M. Burton, had assisted him. Encouraged by Rockefeller, on the alert for any improved manufacturing technique, Burton continued his experiments in the Cleveland plant. One of his notable inventions, a way of “cracking” petroleum, greatly increased gasoline yields. The sulphurous crude oil flowed on, and now it could be “cleaned.” The Trust built a huge refinery at Lima, another, one of the largest in the world, not far away at Whiting, Indiana. Enormous storage vats were erected, miles of pipelines laid to carry the finished, purified product to waiting markets. . . . By his alchemy, Rockefellers man Frasch had transformed a worthless substance into a source of usefulness and wealth. The Standard’s president and top men were called before New , York Senate and Congressional committees during numerous investigations and charges relating to trusts. By now trust was definitely a bad word. The attendant publicity highlighted the company’s power and intensified public animosity. As usual no one in the top eschelon of the Standard did anything to counteract the unfavorable picture that was coming into sharp focus. The ominous power of trusts—the Sugar Trust, the Tobacco, Rubber and other trusts—was a popular topic in the press and with the man in the street. Late in 1889 the New York Supreme Court handed down a decision condemning the Sugar Trust, as a monopoly. In the summer of 1890 the Sherman Anti-Trust Act was passed.

ee Fi

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:56.67, missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0088-left | printed: 138 | viewer_token: 138 | viewer_sequence: 178/340 -->
## Page 138

A few months before this the Attorney General of Ohio had filed a suit against the Standard Oil Company of Ohio on the grounds of monopolistic practices and violation of its charter. Two years of court battles followed. The Standard’s Cleveland legal team of Virgil P. Kline and S. C. T. Dodd was augmented by Joseph H. Choate of New York. They contended that the business of the Standard of Ohio was not conducted by the Trust, but by the Standard’s board of directors. The transfer of its stock had been an act of the stockholders, not the company. Nevertheless the Ohio Supreme Court sustained the State’s charges against the company. With the nation’s rising apprehension of trusts the Court’s conclusions were scarcely a surprise. Now that the Sherman Act was the law of the land, company officials felt an appeal to federal courts would be useless and accepted the Ohio decision. The Trust had to be dissolved. The directors turned to the Standard Oil of New Jersey that operated in a state more lenient with corporations than Ohio. With its name changed to Standard Oil (New Jersey) and its capital increased, it took over the Trust properties, reducing their number for convenience by regrouping. Flagler was made president of the company. Revenues, which continued to rise, were distributed by the same men who had acted as trustees during the ten year duration of the Trust. But now they were liquidation trustees. The Standard Oil Company of New York was likewise expanded with William Rockefeller the president. The Jersey Standard, as it frequently was alluded to, and the New York Standard had largely identical boards of directors. John D.’s name was carried as a member of both boards and as president of the Standard of Ohio. During the first few years of the 1890s, however, he was in poor health and was often absent from meetings. But he kept in close touch. Thus Standard Oil of New Jersey, of New York, and of Ohio, with those of Pennsylvania and Indiana, operated practically as a unit with interlocking directorates. Officials of the companies met regularly at 26 Broadway to conduct business, Rockefeller attending when he could. Legally the trust agreement had been nullified, but practically, it continued in operation.

<!-- page_id: 0088-right | printed: 139 | viewer_token: 138 | viewer_sequence: 178/340 -->
## Page 139

Yet Rockefeller and his associates sensed an insecurity in the arrangement. Following the customary long range planning and consultation, reorganization was affected in 1899. The charter of Standard Oil (New Jersey) was again broadened, its capital again added to, this time from $10,000,000 to $110,000,000 and it came into being as a vast holding company that took charge of the Standard aggregate. It was recognized in its day as one of the richest corporations in the world and soon became more so by increasing its original list of properties. Within five or six years after the holding company was formed, its net assets were just short of $360,000,000 and net earnings over $83,000,000. Until ordered broken up by the United States Supreme Court in 1911 the Jersey giant handled a diverse business, carrying on in its efficient, orderly way. Of the thirteen original directors (more were appointed later) seven served the entire twelve years of the holding company’s existence, namely: John D. and William Rockefeller, Flagler and Payne of the “Cleveland Crowd,” Pratt, Archbold and Charles W. Harkness, taking the place of his father Stephen Harkness, after the latter’s death. McGregor also was one of the originals, but served only a short time. John D. Rockefeller was listed as president, Archbold and William Rockefeller as vice presidents, during the entire life-span of the holding company. The fact was, however, that about 1896 John D. felt the great business he had originated had grown strong enough to permit him to retire. He turned his generalship over to Archbold. The move was not announced for fear such news would have unsteadying consequences for Standard interests. If he shifted the bulk of the load to another’s shoulders, John D. never lost contact with the Standard and remained a strong background force. In other words, the creation of the holding company brought no break in management, and the continuity of the Standard was assured,

<!-- page_id: 0089-left | printed: 140 | viewer_token: 140 | viewer_sequence: 180/340 -->
## Page 140

![0089-left image](assets/photos/0089-left-01.png)

There was nothing permissive about the rearing of the Rockefeller children. Their father and mother followed a carefully thought-out pattern by which they molded their girls and their son, this manchild on whom so much depended. Above everything, they wanted their offspring to grow up as Christians—Christians dedicated to the parental Baptist faith. Rockefeller looked on the wealth that was pouring in on him as from the Lord, while he was its appointed custodian. He regarded his children in much the same fashion and so did Mrs. Rockefeller. “Happy am I,” she wrote to a friend, “and blessed of God above so many mothers in my dear children, my precious jewels—loaned to me for a season to be handed back when the call comes.” John and Laura Rockefeller considered themselves accountable for the children entrusted to them. As it became increasingly evident that their son and his sisters would inherit a great fortune, the parents felt duty bound to provide them with a spiritual framework for that future. Despite the prominence being thrust upon them, the two clung to a retiring way of life, a preference for themselves and even more so for their children. Mrs. Rockefeller was a meticulous housekeeper with well disciplined maids, meals served on a schedule, a place for everything and everything in place. At the same time, the keynote was simplicity. The children’s clothing, for example, while of good material and well made, was plain in style, the girls’ dresses usually of cotton and without frills. This was part of the parents’ effort to keep the girls and their brother as unaware as possible of the family affluence. None of the youngsters, not even young John, ever visited the thriving Standard Oil Company domain in Kingsbury Run, an adventure that would have fascinated any boy. In line with this policy

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number, image_text_overlap:0.227 -->

<!-- page_id: 0089-right | printed: 141 | viewer_token: 140 | viewer_sequence: 180/340 -->
## Page 141

of shielding their children, their father filled out the family pledge cards for their weekly contribution to the Euclid Avenue Church:

> $10 each week $10 each week .20 each week

> Mrs. Rockefeller Mr. Rockefeller Each of our children

Behind the scenes, little suspected by the youngsters, their father was giving large sums regularly. He also could be counted on to make up a deficit and meet any budget emergency. For the little Rockefellers church was an integral part of their lives. Every Sunday morning the coachman had the carriage waiting at the door shortly after breakfast. The whole family went to Sunday School, often the first to arrive. The children felt completely at home in the big church; their father presided over the Sunday School and their mother was in charge of a department. Before long the girls and young John were teaching, too. From the time they were very small the children stayed for the adult church service to listen to a preacher who was so often a visitor at their home that he seemed like one of the family. Friday nights were given without fail to prayer meeting. Mrs. Rockefeller encouraged the children to participate like the grownups. They might deliver a short prayer, or perhaps testify to some personal religious experience. Church “socials” were the fun times for John and Laura Rockefeller, as well as for their youngsters. Highlight of the year, the church picnic, often took place at the Euclid Avenue home or at Forest Hill. The church figuratively flowed into the home. Mrs. Rockefeller’s interest centered in home, church, and children. With Rockefeller’s business often taking him out of town, the children’s upbringing, particularly their religious training, devolved largely on their mother. She had left the strict Congregational denomination of her youth for the equally strict tenets of her husband’s Baptist Church. Everyone in describing Mrs. Rockefeller has spoken of her piety as wife and mother. The New Testament was the stanchion to which she anchored her daily life. Like her husband, she accepted the Bible literally as the inspired Word of God. If she instituted a more ae

EEE Fp

<!-- issues: missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0090-left | printed: 142 | viewer_token: 142 | viewer_sequence: 182/340 -->
## Page 142

strict religious regime than Rockefeller might have adopted, he nevertheless, gave his wife his full support. Each day began with devotions at which the father presided. Punctuality being a watchword with the Rockefellers, late comers were fined one cent an infraction. Before he was nine John, Jr., was exposed to the Rockefeller fetish about keeping accounts and was assigned to recording these debts and payments. Each person, parents and children alike, read a verse from the Bible, or repeated a selection “learned by heart.” Rockefeller expounded on obscure passages and prayed for guidance through the day. Worship ended, breakfast was served. No matter where the family might be, nothing was permitted to interfere with morning worship. Rockefeller had begun to take time out from business to travel with his family, something he enjoyed very much. Midsummer when John, Jr. was twelve, they all boarded a train with a private car, “The Glen Eyre,” in Cleveland for a twoweek journey to California and return. This car was divided into six sections with a number of rooms, such as an observation room, bedrooms, a kitchen and a pantry. In the party were Aunt Lute, Dr. Hamilton F. Biggar, the family physician, Mrs. Biggar, a maid, and the Rev. Edward Judson, a Baptist minister, and son of a noted Baptist missionary. On this trip stops were made for sightseeing at Yellowstone National Park and several cities. An hour was set aside each day before breakfast for the Bible study routine, the preacher in charge. He took for his subject for the trip the Beatitudes with one discussed each day. After tea they would get together for an enjoyable interlude singing gospel hymns. Young John entered it all in his diary. In the family’s schedule at home, Sunday was a special time, What Mrs. Rockefeller called “Home Day” began Sunday after church services. It was her favorite part of the week. It was observed as a rest interlude. No unnecessary work was carried on. A cold repast was eaten at noon to save the cook unnecessary effort. Games and play were not permitted, and no school lessons studied. Even letter writing was frowned upon. Only “suitable” books were read, such as the Bible, gospel stories and Sunday School publications. Novels and similar secular literature were forbidden.

<!-- page_id: 0090-right | printed: 143 | viewer_token: 142 | viewer_sequence: 182/340 -->
## Page 143

> “On Sabbath evenings after tea,” in Mrs. Rockefeller’s words, young John and his sisters gathered around their mother for what she called her “Home Talks.” Each child was asked to concentrate on a “besetting sin.” After discussing it and praying over it, each resolved to conquer the weakness before next Sunday's meeting. The children were constantly urged to weigh their actions by asking themselves “Am I right? Am I doing my duty?” Mrs. Rockefeller readied herself in advance for these sessions, outlining in a little notebook points she wished to make each week. The Home Talks were used also as preparation for the baptism of Alta, Edith and John, Jr. It was a joint ritual taking place at the Euclid Avenue Church. Presumably Bessie already had been through the rite. Mrs. Rockefeller, in a letter of December 5, 1883, described the baptistry as surrounded by plants and flowers with “a dove suspended over it.” Rockefeller, participating in the service, perhaps thought back to his own very different baptism thirty years before at the little Erie Street Church, with Mrs. Woodin and Martha looking on, and no flowers, or dove to grace the baptismal tank. Laura Rockefeller’s parents, the Harvey Spelmans, were zealous supporters of temperance reform, their daughter and her husband no less so. Mrs. Rockefeller liked to recall that 1874 was the year when her beloved son was born, and also the year when Cleveland women, inspired by the “Woman's Crusade” in Hillsboro, Ohio, organized a temperance league, forerunner of the Women’s Christian Temperance Union. After Mr. Spelman’s death in 1881, Grandma Spelman came to live with the Rockefellers. Family talk at this time turned to the prohibition movement, so dear to Grandma’s heart. As culmination of long effort, Ohio “Drys” in 1883 were working for a proposed amendment to the state constitution to ban manufacture and sale of liquor. Rockefeller served on the advisory committee although he usually avoided involvement in public enterprises. The legislation failed to pass, making the Rockefeller clan even more vigorous temperance advocates. If there is any truth in one of the many “Rockefeller stories” that circulated in the city about its famous family, they unknowingly

## Page 144

once may have enjoyed Demon Rum. According to the tale the Rockefeller pastry cook, when making holiday mince pies, surreptitiously added rum to those intended for the help. By mistake one of these pies was served to the family. Afterwards, Mrs. Rockefeller is supposed to have hurried to the kitchen to exclaim that that was the best mince pie Cook had ever made. Deeply devoted to John, Jr., “my only grandson,” Grandma Spelman longed for the day when he would be enrolled in the Cause and could recite for her “The Price of a Drink.” Eliza Rockefeller, too, spent a good deal of time now with John and Laura. Doctor Rockefeller’s absences were stretching out longer so that he returned to the Cheshire Street house only occasionally. Grandma Rockefeller, while she talked less about it, was just as dedicated a prohibitionist, the little boy found. And so was Aunt Lute. Before he was ten, John, Jr. took the step. He signed a pledge to “abstain from tobacco, profanity and drinking intoxicating beverages.” He did not understand much of what he promised not to do, but his parents, his two grandmas and Aunt Lute were very happy over it. Soon he could repeat the lines Grandma Spelman had been waiting for:

> Five cents a glass, does anyone think That that is really the price of a drink? The price of a drink, let him decide Who has lost his courage and his pride And who lies a drowning heap of clay Not far removed from a beast today.

The matriarchal atmosphere that enveloped the young Rockefellers was offset by the dominant influence of their father. Rockefeller had strong views about the father-role in a family, as he wrote in his Reminiscences: “I wish to urge . . . the importance of a father keeping in close touch with his children, taking into consideration the girls as well as the boys who in this way learn by seeing and doing and have their part in the family responsibility.” His relationship with his children was by no means as solemn as this would lead one to believe. According to his son he “was a

<!-- page_id: 0091-right | printed: 145 | viewer_token: 144 | viewer_sequence: 184/340 -->
## Page 145

genius with children.” John, Jr. thought of his father as “a beloved companion.” He was a friend to him and his sisters—their playfellow. Rockefeller’s business associates saw him as reserved and withdrawn, always courteous, but never wasting a moment in small talk or badinage. They would have been astounded could they have observed some of his antics at home. They might have come upon him playing blindman’s buff with a gusto that roused squeals of delight in his youngsters as they struggled to out smart him. Or, maybe the dignified president of the Standard Oil Company, forgetting for the moment those perfectly pressed trousers, would be down on his knees with a child on his back for a ride around the room. Few, if any of the rising tycoons of the period developed anything like such close ties with their children. Indeed, it is said the offspring of the elder Morgan, for example, ran in terror as he appeared on the threshold. John and Laura Rockefeller looked on the theater, dancing and card playing as sinful. They loved music, but stayed away from concerts because these smacked of the theatrical in their eyes. However, the young Rockefellers were not deprived of music. At an early age each child was given an instrument, a fine one in every case. Eminent Cleveland musicians were engaged as instructors. There was a rich field to draw from, for Cleveland was enjoying a flowering of musical talent. Music as a business was so flourishing that Silas Brainard and Son, music publishers and dealers in imported instruments, occupied the entire four-story building at Euclid Avenue and Sheriff Street opposite the Standard Oil Company offices. Music had come into Cleveland with the influx of Germans and other settlers from European cultural centers. There were two or three music schools, as well as choral societies and orchestral ensembles. Best known of the performing groups was the Cecilian Quartet, later renamed the Philharmonic String Quartet, the “Pride of Cleveland.” Its members—the musical Who’s Who of the city— were Sol Marcosson, first violin; James D. Johnston, second violin; Charles Rychlik, viola and Charles Heydler, ‘cello. Bessie played the viola and, it is said, was taught by Rychlik,

— EE Fp

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:63.62 -->

<!-- page_id: 0092-left | printed: 146 | viewer_token: 146 | viewer_sequence: 186/340 -->
## Page 146

the master of that instrument. Alta chose the piano and found her hearing impairment no problem. James H. Rogers, distinguished pianist, organist and composer, was her teacher. At the time he was organist and choir director at the “Rockefeller Church,” a post he held until taking up similar duties on Sundays at the Unitarian Church, Euclid Avenue and Genesee (East 82nd) Street, and on Saturdays, playing at the Jewish Temple across the Avenue. He also had a long tenure as music critic of the Plain Dealer. The ‘cello, taught by the gifted Heydler, was Edith’s instrument. Young John, violinist, began with the violin when he was seven and from the first showed considerable ability. It is believed that Sol Marcosson was one of his teachers. Before long there was an accomplished Rockefeller quartet. Concerts were frequently staged at home with the players’ parents an appreciative and musically knowledgeable audience. The four also played frequently at church gatherings where their appearance was always warmly welcomed. School lessons were handled by private tutors. Educated at home, the children were safe from undesirable contacts and could grow up in an environment of their parents’ choosing. No television or radio, and as yet no telephone, intruded with the corrupting voice of the world. John, Jr., like a prince of the realm, was singled out for special attention. “What he did, where he went and with whom he went,” was the constant concern of his parents. It apparently never occurred to any of the children to question the parental dictum. Mrs. Rockefeller ruled firmly but gently. Her son called her “a saint.” Reminiscing years later, he declared that he could not recall hearing his mother raise her voice in displeasure or impatience. As for John D., Sr., his children waited eagerly for his homecoming whether from out-of-town trips or from a day in the Cleveland office. The youngsters, it would seem, could not get enough of him. One of them said: “He never told us what to do or what not to be.” The austere and restricted upbringing of the young Rockefellers was leavened by long and joyous stays at Forest Hill. The exclusive expanse of woods and lakes wove its spell over all the Rockefellers who looked upon it as their real home.
