## Page 147

### 23. Forest Hill

For the Rockefeller children, Forest Hill was a wonderland, a vast green province providing happy freedom to offset their otherwise circumscribed routine. When the family took possession of the big rambling house in 1878 after the abortive experiment with “paying uests,” Bessie was twelve, her sisters seven and six, and little brother four. Thus, they did much of their growing up in this land that lay as nature had made it. Forest Hill was as much a boon to their father. It supplied him with health-giving relaxation and recreation through the most tense and high pressure years of his life. Here was reawakened his boyhood love of the out-of-doors. Brought to the surface was a latent but very rea] talent for landscaping. With a zest he went about the refinement of the grounds to which he added bit by bit. In all, he acquired seven hundred acres that stretched like a continuous park beyond the East Cleveland line and into Cleveland Heights—an enclave apart from the world and yet within driving distance of the Standard offices in downtown Cleveland. Captivated by the many magnificent forest clumps on the rolling expanse, Rockefeller plunged into the study of tree management, a subject that held his interest the rest of his life. He mapped out eighteen miles of excellent roadways. Paths and trails were cut through the terrain so that the furthermost parts of the estate could be reached on foot, by bicycle or on horseback. Forest Hill encompassed a fully functioning farm with houses for workers and their families, barns for stock and carriages as well as stables for Rockefeller horses, some of the finest thoroughbreds in the country. He raced them on a well-planned track, and when he took up golf, a nine-hole course was laid out by experts. The peaceful, secluded precincts also made simple pleasures possible. He arose at six. On a fine summer morning he liked to walk barefoot over the dewy, well-groomed lawns.

— eee Fp

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:72.63 -->

<!-- page_id: 0097-left | printed: 148 | viewer_token: 148 | viewer_sequence: 196/340 -->
## Page 148

No money or thought was spared in fashioning Forest Hill fon the enjoyment of his children. They were an isolated little group with few playmates except an occasional visiting cousin. Nevertheless, there was plenty to occupy them, hillocks and ravines to explore and the always lively doings at the farm. Streams were dammed to make two lakes, one big enough for swimming and boating. Following the example of his own father, the vigorous Doctor Rockefeller, John D. taught his brood to swim and handle a boat. His own swimming outfit brought many a laugh from his children, for in the water he wore a straw hat to protect his head from the sun. During winter sojourns, there was ice skating in which John D. excelled and saw to it that his youngsters did also. Special sections of the estate were set aside as play spots and were smoothed for small-wheeled vehicles. Rockefeller wanted to buy a velocipede for each of the three youngest children. Their mother, however, vetoed the idea. One would do, she said. In that way they would learn the lesson of sharing. By the time John was eight, his father was seeking a pony for him, one that was “lazy and safe.” The children were imbued at an early age with their father’s enthusiasm for horses. He gave them lessons on carefully selected animals, and soon all four were accomplished in the saddle and with the reins. Bicycles were greatly favored by Rockefeller, and he kept a supply on hand for family and visitors. He was known to lead unsuspecting guests over winding paths on long and arduous cycle expeditions. With a white handkerchief tied to the back of his bicycle as a guide flag, he gleefully tried to loose his panting followers in the maze of the beech woods. Rockefeller’s far flung operations took him more and more frequently to New York. He missed his wife and children on these trips, and decided to take them with him. At first they stayed at a hotel. But they disliked the lack of privacy. In 1884 Rockefeller bought a large brownstone house at 4 West 54th Street. Flagler, who some time before had left Cleveland for New York, lived around the corner. He, along with other Standard Oil officials, often

<!-- page_id: 0097-right | printed: 149 | viewer_token: 148 | viewer_sequence: 196/340 -->
## Page 149

dropped in. Baptist ministers and Baptist missionaries from far corners of the globe, as well as strangers on begging errands for all sorts of causes, also found their way to the house. When in New York, the Rockefellers worshipped at the Fifth Avenue Baptist Church. John D. was in demand for talks before Bible study groups. His wife taught a Sunday School class of young men. Among her scholars was Charles Evans Hughes, who became a close friend of the family. One of his visits to Forest Hill was in 1906 shortly before his election as governor of New York State. Another member of that class was Harry D. Sims, destined to be Rockefeller’s private secretary and a prominent citizen of Cleveland. Despite their active participation in the New York church, John and Laura Rockefeller never felt as much at home there as in their Cleveland church. Under the pressure of business demands in the East, Rockefeller made New York his legal residence. This step did not alter the fact that he and his wife thought of themselves as Clevelanders, and Clevelanders so regarded them. They returned early every spring to live at Forest Hill until late fall. They arrived at the Doan (East 105th) Street station of the New York Central with their children and a sizeable retinue of retainers to be greeted by representatives of the local press and a flotilla of carriages to take the travelers to The Hill. The Euclid Avenue house functioned as little more than a way stop, but always was well cared for. It also proved to be a convenience for the family on Sunday trips to and from services at the Euclid Avenue Church. After morning worship, parents and children gathered there for their midday meal. They brought their food with them. In deference to the Sabbath as a day of rest, it had been prepared and carefully packed the night before in the Forest Hill kitchen. Rockefeller horses could be skittish. Mrs. Rockefeller often arranged to have a second groom accompany them to hold the horses’ heads as she alighted from the carriage at the church. En route she required the coachman to keep the span at a steady pace with no change of speed and no stopping. It was not an easy feat to

EEE Fp

<!-- issues: low_ocr_confidence:66.6, missing_printed_page_number -->

<!-- page_id: 0098-left | printed: 150 | viewer_token: 150 | viewer_sequence: 198/340 -->
## Page 150

achieve, particularly after a trolley line was put into operation along Willson Street which the party had to cross. To avoid being held up here the coachman had to guage his progress against the time schedule of street cars. In the afternoon, following a quiet few hours at No. 997, with everyone—horses, too—well rested, they headed back “to the country.” Rockefeller’s weekday “commuting” to the Standard Block was very different. He set out in the morning behind a quick-stepping pair, took the reins himself and drove down Euclid Avenue as fast as the law allowed. In the evening he managed the team the same way, never missing the chance for a bit of a race when he encountered other drivers of fast horses. Few have had closer association with the Forest Hill family than Dorothy Rudd Sims of Cleveland, widow of Rockefeller’s secretary, Harry Sims. Her aunt, Mrs. William C. Rudd, was Mary Ann Rockefeller, John D.’s sister, while her mother, Mrs. George A. Rudd, the former Ada Piper, had tutored the Rockefeller children at Forest Hill. George Rudd and his brother William were friends of Rockefeller since the days when they worshipped together at the Erie Street Baptist Church. The Rudd brothers founded the Chandler & Rudd Company, with William as president. From early childhood the domain on the Hill was familiar territory to Dorothy Rudd. She often accompanied Rockefeller on his afternoon drives, and with her parents had dinner numerous times at the Homestead. The fare was plain, she recalled, meals formal. The family dressed for dinner, which lasted exactly one hour. Rockefeller ate sparingly, habitually waiting until any hot food had cooled. Leg of lamb was served frequently, as it was his favorite meat. However, his meal sometimes consisted mainly of bread and milk. John D., Jr. affirmed more than once that his father chose this and similar simple dishes out of preference, and not, as often stated in the press, because of stomach trouble. Following dessert, usually something wholesome like rice pud: ding, the two butlers cleared the table, removed the cloth and brought on “Numerica.” It was a game then much in vogue, consist: ing of small colored cardboard squares each bearing a numeral

<!-- page_id: 0098-right | printed: 151 | viewer_token: 150 | viewer_sequence: 198/340 -->
## Page 151

Rockefeller thoroughly enjoyed the contest. Two rounds were played. Conclusion of the second round signalled departure time for guests. Woe to the unwary one who tarried, for the Rockefellers retired early. A carriage waited at the door. The discerning could hear the horses’ impatient stomping, Mrs. Sims added. Visitors were driven down the hill to the Forest Hill gate. They could start homeward via a streetcar, or perhaps in their own conveyance that might have waited for them beside the lodge at the entrance. As a child Mrs. Sims was in awe of Mrs. Rockefeller. However, she came to realize that the mistress of Forest Hill was a kindly, sweet-person, her strict ways merely expressions of a perfectionist. The household staff was devoted to her and stayed with her for

years. Mrs. Sims remembers her Aunt Mary Ann as quiet and reticent, clad in nondescript black, often with a black shawl over her shoulders. She was seldom at Forest Hill, despite the fact she and her husband lived nearby on Euclid Avenue. For a period each year she took her children to Saranac Lake for their health. The rest of the time she stayed in her big house where she kept no help and had little company. Like her brother, John D., she felt it her duty to share her wealth and is said to have given generous assistance for years to a considerable number of deserving and needy individuals. However, she never permitted anything to be known about her benefactions. Mary Ann’s husband, congenial with the Rockefellers, was often on The Hill, welcomed as dinner guest or golfing partner. One day when Dorothy, a teen-ager enrolled at Laurel School, a private school for girls in Cleveland, was watching a golf game, Rockefeller introduced her to a young man who had arrived that morning from New York. The newcomer was Harry D. Sims. In 1915, she and Sims were married. William Rudd died within the year and Sims resigned his secretarial post with Rockefeller to become president of the Rudds’ Company. Sims held the position until his death forty years later. The Rockefeller-Sims friendship continued as long as Rockefeller lived. After he left Cleveland, Mr.

and Mrs. Sims with their three sons were visitors more than onc at the Rockefeller estate at Pocantico Hills. Besides lessons ther was work to be done and money earned, saved and given away Each child was assigned a specific task such as laying out a flowe garden or vegetable plot that must be kept free of weeds; the pay one cent a weed. Raking leaves, chopping kindling, and gather ing the chips, breaking stone—these brought a few cents an hour It had to be an honest hour with no minutes wasted. Their weekl contribution to Sunday School and church came from these wage: It was their duty, they were told, to give as well as to get. John D., Jr. might be the heir apparent to a kingdom. Nonethe less, his parents were determined that he would not be indulge but would be trained in thrifty ways. Guided by his father, he be gan at an early age to keep records of whatever money came hi way. In one account book he noted that he had been paid ten cent for sharpening pencils; two cents for killing flies; ten cents for put ting up a thermometer and a dollar for repairing a vase. Out of sev eral months of such income he gave $8.76 to Sunday School anc church and spent fifteen cents for candy. The little boy tagged after his father all over Forest Hill. H would stand beside him watching intently as a big tree was set it place or a row of saplings planted. It was the beginning of a life time association that must rank as one of history’s outstandin; father-son relationships, untroubled by any generation gap. As the boy grew older he assumed responsibility for the estat payroll and acted as overseer for work in progress. He tried som« landscaping and road building of his own. This delighted his father who encouraged him to make any changes he wished in the ground layout. When young John was sixteen, he spent a year at Forest Hil because of ill health. In February and March, he helped in the an nual “sugaring off.” A farmer skilled in maple syrup making wa engaged, buckets hung on sugar maples and a sugar camp set up it

<!-- page_id: 0099-right | printed: 153 | viewer_token: 152 | viewer_sequence: 200/340 -->
## Page 153

the maple grove. Working along with the man, the lad charted the number of trees tapped, the yield in sap and the quantity of sugar and syrup produced. He recorded the data in a neatly kept notebook. Mr. and Mrs. Rockefeller came from New York for brief visits. At such times, father and son delighted in skating on the lake, John Jr. often pushing his mother over the ice in a chair. In the father’s Reminiscences, pages are taken up with descriptions of his landscaping experiences at Forest Hill, especially “the pleasure and satisfaction of saving and moving trees.” He kept detailed records of methods and results, the accumulated practical data on various species and their characteristics as to which could be moved safely, which could not. Uprooting and restablishing a tree was not entered into casually, but only after minute advance planning, and for a purpose, most often to open up a view. Applying the same landscape techniques developed at Forest Hill, John D., Sr., transferred to his three-thousand acre estate, Pocantico Hills, in Tarrytown, New York, and to his more modest place at Lakewood, New Jersey. Forest Hill’s influence became even more far-reaching in the case of John D., Jr. One of his absorbing concerns was preservation of scenic and historic areas in various parts of the country. Through his leadership Rockefeller wealth was diverted to such objectives as The Great Smoky National Park. He arranged to have $5,000,000 from the Laura Spelman Rockefeller Memorial that honored his mother added to a like sum raised by public and private subscription. The total $10,000,000 made the park possible. He thought of the Rockefeller portion as a “memorial to the beautiful spirit of my mother.” Other natural preserves saved for the American people by the millions and vision of John D., Jr. include: Palisades Park along the Hudson; Acadia, the first national park east of the Mississippi; the Shenandoah district, and the country of the Grand Tetons. Dramatically demonstrating his passion for trees, he opposed the private exploitation that jeopardized California’s redwoods, the earth’s oldest and tallest living things.

Such accomplishments are described in a handsome book, Th Heritage of Every American, the Conservation Activities of John D Rockefeller, Jr., by Nancy Newhall published in 1957. The autho: emphasized that his adult preoccupation with preservation wa: inspired by his boyhood days at Forest Hill. “Under the tutelage of his father,” she stated, “he first developed his lifelong interest ir landscaping, and also in trees and parkland drives.” The practical knowledge he gained at Forest Hill, it is claimed, characterized all his subsequent conservation undertakings.

![0100-left image](assets/photos/0100-left-01.png)

Why did Rockefeller bestow all those millions—about $35,000,000 —on the University of Chicago, instead of Western Reserve University in Cleveland where his fortune was generated? What of the stories bandied about to this day that he offered a sizeable sum of money to Western Reserve, but it was refused by a prominent Clevelander who for many years headed the university’s board of trustees? Anyone delving into Cleveland lore about Rockefeller soon discovers that such questions and innuendos still circulate by word of mouth and have found their way into public print. e 8 © @

With a seemingly wistful backward look at what might have been, Rockefeller stated in his Reminiscences “the plan had been to send me to college.” As we have seen, his mother was the parent, it is assumed, who had dreamed of his going to college, maybe to Western Reserve College in Hudson. However, “the plan” did not materialize. As he grew older John D. gave increasing thought to education, a concern that may have been kindled by regret over the opporanal

<!-- page_id: 0100-right | printed: 155 | viewer_token: 154 | viewer_sequence: 202/340 -->
## Page 155

tunity denied him in youth. In his memoirs he stressed what he saw as “the great claims of higher education,” convinced that “we cannot overemphasize the importance of this matter.” It seems certain, therefore, that he would have followed with keen attention the agitation about moving the then impoverished Western Reserve College from Hudson to Cleveland, a subject that was debated in the press and the community generally for many years from the late 1870s on. First expressed publicly in the Cleveland Herald in December 1877, the idea was welcomed by Clevelanders. Ever since the closing of Cleveland University in pre-Civil War days, there had been hope of setting up another college in the city. Understandably, vehement opposition to the removal developed among Hudson residents, alumni, and certain members of the academic community. With prayerful fervor their forebears, led by the generosity of the village founder, David Hudson, had established this “Yale of the West” in 1826, and villagers did not want to lose it. But the fifty-year old citadel of learning was in trouble. Enrollment was falling off. There was difficulty meeting operating costs and endowments were dwindling. The scheme of uprooting the college seems to have been inspired by a Clevelander, the Rev. Hiram C. Haydn, long a college trustee, and pastor of Cleveland’s fashionable First (Old Stone) Presbyterian Church, which he always referred to simply as “The First Church.” The college president, Carroll Cutler, discouraged by the problems facing him, also favored relocation. Both men, however, were aware of the strong opposition they would encounter and the many other difficulties in the way of such a drastic step. Never lacking in self-confidence, Dr. Haydn, practically singlehanded, undertook to direct the delicate negotiations for the move as he revealed in his book, From Hudson to Cleveland. After two tedious years, the discussions came to a head abruptly upon announcement in January 1880 of a bequest by Leonard Case, Jr., recently deceased. He had left property valued in excess of $1,000,000 to found a polytechnic institute in Cleveland that would be known as Case School of Applied Science.

Further, since boyhood he had made allocations in increasing amounts to all manner of local causes, in cluding numerous cultural organizations. One might expect, there fore, that he would have been appealed to at once for a contribu. tion toward underwriting a new educational setup. As far as can be ascertained from records of the period in pres: ent-day Case Western Reserve University archives, no attempt was made to gain his backing. Because of the quiet way in which he handled his business and his unostentatious private life, the size of his fortune was not suspected. Nevertheless, he was known as a very wealthy man. But Dr. Haydn had no need of Rockefeller’s help. As he admitted later, he already had a sponsor and had had one from the start. On the other hand, Case’s incorporators, headed by highly regarded Judge Rufus P. Ranney, lost no time in getting in touch with Rockefeller. Levi Kerr, secretary of the group and Case’s cousin, wrote Rockefeller before the incorporation was completed, asking him to serve as a trustee of the school. Rockefeller replied that he considered the invitation “a high honor and a compliment.” But it was his “duty in view of the great responsibilities I am now entrusted with, to decline.” Actually, he skirted appointment to boards or committees with a few exceptions such as the office of vice president of the Western Reserve Historical Society, as a trustee of Vassar College, and briefly, likewise, at Denison University in Ohio. He even refused membership later on the board of the University of Chicago, visiting there only twice in the first ten years of its existence. Case School's first president, Cady Staley, apparently made no effort to nurture the contact with Rockefeller, although Forest Hill,

where the oilman lived most of the year, was only a few miles from the campus. Bulwarked by Leonard Case’s million dollars, Staley probably saw no necessity to court another rich man. Stone would supply the money, under certain terms: First, the name of the college be changed to Adelbert College of Western Reserve University, honoring the memory of his gifted, only son who had drowned while a student at Yale; next, a site to be approved by him be provided by Cleveland citizens in close proximity to Case School, and lastly, the present board of trustees be reduced to eight members, with men of Stone’s choosing. ## Page 159

proved to be the first trickle of the millions he poured into the Midwest city. It is not enough to say that the pull of the Baptist faith opened his purse strings to Chicago. Granted his unwavering loyalty to the, Baptist doctrine influenced much of his giving. Yet many of his millions, particularly as his wealth increased, went to causes outside this denomination, including those of Roman Catholics, whose efficient management of his gifts he praised in his Reminiscences. In Cleveland trying delays held up Adelbert College’s dedication. Although the two campus buildings, Main Building and a dormitory, were not finished, the exercises went ahead in October 1882. Earlier that year the Standard Oil Trust had been organized. Though he was probably at Forest Hill only a few miles away, the Standard Oil head was not in the thoughts of those gathered on the new campus. Stone was the prominent personage and heard himself extolled for his generous gift to education. At the time, however, he was a harassed and tragic figure. A railroad bridge which he had built near Ashtabula, Ohio had given way in 1876. A heavily loaded passenger train was plunged into the ravine below with great loss of life. In an investigation it was claimed that Stone had insisted on use of iron instead of wooden timbers in the truss, although warned by engineers that such a bridge would not be safe. Stone’s health broke under the strain of accusations and charges, some of them said to be unfair. Within a year after opening of the College, he committed suicide. Before long it was apparent that the endowment from Stone which had seemed so munificent, was inadequate. “The ungracious task of cutting salaries (thereby losing faculty and students) was inevitable,” in Dr. Haydn’s words. “For,” he explained, “as yet the College had not made friends in the new location.” During the emergencies of these critical years no appeal was made to Rockefeller, who was being besieged by so many supplications. He was not yet committed to the University in Chicago, and anyway, had he been so inclined, he could have found ample resources for large scale giving to both Chicago’s and Cleveland’s learning centers. Rockefeller detractors—and they were numerous—contended he

## Page 160

was shunned by Dr. Haydn and cohorts because of his bad image. It is true that in his home city in the 1880s Rockefeller was hated by those like the refiners who claimed he had ruthlessly forced them out of business. In addition, his reputation was damaged by episodes like that of the widow Backus. Also, Rockefeller’s standing was further hurt when Henry Demarest Lloyd published his article, “The Story of a Great Monopoly” in the Atlantic Monthly for March 1881. In a real sense it was the opening salvo of the muckrakers’ campaign against the Standard Oil Company. Lloyd attacked railroad rates that encouraged monopolies, he twisted statistics to suit himself and was careless of his facts. He portrayed Rockefeller and the Standard as bribing railroad heads, manipulating Congress, the courts, and legislatures without citing any evidence. As in the Backus case when it was current, Rockefeller refused to answer Lloyd’s unwarranted charges. Thus in Cleveland, as elsewhere, the impression took shape that, if unrefuted, the accusations must be true. Yet what of Stone, widely disliked, but whose money was gratefully accepted? Even the judicious Nevins wrote of him: “Everyone hated his arbitrary ways, fierce temper and biting tongue.” Stone, nonetheless, had his friends. Among them was A. A. Pope, Cleveland businessman, who completed Adelbert College’s main building in Stone’s name. Stone’s daughters, Mrs. John Hay and Mrs. Samuel Mather, demonstrated their regard by erecting the Amasa Stone Memorial Chapel, one of the most beautiful buildings on the University campus. Stone’s name is perpetuated in Cleveland also by Amasa Stone House, a home for “aged gentlewomen,” established by him with a $10,000 endowment gift and in operation to this day. After Stone’s death his other son-in-law, Samuel Mather, was appointed to the board of Western Reserve University. Scion of early Ohio landowners with an illustrious New England lineage, Mather had expanded with great success the pioneer iron ore and lake shipping enterprises of his father, also named Samuel. The son organized the Pickands, Mather Company, ore merchants, and became wealthy. On the new campus he picked up the reins where

<!-- page_id: 0103-right | printed: 161 | viewer_token: 160 | viewer_sequence: 208/340 -->
## Page 161

his father-in-law had dropped them and served forty-three years as a devoted chairman of the board of trustees. The aristocratic Mather moved in Cleveland’s most exclusive society. The Rockefellers were not included in such circles, nor did they care to be. Mather and Rockefeller, according to the latter’s statement, met only once and then for ten minutes at Rockefeller’s New York City home. The occasion was a business conference during which Mather agreed to take charge of building twelve orecarrying ships for Rockefeller who had invested in ore mines in the Mesabi region. During the depression years of 1893-1896, Rockefeller had expanded his holdings in these mines. To move the ore to the head of Lake Superior for shipment down the lakes, he completed a railroad begun by previous investors and erected magnificent loading docks at rail terminals. A logical next step, he decided, was to build his own ore-carrying vessels. Familiar with Mather’s extensive ore and shipping business, he picked him as the expert he wanted to construct twelve 5000-ton ore ships that would be the largest such carriers on the Great Lakes. Frederick T. Gates, then in charge of Rockefeller’s mining operations, brought Mather to the Rockefeller residence shortly before the dinner hour. As he arrived, the Clevelander announced, probably a bit pompously, that he could stay only a few minutes. Describing this meeting in his Reminiscences, Rockefeller wrote: “I told him I thought we could finish our affairs in ten minutes (one can imagine the dignified politeness with which he said it) and we did.” At first Mather demurred at the proposal, recognizing that with these ships Rockefeller would become his competitor. Rockefeller replied that someone was going to build them for him, and Mather might as well have the profit from the undertaking. “We gave him the order for about $3,000,000 worth of ships and that was the only time I saw him, But Mr. Mather is a man of high business honor and we never had occasion to regret it,” Rockefeller concluded. As demands for raw material increased among steel and iron mills, Rockefeller added to his ore ships. Operated as the Bessemer

Steamship Company, with offices in Cleveland in charge of L. M. Bowers, the fleet soon numbered twenty-eight, and was the largest aggregate of ore-carriers in the world. Rockefeller had no intention of staying in mining. In 1901 he sold his ore lands and the Bessemer Steamship company (now handling nearly sixty vessels) to the United States Steel Corporation for $88,500,000. In his quiet, shrewd bargaining with the bombastic John P. Morgan, he had upped the latter's “outside” offer by $5,000,000. What he finally received, Rockefeller contended, was a “reasonable” price. It is said his profit was $50,000,000. He had made it in less than ten years in a business he viewed as a sideline. He was named a director of the steel corporation and was its largest single stockholder. In that earlier, brief, but most productive, business conference between Mather and Rockefeller, neither would have had opportunity, nor inclination to discuss a subject with which both were deeply concerned—higher education. Mather at the time was at the helm of Western Reserve University as chairman of its board, and Rockefeller, the good angel of the new University of Chicago. In 1961 the Plain Dealer in a bitterly antagonistic article about Rockefeller, printed its version of his relations with Western Reserve University: “When he (Rockefeller) offered millions to lift Western Reserve to the top cloud of the academic heaven a trustee said coolly: “Tell Mr. Rockefeller Cleveland will take care of its own.’ The money went instead to the University of Chicago.” The theme was repeated six years later in the Reserve Tribune Magazine, an undergraduate campus paper. The story was based on an interview with a university staff member who was quoted as saying: “There is little doubt that Western Reserve could have gotten [sic] the heavy financial backing that John D. Rockefeller finally gave to the University of Chicago . . . if it really had gone after his support.” Continuing: “Reserve graduates after the turn of the century believed that Rockefeller actually tried to endow this university but was turned down because of the influence of the Mather family.” There is no evidence that Rockefeller ever proffered unsolicited

aid to Western Reserve University. Such a move would have been contrary to his habit. He was not given to throwing money at institutions or individuals. There were too many clamoring for it. Further, contrary to the assertions that Rockefeller offered the University a large gift and was “turned down’ is the fact that eight years after the Hudson college’s removal to Cleveland, Hiram Haydn was imploring him to come to the aid of the struggling institution. In the spring of 1888 the minister wrote to Rockefeller who was then in New York, asking for an opportunity to lay before him “the educational situation here.” The financial picture had not improved and, in fact, was so precarious that when President Cutler resigned, no candidate could be found willing to take his place. In the emergency Dr. Haydn filled the post, temporarily, as a “loan” from his congregation. On Rockefeller’s return to Cleveland the minister had a conference with him at Forest Hill but made no headway. However, having “discovered” Rockefeller at last, the acting president of Reserve appealed to him again and again during the next few years. In long, discursive, often emotional letters he outlined university needs at least one of which, hopefully, Rockefeller might be persuaded to underwrite: endowment and scholarship funds for Adelbert and the College for Women; establishment of a law school; money for the Medical School (then on Erie Street); a home for the Conservatory of Music and an art hall. A fireproof building for the library was an essential and anyone making that possible “would have a monument far ahead of Garfield’s.” (Construction of the imposing James A. Garfield monument in Lake View Cemetery was then in progress.) Rockefeller had not the slightest interest in erecting a monument to himself, nor was he moved to finance any of the other university projects. A problem almost overwhelming Haydn was the failure of local citizenry to back “our University,” he told Rockefeller. Clevelanders viewed Adelbert College as a memorial to the Stones’ son, as indeed it was, and looked to that family for its support. The minister argued that a gift from the oilman would stimulate others to 7020

In the Haydn-Rockefeller correspondence no mention occurs of Samuel Mather. Further, he seems never to have made any overtures to Rockefeller in the name of Western Reserve University with which he was so long and so prominently identified. But that is far from saying he (or any member of his family) would have blocked a Rockefeller gift earmarked for the school. If Mather at times appeared to outsiders as somewhat aloof, there can be no impugning his years of able leadership and many contributions to the University, not the least being his money gifts, which are difficult to estimate accurately, but are said to have added up to $4,000,000. Finally it is noteworthy that he was chairman of the board of trustees in 1903 when Dr. Charles F. Thwing, the University president, turned to Rockefeller for funds. His appeal bore fruit, and certainly Mather did nothing to interfere with the gifts that were then forthcoming. Beginning with his first $2,500, until shortly after Rockefeller’s death in 1937, Western Reserve University received $2,005,000 from Rockefeller personally, his General Education Board or the Rockefeller Foundation. Of this amount, $1,590,500 went to the School of Medicine as follows:

$1,000,000 in 1927 for construction and equipping the Pathology Laboratory which was planned by Dr. Howard T. Karsner, the director. 300,000 in 1929 undesignated. 250,000 in 1910 toward a $1,000,000 endowment fund raised under direction of Drs. E. F. Cushing, George W. Crile, John F. Sawyer and Frederick C. Waite. 25,000 in 1932 awarded to Dr. Gerald Shibley for clinical study of whooping cough. 15,000 in 1937 for the Public Health Nursing program. The $417,000 remainder (except the $2,500 land purchase pledge) represented allocations in varying amounts to university endowment funds; the School of Applied Social Science from 1932-1939, and $35,000 in 1935 for the dramatic program and alterations at Eldred Hall. The sum included $125,000 that Rockefeller gave in

Case School, like Western Reserve University, took no steps toward awakening Rockefeller’s interest in its program until years later. However, Case’s second president, Dr. Charles S. Howe looked up the oilman shortly after assuming office in 1904. The new president wanted a mining and metallurgy building, which he estimated could be constructed for $110,000, and also a physics lab- | oratory that would cost $75,000. “Would Rockefeller supply the required $185,000?” he inquired. After detail plans and cost sheets were submitted, the answer came back promptly from John D. Rockefeller, Jr., now helping with his parents’ giving-program: His father would send not only the $185,000 requested for the two buildings, but $15,000 besides “thereby covering in advance any incidental and unforeseen expenses that may arise.” He added: “This money will be paid when and as you request it.” The physics laboratory, with “Rockefeller” incised over the door, was built in 1906 and still stands. The other building, erected about the same time, was razed in 1961. Encouraged by his appeal for these two campus structures, Howe approached the Rockefellers with a more ambitious proposition. For a decade after his inauguration, the Case president carried on a vigorous correspondence with Starr J. Murphy, one of the influential administrators of the Rockefeller philanthropies and a trustee of the General Education Board. In his letters, Howe described

<!-- page_id: 0106-right | printed: 167 | viewer_token: 166 | viewer_sequence: 214/340 -->
## Page 167

his concept of Case as “a great technical university,” and asked from between $8,000,000 and $10,000,000 over a seven-year period to create it. When he realized he was making no headway with this idea, he trimmed his appeal to $350,000 for an engineering building. But Murphy was no more receptive to this than to the more ambitious proposal. As to the Mather-Rockefeller-Western Reserve University epic, the final chapter was enacted in 1922. With the growth of the University and the expansion of its graduate schools, Mather realized that Adelbert College and the College for Women (later Flora Stone Mather College) were becoming increasingly crowded. More campus space was urgently needed. Not far away lay beautiful Forest Hill, the vast park-like expanse of the Rockefellers. Although as well cared for as ever, it was entirely vacated. In 1917 the Homestead had been destroyed by fire. Mrs. Rockefeller was dead. Rockefeller had left Cleveland. Mather envisioned the estate as a wonderful site for an undergraduate campus, and for some other departments and functions of the land-locked University. The idea had occurred to other Clevelanders, including a young banker, Ralph Hayes. He had been secretary to Newton D. Baker, when the latter was Secretary of War, and was well-known. Also Hayes was a loyal Adelbert alumnus. A visit to New York was called for. Mather was not able to go at the time. Hayes undertook the assignment, his expenses paid by Mather. He could not see Rockefeller, but was referred to his son. John D., Jr., listened to what he had to say, and then replied with courteous finality that there were other plans for Forest Hill. Cleveland had to wait a few years to learn what those plans were. Utilizing Forest Hill as a college campus had been discussed before by university officials. In 1918 and again in 1919 Sidney Wilson, secretary-treasurer of Western Reserve University from 1918- 1936, broached the matter to Murphy. Wilson wanted a University of Cleveland, a subject talked about by many people, he stated, among them Mayor Baker. “In case Mr. Rockefeller does not rebuild his house at Forest Hill, do you think,” he queried, “he

Bessie Rockefeller attended Vassar College from 1886 to 1888. Her father was a trustee of the college during these years and until 1905. He had become interested in Vassar through Dr. Augustus H. Strong whom he knew well when Dr. Strong was pastor of Cleveland’s First Baptist Church. He was now head of Rochester Theological Seminary, a top flight Baptist divinity school, and chairman of the board of trustees of Vassar. With his thoughts on the place higher education might fill in the philanthropy program he was formulating, Rockefeller was stimulated by his visits to the college campus. Contacts with the president, James M. Taylor, and fellow trustees gave him insight into the academic world. In these years the new University of Chicago was \conceived. After long deliberation and much travail, plus guidance as well as money from Rockefeller, the University was born in 1892. Much of the spirited discussion in the formative years took place at Vassar and at Forest Hill. It was at Vassar where Rockefeller first met William Rainey Harper. He was destined to be the University of Chicago’s first president and as such to launch the institution into stellar orbit. Long before this the educator and the oilman had become close friends. Dr. Harper and his wife were familiar figures at

<!-- issues: image_text_overlap:0.292 -->

<!-- page_id: 0107-right | printed: 169 | viewer_token: 168 | viewer_sequence: 216/340 -->
## Page 169

Forest Hill where they were welcomed as favorite guests and beloved by all the Rockefellers. The two men were introduced in 1888 at breakfast in the big, high-ceilinged dining room of Vassar’s Main Hall. The short, stocky young professor—he was thirty-two—occupied the chair of Biblical Literature at Yale, and was a Baptist. He came to Vassar on Sundays as visiting lecturer on Bible subjects. Rockefeller seems to have been impressed at once with the engaging scholar. He records the meeting in his Reminiscences: “as I frequently spent weekends there (Vassar) I saw and talked much with the young professor . .. and caught in some degree the contagion of his enthusiasm.” Another with whom Rockefeller first became acquainted at this time was the former Minneapolis Baptist preacher, Frederick T. Gates, who also spent weekends at Vassar and later was appointed a College trustee. Gates was to be of utmost importance to Rockefeller in many capacities, his best-known role that of almoner and molder of the Rockefeller system of philanthropy. At the moment he was executive secretary of the newly formed American Baptist Education Association. Rockefeller supported it and thus knew of Gates. No account of Rockefeller in Cleveland is complete without reference to Gates. In and out of Cleveland for conferences with Rockefeller on countless subjects, Gates at one period was the most frequent visitor at the Homestead. During his Cleveland pastorate, Dr. Strong and his family looked on the Rockefeller estate as practically a second home. Their bookish son Charles, who often accompanied them, used to go off to the Forest Hill beech woods, perch on a low tree limb and read aloud for hours to the young Rockefellers. The two families had made a joint trip to Europe and were drawn together even more closely with the marriage in 1889 of Bessie and Charles. Along with “Old” University of Chicago, Baptist colleges and universities throughout the country generally were struggling and weak. Intellectuals in the sect saw the need for a great new American university that would be under Baptist aegis but not narrowly, sectarian. As the country’s leading Baptist layman, Rockefeller was \ counted on to support all sorts of denominational causes. It was

taken for granted he would underwrite the proposed University And he was giving careful consideration to the idea, studying it ir his usual deliberate way. Dr. Strong came forth with the vision of a grandiose center o| Baptist learning to be located in New York City. With his trustee duties bringing him frequently to Vassar, he had ready-made op portunities to try to win Rockefeller’s approval of his concept. He was an eloquent and persistent advocate, but, despite some good points which Rockefeller saw in the scheme, he felt it impractical. However, he was at a certain disadvantage in discussions with Strong because of the family relationship between them. From contacts through the Education Society in various parts of the country, Gates was convinced the contemplated university should be somewhere in the Middle West. He agreed with Dr. Goodspeed, of Morgan Park Seminary in Chicago: The place for the institution was in that city where it would rise phoenix-like from the ashes of the defunct University. One evening at Vassar, Rockefeller and Dr. Harper were at the President's home, the three deep in the ever-present topic of the proposed university. Rockefeller turned to Harper and asked him if he would accept the presidency if and when the university came into existence, and at the same time, he wanted to know where he thought it should be situated. The young visiting lecturer from Yale replied that he would be willing to give his life to such an institution wherever it was to be. However, he indicated that his preference for its site was Chicago. In the opinion of Rockefeller and many others, Harper as head would insure the institution’s success. So the question of location seemed to be settled. Opposing views respecting the projected university were heading up to a great extent on the Poughkeepsie campus with Dr. Strong increasingly vehement and Rockefeller in a difficult pivotal position. Nevertheless, the oilman enjoyed his sojourns in the peaceful precincts, walled as they were against the world he knew. President Taylor, who had left a Baptist pulpit for his current position, welcomed him warmly, took him home for tea and for dinner with Mrs. Taylor and the children. The campus’s fine stand of trees, old

monarchs and newly planted saplings, reminded him of Forest Hill, as did Vassar Lake where he sometimes skated with the president. However, he avoided the fancy curlicues that “Prexy” indulged in to the delight of his “young ladies.” Fellow trustees accepted the president of the Standard Oil Company as a valued associate, heedless of his ogre-image as projected by the muckrakers now snarling fiercely after him in full pack. He was among friends, and, basking in their midst, was relaxed and genial. His wry humor coming into play, he dipped freely into his store of jokes and listened with appreciation to the others’ lively anecdotes. One about Bessie then going the rounds of the campus amused him greatly. It seemed that while shopping in Poughkeepsie for furniture for her college room she saw a piece at Luckey Platt & Company that she liked but found she had not enough money with her to buy it. Did she think that her father would pay for it if the store billed him, the clerk queried hopefully. Bessie was sure he would. “What does your father do?” the man asked. “Well,” Bessie hesitated, “I don’t really know.” (Chuckles from her father as he heard this.) “What is his name?” the merchant now enquired. “Mr. Rockefeller.” “Not the Mr. Rockefeller!” (More chuckles from the Mr. Rockefeller.) The flustered salesman assured his customer that what she had selected would be dispatched at once to her Vassar dormitory.

Bessie’s father added it to his collection of stories, and took delight in retelling it. Also as a student at Vassar at the same time as Bessie, was another Clevelander, Adella Prentiss, who had been at Forest Hill and knew the family. She was an accomplished pianist. On one of John D., Jr’s visits to the College the two young people staged a pianoviolin concert in the chapel, Trustee Rockefeller applauding in the audience. Back in her home city after graduation and study in Europe,

Realizing in 1918 that the paramount need was a permanent home for the fledgling orchestra, she succeeded in interesting the Chamber of Commerce whose “president, with one or two others presented the matter of a music hall for Cleveland to Mr. Rockefeller’s advisors at 26 Broadway.” (Rockefeller by then had left Cleveland.) Response at first seemed favorable. But the Rockefeller field representative, learning of plans to build a Hippodrome in Cleveland advised the oilman that the city would not need a music hall. If she did not gain all she had hoped from her friends the Rockefellers, she recognized, as she stated in her book, Music Is My Life, that “The great gifts of our Hall (Severance Hall) and the endowments that largely support the Cleveland Orchestra today (1947) were made possible by the Standard Oil fortunes . . . inherited by the Severances, the Blossoms and the Boltons.” J Vassar College fared better than the Cleveland Orchestra at the hands of Rockefeller, Sr. Despite his own love of music, he proved | less ready to support the arts, than higher education. \ On the Poughkeepsie campus, he gave money for a dormitory that he named “Strong” for Bessie. Next to it he erected a similar building, “Davison House,” in memory of his mother Eliza Davison Rockefeller. He supplied funds for the College recitation hall that President Taylor christened Rockefeller Hall, but which was always “Rocky” to the students. There were other funds supplied by Rockefeller. However, he had not been “besought for his money, but for his counsel,” Dr. Taylor emphasized in reporting these gifts. At a Baptist convention in Boston in May 1889, the Education Society officially endorsed establishment of a college—later to be a university—in Chicago. Only after this vote did Rockefeller allow Gates to announce his gift of $600,000 as the initial sum in the million dollar endowment fund to be collected as the first step in starting the new institution. The assemblage broke into a pandemonium of cheering and prayerful outcries of gratitude to “our great patron,” ending by jubilantly singing the Doxology. The proposed learning center was to be Christian in character, under Baptist auspices forever, but administered in a spirit of the widest liberality. William Rainey Harper, it was assumed, would be its president. He was acknowledged not only the most accomplished scholar the Baptists could produce, but was equally admired in all academic circles. Rockefeller refused absolutely to permit the university to be named for him. It was to be the new University of Chicago. Although his gift grew to about $35,000,000, no building on the campus carried his name until after his death when the handsome Rockefeller Memorial Chapel was erected.

Now president-elect of a still nebulous university, Harper ar rived at Forest Hill. He was delighted with the lovely place anc felt at home immediately. He was back in his native Ohio. His birth place was a humble log cabin in New Concord, Muskingum County Local Muskingum College admitted him as an undersized boy ir short pants. When he was sixteen, he was graduated and for ¢ short time stayed on at Muskingum to teach Hebrew. At eighteen he received his Ph.D. from Yale. On his first day at the Rockefeller home, he came down to breakfast to find the whole family at the table, the young Rockefellers as well informed and interested in proceedings about the University as their parents. This was in line with Rockefeller’s effort to include all his family in his philanthropy projects. The youthful educator won them all. “Harper was a man of exquisite personal charm,” Rockefeller wrote of him. “We count it one of the rich and delightful experiences of our home life that Dr. and Mrs. Harper could occasionally spend days together with us.” The Forest Hill visitor was widely known for his erudition: he was a master of Assyrian, Hebrew, Aramaic, Syrian—all the tongues of the Bible. But he seems to have worn his wisdom lightly, never letting it get in the way of friendliness, After the endorsement at the Boston convention, there remained the seemingly endless preliminary details to be faced in the task of bringing into being the long hoped for university. Numerous sessions took place at Forest Hill. In 1890 at least three, maybe more, important conferences were staged there with Harper and Gates in attendance. The indefatigable Gates, soon invaluable, acted as catalyst, well-informed and practical. It is clear that much of the framework of the University of Chicago was hammered out at Forest Hill, with Gates one of the skilled craftsmen. From now on he was often here as he undertook more and more assignments from Rockefeller, While he may have liked Forest Hill, he apparently did not enjoy Cleveland. Once overhearing a remark that all Rockefeller cared for was making money, he is said to have retorted hotly: “In heaven’s name, what else could he do in that city!”

Gates was utterly loyal to Rockefeller, who in turn came to have complete confidence in him. By the time Rockefeller wrote his Reminiscences, Gates, as he noted, had been involved “with my personal affairs for many years.” He was chairman of the General Education Board and had “taken cares of many kinds off my shoulders.” In the early stages of the Baptists’ plans for a university, Gates was enroute to Chicago from New York one day on the same train with Rockefeller, who was getting off at Cleveland. Gates then was only slightly acquainted with this most famous of all Baptist laymen. He took the opportunity to scrutinize the great man and recorded his impressions in his manuscript autobiography, supplying a vivid picture of Rockefeller at this period. Gates was impressed with the neatness of his clothes, which were spotless and “inconspicuous though of the costliest material and workmanship.” He was struck with the absence of jewelry—“no watch guard or chain ...no rings and not even a pin in his necktie.” Gates noticed what was Rockefeller’s customary way of managing the tipping problem: he held out a handful of loose change and let the recipient take what he thought was right. Rockefeller emptied into a collection plate at Cleveland’s Willson Avenue Baptist Church one Sunday evening in June 1882. He had attended the church program at the urging of its pastor, the Rev. George O. King, who wanted him to hear a special program. Two charming New Englanders, Miss Sophia B. Packard and Miss Harriet E. Giles, were the speakers. They described most movingly a school they had opened the previous year in Atlanta, Georgia for Negro girls and women of various ages. Practically all those attending were illiterate, but eager to learn to read and write. The speakers pointed out that enrollment had increased steadily. Contributions from Rockefeller included $7,500 toward purchase of land; $40,000 for a brick building that was named Rockefeller Hall; $81,000 for heating plants; $55,000 for two buildings named for Miss Packard and Miss Giles. When he rejected the founders’ wish to name the school for him, they selected Spelman in appreciation of the anti-slavery activity of Harvey Spelman. Nevertheless, the name, if unofficially, honored his wife and two daughters as well. Spelman Seminary became Spelman College (for women) and is now affiliated with Atlanta University. Through the years Miss Packard and Miss Giles made long and restful visits at Forest Hill, often encountering other house guests. One time in 1903 when Miss Giles, by then president of Spelman, spent a fortnight there, another president, W. H. P. Faunce of Brown University and his wife also were staying at the Hill. Spelman’s president kept a diary of each day's doings and recorded the menu for every meal! Brown was the alma mater of John D., Jr. and Dr. Faunce a leading Baptist. All in all it was a compatible group the Rockefellers had gathered together. At Spelman’s fiftieth anniversary in 1931, Rockefeller sent his congratulations regretting he could not attend. He wrote: “Of all the investments we have made as a family, Spelman stands among the | best.” His family carried on. Recorded personal gifts from the senior Rockefeller totaled $316,000. His son, who was indoctrinated | in the Spelman tradition at the age of ten, gave $836,000. There were other sizeable gifts not accounted for in these figures. From 1903 on, annual contributions from $6,000 to $12,000 were made to Spelman for current operations. In 1928 the Rockefeller General Education Board gave $1,500,000 toward a $3,000,000 endowment fund then being raised. At the same time Spelman received $1,000,000 from the Laura Spelman Rockefeller Memorial Fund which was applied to the endowment. Among the many tangible evidences on the campus of Rockefeller-Spelman connections is Sisters Chapel, a memorial to Mrs. John D. Rockefeller and her sister Lucy. The Abby Aldrich Rockefeller dormitory honoring his wife was a gift of John D. Rockefeller, Jr.

## Page 179

following the precept “to get is to give” that she had so firmly taught her children. It was not too long a walk from 33 Cheshire Street to the Euclid Avenue Baptist Church. Eliza was in her pew every Sunday, rejoicing in her son’s presence nearby and in his faithful attendance on the Lord. The untimely death in 1878 of the beloved Lucy, who was Eliza’s first born, was a deep sorrow to her and to the entire family. Lucy’s husband, Pierson D. Briggs, was employed in the purchasing department of the Standard Oil Company. There was one surviving child, ten-year old Florence. The little girl was very dear to her Uncle John and Aunt Laura and spent a great deal of time with them, joining the young Rockefellers more like a sister than a cousin. Pierson Briggs remarried and with his second wife, the former Laura Price, lived on Euclid Avenue a bit east of the Rockefeller town house. Florence married William W. Benjamin, Jr. in Cleveland in 1891. The couple’s children, Lucy Avery and William, were welcomed like grandchildren at Forest Hill. After Lucy’s death Eliza began to fail. The hard experiences of her life were taking their toll. And what of her mate, the botanic doctor? His absences lengthened steadily. His name was no longer listed in the Cleveland City Directory. Occasionally he turned up. One day in 1885 he was seen driving along Euclid Avenue in a smart rig. He hitched his fine span of horses in front of the Standard Building and bounded up the stairs to see John D. He made rare and surprise visits at Forest Hill, always warmly welcomed by his grandchildren. They listened to the yarns he spun with gusto, liked the lively tunes from his violin, which he held at his waist. Once, bringing John D. Jr. a rifle, and the girls bows and arrows, he drilled them enthusiastically in marksmanship. On one recorded visit to New York, he dropped in at 26 Broadway and greatly enjoyed a tour of New York under guidance of John D.’s secretary. In robust health, well dressed and seemingly prosperous, he was as jovial as ever. “All the family loved him,” John D., Jr., said of him years later.

<!-- page_id: 0117-left | printed: 180 | viewer_token: 180 | viewer_sequence: 236/340 -->
## Page 180

His appearances grew more infrequent until the unpredictal man stayed away altogether. Eliza, it is said, received an occasion letter from him, but did not know his whereabouts. He was su posed to have provided her with an address in the Far West wh« he picked up communications from her. A stoic, and never tall tive on any subject, Eliza was silent about William Avery Rocl feller. And so were her children. After their marriage in 1872, Mary Ann and her husband mov to their new home on Euclid Avenue in East Cleveland. Eliza w too feeble to live alone. The Cheshire Street house that had so lo ministered to the family was sold to the Fleischman Yeast Co pany for commercial purposes. When East Prospect (Carneg: Street was laid out, the house was in its path and had to be to down. A billboard was erected and stood for some time at t southwest corner of 19th Street and Carnegie on what remain of the original Rockefeller lot. Eliza divided her time among her children, mostly at the hom of her sons, where she was cared for tenderly. Winters she spe in New York with William, summers partly in Wickliffe wi Frank’s family and at Forest Hill. Although relations between h oldest son and youngest son were strained for years, their moth ignored the friction and was impartially devoted to both. In March 1889 Eliza, who had begun life as a backwoods far girl, died at age seventy-six in the Fifth Avenue mansion of h son, William, Her three sons accompanied her body to Clevelai where the funeral service was held at John’s Euclid Avenue re: dence. Among the several Baptist ministers officiating were form pastors at the Euclid Avenue Baptist Church, the Rey. S$. W. Du can and the Rev. George T. Dowling. Eliza had known them we and they had come from charges in the East to honor her. The hou filled up quickly with Rockefeller relatives and friends. Willia Avery Rockefeller was not among the mourners. Everyone notic that he was missing, but no one mentioned the fact. Eliza was buried in Lake View Cemetery in a large, high-lyii lot purchased some time before by John D., who intended it as family burial plot. A few years later he marked it with a majest

<!-- page_id: 0117-right | printed: 181 | viewer_token: 180 | viewer_sequence: 236/340 -->
## Page 181

granite obelisk incised with the single word “Rockefeller.” It is the tallest shaft in the cemetery, topped in height only slightly by Garfield Monument which is close by. An unsubstantiated story circulated that John D. asked the ministers who spoke at his mother’s funeral service to indicate she was a widow. In line with this, Flynn in God’s Gold stated that the headstone placed at Eliza’s grave was inscribed “widow of William Avery Rockefeller.” The marker, however, carries only the words “Eliza Davison Rockefeller,” and “Mother.” Before Eliza’s interment there were already a number of her progeny in the Rockefeller plot. Lucy was lying beside small graves of children she and her husband had lost. Alice, infant daughter of John D. and Laura, was not far away. A second cluster of tiny headstones marked resting places of babies of Frank Rockefeller and his wife, the former Helen Scofield. But these children were not to remain in this peaceful bit of earth beside their cousins. In an upsurge of burning anger at John, Frank had the bodies disinterred in 1909 and placed in another part of the cemetery. Frank never did anything quietly. Consequently, Cleveland soon learned, and was shocked at the news, that he had moved his children so that they would not lie near his brother John, who was to be buried in this lot. From early years these two brothers’ temperaments got in the way of any real understanding between them. John was dismayed, for example, at Frank’s prank by which he dodged regulations to enlist in the Civil War, but stood by him, That sort of recklessness and irresponsibility did not wholly leave Frank as an adult. John took both of his brothers into the oil business with him, made them both vice presidents of the Standard Oil Company. Discontented, Frank left the company to return and depart again. John, nevertheless, kept him on the payroll the while. William stayed and became invaluable to John. He was rewarded for his fidelity and his ability rising to success and wealth on his own account. Frank declared William was under John’s thumb. He loved William as much as he hated John. Frank resented being catalogued as “John D. Rockefeller’s broth-

er.” The feuding between his father-in-law, William Scofield, an his brother, as already mentioned, helped influence him again: John, particularly during the hard-fought battles between th Standard Oil Company and Scofield’s outfit. But Frank was as warr hearted as he was hot headed. After a confrontation with his broth er he would go to him in contrition, ask his forgiveness and voy to end all such actions. Disregarding the brothers’ quarrels, their families remaine friendly, their children visiting back and forth between Forest Hil and the Frank Rockefeller summer place seven miles away at Wick liffe. Frank shared the Rockefeller fondness for horses and had | track at Wickliffe where he trained his racers. Frank also owned a remarkable 8,000-acre ranch, Soldier Cree! Park in southwestern Kansas. It might be more accurate to say tha John D. and William owned it, for they carried mortgages on th ranch from the time Frank bought the first acres in 1879. In a serie of complicated transactions by which Frank kept adding to hi holdings without decreasing his indebtedness, the mortgages ulti mately amounted to over $168,000. In addition, there had beer numerous other loans by his brothers for ranch operations. The ranch was known for its prize Hereford cattle and fine sheep There was a stable of pedigreed horses and a herd of buffaloes fa mous throughout the region. Despite certain eccentricities—he im ported fifty cats to take care of the rodents and the cats becam more troublesome than the rodents—Frank developed a splendic ranch. He was proud of it and spent a good deal of time on it, ofte1 entertaining Cleveland friends there. In the early 1880s Frank formed a partnership with James Cor rigan, a flamboyant, adventurous Clevelander. The two went int debt to purchase the Franklin Iron Mining Company in Wisconsin When the panic hit in 1893 they were in trouble, and went separate ly to John D. to be bailed out. Several times he loaned them larg sums in their emergencies, including for example, one sum o $140,000 and another of $80,000. John D. also advanced a total o about $265,000 on Franklin Mine paper, with Frank as signer anc Corrigan endorser of some of it.

## Page 183

Earlier in the 1880s, James Corrigan and his brother John had operated an oil refinery in Cleveland which they sold to the Standard. Jim thus acquired 2,500 certificates of Standard Oil Trust. He put up this block of stock as security for the loans from John D. —a sound business arrangement. As the nation’s economy continued to slip, so did the affairs of Jim Corrigan and Frank Rockefeller. They were experiencing difficulty in meeting payments on their loans; the mining venture was an especially heavy burden. At their request, John D. in March 1895 helped them by buying Corrigan’s Standard stock. He paid $420,000 or at the rate of $168 a share. It was the top market price on the day of the sale. John D. had sold some of his own stock at this figure at about this time, and also some for $140. It was time of tight money and the partners would have encountered difficulty getting such a large amount of cash anywhere—it certainly would not have been forthcoming on Wall Street. However, they were still in trouble. To ease their situation, John D. cancelled interest payments due him from Corrigan on one loan, surrendered the Franklin Mining certificates he held as security on another, and released Corrigan from liability as endorser of Frank's $130,000 note. Nevertheless, the partners were forced to give up their mining venture, selling the company to outsiders for much less than it was worth. Frank and Jim blamed Rockefeller for their plight. They claimed that he had pressured them for their debts, thus compelling the sacrifice of the mining company. As the country’s economy rallied, Standard Oil stock like that of all businesses increased in value. Corrigan watched the upward spiral, becoming more and more dissatisfied. Finally in 1897 more than two years later, encouraged according to Nevins by the prominent Cleveland lawyer, Judge Steveson Burke, Corrigan sued John D. Rockefeller. He demanded the return of the Standard stock or payment at the rate of $500 a share, a price he himself seems to have set. Rockefeller, he alleged, had cheated him, knowing the stock would be worth more. John D. had misrepresented the surplus of the company, and further, as a company trustee, had no right to deal in the stocks, he claimed.

The matter was submitted to arbitration. Rockefeller’s lawy« Virgil P. Kline and George W. Murray, considered the issue so it portant that books of the Standard Oil Company were brought in court. It was the first time these documents had left 26 Broadwe By a unanimous decision in 1899 Rockefeller was complete cleared: as a director rather than a trustee he had a legal right buy the stock; he had not misrepresented the surplus. In short |] “was actuated by a desire to accommodate the plaintiff and relie him of his embarrassments.” There was a second lengthy hearing at Judge Burke's insistenc The decision was again unanimously in John D.’s favor. Corrig: took the case to the Ohio Supreme Court which, in 1902, with 1 dissenting voice, likewise exonerated John D. Frank, of course, had been deeply involved in the whole affa’ What had been an issue between the brothers for twenty years w thus concluded. The case gained much attention. Despite John D clear vindication, Clevelanders chose to view him as a rich man wl had ground down his brother. Everywhere the public was reac at this time to think the worst of the Standard Oil head. The Frank Rockefellers had a Cleveland home on Longwoc¢ Avenue and were well and favorably known in the city. Differir sharply from taciturn John D., Frank was jovial and outgoing. A together a likeable man, he had many friends, among them a coter at the Union Club who were ready to overlook his faults. Howeve Frank would break out with loud denunciations of his brother, e pecially when in financial straits. After the Corrigan suit he w: more abusive than ever, declaring John D. had taken advantage « him and his partner. In March 1905 an article by Ida Tarbell piping on the same ther appeared in McClure’s Magazine, much of it the result of a vis with Frank. He was never reluctant to meet the press. Attorne Kline was so incensed at what he termed Miss Tarbell’s biased, it accurate statements that he issued a formal denial. Frank, his wife and daughters, Alice and Ann, withdrew fro: the Euclid Avenue Baptist Church, in December, 1908. Broadcas ing the fact, Frank explained he no longer could worship under th

## Page 185

same roof with his brother. John D.’s feelings were deeply wounded by Frank’s behavior, the suit and Corrigan’s unjust charges. The breach between the two brothers was irreparable. They never again spoke to each other. The younger Rockefeller was to hold forth in further vindictive interviews. At his death in 1917, John D. traveled from New York for the funeral to stand sorrowfully beside the grave in Lake View Cemetery. Inglis came to Cleveland with Rockefeller. They called on Frank’s widow and children, all of whom received Rockefeller with utmost affection, Inglis recorded. While in the city, Rockefeller took time to look up some familiar old landmarks. Going over the Superior Viaduct, he had his driver stop while he gazed down on the Flats and Merwin Street, marvelling at the changes since his youth when the river front was home territory to him. When Frank died, his two brothers cancelled the mortgages and all other loans made to him on his Kansas ranch. Soldier Creek Park passed to Frank’s widow unencumbered with debt. Mrs. Rockefeller, who died in 1932 in Cleveland, sold it in sections, realizing over $100,000 from the property, according to one account. In pleasant contrast to the trying experiences with Frank, an event took place in 1900 that was of great gratification to John D. Rockefeller and his entire family. This was the dedication of Alta House on Mayfield Road in the Murray Hill district. The neighborhood, better known as “Little Italy,” was peopled largely by Italians newly arrived from their homeland. Through the influence of his daughter, Alta, Rockefeller developed a genuine concern for Alta House. Later known as Alta Social Settlement, it had blossomed from a modest day nursery and kindergarten started by some enterprising local residents like Joseph Carabelli, monument maker, and his wife. Alta Rockefeller was interested from the first, stopping by to observe the busy program, and before long spending considerable time at the center. Her father often drove along steep, dusty Mayfield Road. He observed the industrious people and their youngsters spilling out of humble, crowded homes. Men from Little Italy were on the labor

Cowl president of the Chamber of Commerce signalled the chairman th

## Page 187

he wanted the floor. He had an important announcement for the meeting. John D. Rockefeller, Cowles told the audience, was making a centennial gift to Cleveland of $300,000 in cash as reimbursement for certain parcels of land in the Doan Brook Valley purchased by the city for a parkway. Lack of funds heretofore had prevented carrying out plans for this development. As loud applause started, Cowles held up his hand for silence. He had more good news: Rockefeller had assigned the city 270 additional acres for parkland in the same valley. He had bought the land for approximately $270,000. Now the cheers rose to the rafters. There were cries of “Rockefeller, speech, speech!” But Rockefeller was not present. After the meeting quieted, Cowles went on to explain that with this money and land it would be possible to create the long hoped for seven-mile stretch of parkland which would link Gordon Park with Wade Park. He was listened to with respect for he was an authority on Cleveland real estate. Besides, as everyone knew, he handled Rockefeller property as well as that of Charles F. Brush, developer of the arc light and famed for innovations in the field of electricity. In a few years a parkland was created between Gordon and Wade Parks, called Rockefeller Park. Another Rockefeller gift of parkland to Cleveland at about this time is now a nature preserve known as Shaker Lakes Park. Originally part of the Shaker colony, it is a scenic wilderness traversed by Doan Brook where it feeds the two natural Shaker lakes. The area contains some of the finest ancient trees in this part of Ohio, as well as rare plant life. The Shakers, reduced to a handful of Believers, sold their holdings in 1889. Prompted by his love of trees and nature in general, Rockefeller bought the section to save it, the deal involving several separate parcels. In the Park Commission offices in Cleveland, there can be seen the thick and battered roll of deeds, each bearing signatures of John and Laura Rockefeller and the conditions by which all these parcels of land were transferred to the city: they were to be used for park purposes forever. 10°

## Page 188

Rockefeller offered Cleveland $100,000 for a bridge over Roch feller Park at Superior Street, providing the city added $20,000 complete the structure. This was complied with. Architect Schwei furth planned the span which, when completed, was praised for design. Contractors were the Clements Brothers, Robert, Willia and David. With some other parcels, Rockefeller’s total gifts to the City Cleveland for park purposes came to $865,038.87, according to re ords in the Rockefeller Archives. As a heavy investor, Rockefeller participated in a notable Clev land enterprise, erection of The Arcade. The company backing tl unique building was headed by Stephen Harkness, as presider and Charles Brush, as vice president. The “architectural marve' which was built at cost of $867,000, was rated among the worl arcades as second only to Milan’s. Architects John Eisenmann ar George H. Smith won particular comment for the broad stairwe that cascaded from Euclid Avenue to the lower level of Superi Street. The ingenious glass roof prompted the name “crystal pa ace.” As construction of the building was getting under way, Har ness died suddenly, succumbing in 1888 to a heart attack while c board his yacht “Twilight” off the coast of Florida. Among stockholders in The Arcade had been his friend Lou H. Severance, treasurer of the Standard Oil Company. In 1894 Ser verance married Harkness’s daughter, Florence, who was twenty five years younger than her bridegroom. Within a year of the wedding, death came to the bride who had been in ill health fc some time. In her memory Severance and the Harkness famil erected the Florence Harkness Chapel on the campus of the Colleg for Women, Western Reserve University. Cleveland was proud of The Arcade. In 1940, on the fiftieth ar niversary of the building's official dedication, the Euclid Avenu front was “modernized.” Over the entrance plaster medallions wer inserted framing bas relief likenesses of Harkness and Brush. During Cleveland’s centennial year, its most famous citize stepped down from leadership of the Standard Oil Company. Al though Rockefeller retained the title of president until 1911, he n aes

## Page 189

longer carried the full load of the industry he had created. Since no official announcement was made of Rockefeller’s new status, the public still held him responsible for all acts of the Standard. Consequently he continued to be the critics’ target. He had relinquished leadership on the eve of the automobile era. The company was moving into a radically different gear to meet the mounting demand for gasoline. Once a worthless by-product, gasoline now superseded kerosene as an oil product. Plants were revamped and procedures overhauled to keep pace with the booming auto sales. In 1900, the country boasted of 8,000 cars, largely rich men’s toys that frightened horses and stalled on highways. In 1915, the count was 2,500,000 and a new need for gasoline was forecast for aviation. Fresh oil reserves in the West and Southwest were being tapped to satisfy the thirsty motors. As stated in his Reminiscences, Rockefeller had hoped to give up business at the age of fifty. “Having begun work so young,” he wrote, he felt entitled to freedom “to devote myself to a variety of interests other than making money.” Confused conditions brought on by the recession of 1893 compelled postponement for a few years. After some time, the economy stabilized and by 1896 he was able to move into the background. | He now could turn to planning his philanthropies—he was to spend the rest of his life giving away most of his great fortune. He had time for his landscaping and his newest hobby, golf. But the first priority was his health. His normally robust physique gave way from the pressures and strains under which he had labored so long. He was stricken with a disorder diagnosed as alopecia, resulting in loss of all his hair. In a memorandum book in which at one point Mrs. Rockefeller recorded certain memorable events in her life, she noted that in March 1901 “Alta began housekeeping at No. 5 W. 53rd St., N.Y., Lute’s birthday. John’s moustache began to fall out, and all the hair on his body had followed by August.” Specialists were consulted and a rigorous treatment prescribed. Before long, however, he put himself in the care of one of Cleveland’s prominent physicians, Dr. Hamilton F. Biggar, a homeopath. ae ah
