The Rockefellers, long adherents of this school of medicine, hi consulted the doctor previously, and were well acquainted wi him. As noted, he had accompanied the family on several triy Doctor and patient were the same age and a companionable pa Along with mild medication, Dr. Biggar instituted a “comm« sense way of life” for the ailing man. He was to eat simple foo leave the table while still hungry; exercise daily in the open air a1 get plenty of sleep. The regime appealed to Rockefeller. He px ferred plain food and was never a big eater. The muckrakers made much of his eating habits. A bad stomac it was claimed, held him to a “pauper’s diet’—doubtlessly in ret bution for his sins! With little liking for theater and concert goi1 or similar late night entertainment, the Rockefeller household we: to bed early by choice. Rockefeller’s health was restored, but n his hair. He wore a black skull cap for a time, later replacing it wi a more becoming wig. Some time before his illness, probably in 1899, Rockefeller h¢ become intrigued with golf, a sport that was gaining popularity many parts of the country. According to one tale, his interest in t] sport was first awakened during a game of quoits he was pitchir with a friend at a Lakewood, New Jersey country club. His partne noticing his accuracy with the rings, suggested he should take vu golf. That very afternoon he was persuaded to try a few shot He had beginner’s luck. His first ball soared out like that of a ve eran player. He promptly put in a four-hole course at Pocantico and by 19¢ had a nine hole links laid at Forest Hill. At the latter place tk terrain was more suitable and greatly to Rockefeller’s liking. Fe’ went into golf more seriously than the Squire of Forest Hill. H took lessons, secretly, from Joe Mitchell, famed professional at th Lake Shore Country Club, sending a carriage for him at eight ever morning. The Standard Oil head devoted the same careful atte1 tion to the game as to his business. Mrs. Rockefeller caught golf fever, and also with extreme secrecy she employed Mitchell as a coach. In due time she appeared at th first tee with her clubs, to be met by John D., who kindly offere

<!-- page_id: 0122-right | printed: 191 | viewer_token: 190 | viewer_sequence: 246/340 -->
## Page 191

to show her how to play. Meekly she stepped up, swung, sent her ball neatly down the fairway—and burst into laughter at her husband’s astonishment. Good weather or bad, Rockefeller was on the links every morning, Scores of Cleveland’s prominent citizens and some not so prominent played golf at one time or another at Forest Hill with him. There were three who made up a more or less regular foursome: Levi Scofield, Dr. Biggar and Dr. E. B. Rhodes, prominent East Cleveland physician. The Rhodes home was on Mount Union Street, its back yard bordering Forest Hill. When one of the cronies was absent they pressed in as substitute Ralph R. Snow, an attorney and former minister at the East Cleveland Baptist Church, or maybe Rockefeller’s brother-in-law Rudd. Occasionally Mrs. Rhodes played with Mrs. Rockefeller and Miss Spelman, who also became a golfer. Sometimes the women went around the course in Mrs. Rockefeller’s electric brougham, a kind of elegant precursor of the modern electric golf cart. If Rockefeller pursued golf as a health measure, it was one he enjoyed thoroughly. Ida Tarbell had her own interpretation: he played to keep fit so that he could live longer and be able to make more money! Perhaps she had heard of the tryst between Biggar and Rockefeller who vowed to play a round of golf when they reached one hundred. Dr. Biggar, however, died at age eightyseven in 1926. Biggar, a Canadian, had practiced law briefly in Toronto before coming to Cleveland in his mid-twenties to study medicine. He achieved wide recognition as a physician and surgeon. He was instrumental in founding the Homeopathic Hospital College, forerunner of Huron Road Hospital, to which Rockefeller was a generous contributor. The Biggars lived on Case Avenue not far from the Rockefeller Euclid Avenue residence. The doctor kept a stable of good horses ready to carry him at top speed to a patient's bedside. The big, handsome man had an outgoing, buoyant personality, the kind of physician whose patients said made them feel better the moment he entered the sick room.

Her information on the Standard was largely obtained from one official, H. H. Rogers, and he could be adroit in deflecting criticism from himself to Rockefeller. Apparently little effort was made to consult other Standard executives, and seemingly none at all to get in touch with Rockefeller. Standard Oil men winced at those of her barbs which hit home. Company leaders resented the unfair presentations and wanted to issue an official rebuttal. Rockefeller would have none of such a notion. “Not a word,” he said, “not a word about that misguided woman.” He would leave the matter to the judgment of the future. But Miss Tarbell was convincing. Public opinion turned more strongly than ever against him.

> 28 The Fruit Tree Gets The Clubs

A widely publicized thrust was made at Rockefeller during the muckraker era which pained him as much as any he had endured. But it also had its humorous side, which even he could appreciate. It was a comedy of errors with the big scene staged in Columbus, Ohio one Sunday morning in March 1905 at the Broad Street Congregational Church. The eloquent pastor, Washington Gladden, mounted his pulpit to deliver ostensibly a sermon on Christian ethics in business. Actually, it was a series of jibes against the Standard Oil Company as a monopoly with sharpest stabs aimed at Rockefeller. He had created, the preacher thundered, “a colossal estate whose foundations were laid in the most ruthless rapacity in commercial history.” The good man’s anger had been kindled, by a $100,000 donation from Rockefeller to the Board of Foreign Missions of the Congregational Church. Gladden did not want the gift. “It was tainted money!” he declaimed. Tainted Money! The phrase was a stroke of genius. Every newspaper in the land grabbed the words for headline use as editors played up the lively issue. About this time Rockefeller gave raul

During the entire year of 1905 Rockefeller was almost constantly in the public eye, particularly so in Cleveland. Through the years the hotel had become a Cleveland landma cherished by many for its association with Lincoln and othe among the great. Despite a hue and cry to preserve the venerate structure, Rockefeller razed it. For some time, however, the Bar Street portion survived, carrying on a modest business. Called an early example of modern functional design, the Rock feller Building remains a desirable office block. The architects, Knc and Elliott, patterned it after a Buffalo bank building planned t Louis H. Sullivan. Rockefeller appreciated the good lines of h building and was proud of it, but he never stepped inside it. One of the first tenants in the new structure was the Central N: tional Bank, which moved from the Perry-Payne Building wher the institution had been organized fifteen years previously by Col nel Jeremiah J. Sullivan. The Perry-Payne building, on the sam side of Superior Street a short distance to the west, was the city finest office building when erected by H. B. Payne in 1889. Th Perry in its name honored Payne’s wife, the former Mary Perr Shortly, Rockefeller transferred this block to his son for a do lar consideration. John D., Jr. dropped in at the building on h visits to Cleveland. From its upper floors, were he so inclined, h could view the busy Standard Oil Company complex sprawling ot from its original location along Kingsbury Run. Or at another var tage point he might gaze down over the Flats and Merwin Stree where his father had started work just fifty years before the Rock feller Building was dedicated. In 1920 John D., Jr. sold the building to Josiah Kirby, Clevelan financier, later imprisoned for mishandling the Cleveland Discour Company, of which he was president. Kirby erected a big, ey« catching electric sign, “KIRBY,” on the roof. Although later he sol the building, the glaring sign remained. The blatant change name had aroused the senior Rockefeller, and he ordered his son t buy back the building. In 1923, after some negotiations and pay ment of $2,937,000, he succeeded. The Rockefeller name was re stored at the building’s entrance, and the sign removed from it roof. Shortly the building again passed out of Rockefeller hand: this time with an agreement that the Rockefeller name be retaine by the new owners.

In McClure’s Magazine for July, Ida Tarbell denounced John D. again for his action in the Frank Rockefeller-Corrigan controversy. In the same article and a following one in August, she attacked the Oil King with a burst of venom and bad taste hard to reconcile with a woman of her attainments. Her purpose, she stated, was to warn the public about Rockefeller. The American people, she wrote, had a right to such information. He was not only the richest man in the world, he was the most important because through the millions he was giving to education and religious bodies, he threatened to control these great national forces. Since she considered origins revealing, she turned her attention to his father: his cancer cure peddling; his alleged “horse rustling”; the rumored trouble with the law in New York State; his long mysterious absences. Now, she announced, William Avery Rockefeller had disappeared. She set about analyzing John D., both from a profile sketch of him currently being circulated and from studying him firsthand in Cleveland. She had come to the city, meeting by prearrangement a local newspaper reporter who was to guide her to Rockefeller haunts. The two attended a Sunday service at the Euclid Avenue Baptist Church. They selected seats from which Miss Tarbell’s victim was directly within her range of vision. It was late fall, the last Sunday before Rockefeller left for his home in New York. Parishioners crowded around him to say good-bye. He was more the focus of attention than the minister, the writer observed. By her own descriptions of Rockefeller he was obviously among friends, Yet Miss Tarbell claimed he was in the grip of fear—“fear, fear” she wrote. And in this her shaft fell wide of its mark. No one ever before had accused Rockefeller of being afraid. Once during a particularly bad epidemic of threats and crank letters, it was suggested that he employ a bodyguard. Rockefeller ridiculed the idea; he could take care of himself. Watching him as he moved about in the Sunday School room that day, Miss Tarbell was impressed with the sense of power he exuded in every move. But he also seemed “the oldest man in the

Holden, J. H. McBride, D. Z. Norton, Andrew Squire, J. F. Whitelaw and J. B. Zerbe. They formed the committee on arrangements. Each man was to ask those among his acquaintances he thought would like to join the delegation. On the appointed day, September 26, 1905, more than four hundred men met at the Lodge, the Euclid Avenue entrance to Forest Hill, the big iron gates wide open now. Most had come by streetcar, some in chauffeur-driven automobiles and a few by horse and carriage. The visitors walked across the lawn up the hill to the house where Rockefeller, assisted by his wife and Miss Spelman, received them in the big parlor. As reported the next day on the first pages of Cleveland newspapers and others throughout the country, his callers found Rockefeller in glowing health. He was lithe of step, keen-eyed and younger than his years. The contrast was striking between this vigorous, confident man and the image Miss Tarbell, beclouded by hate, had projected a few weeks before. Faultlessly garbed in a cutaway coat and striped trousers, Rockefeller shook the hand of each guest, calling many by first name, stopping to chat or exchange anecdotes. He was a jovial host, obviously pleased with this demonstration of friendship and goodwill. The crowd overflowed to the veranda and lawn and listened to speeches. The first orator was Andrew Squire, nationally known corporation lawyer and senior partner of the firm of Squire, Sanders and Dempsey. There was a talk by Liberty Holden, a man as prominently identified with civic causes as with the newspaper business. In his response, emotion almost got the better of Rockefeller a time or two. “This is a very great honor,” he began and stopped. Collecting himself, he went on: “I shall always think of this afternoon as a very great day in my life.” He pointed to the big American flag waving above his house. It was there in remembrance, he explained, “of the day when I succeeded in obtaining a chance to work in Cleveland. That was just fifty years ago today,” he added dramatically. A murmur rippled over the gathering. Totally ignorant of the

## Page 201

At the conclusion of the talks refreshments were served. As the callers prepared to leave, each signed a sheet of parchment provided by the committee and left it with Rockefeller as a memento. Afterward a book in a limited edition of four hundred and twentyfive copies was published entitled Forest Hill, September 26, 1905. In it were recorded the remarks made that afternoon and facsimilies of the signatures of four hundred and nineteen leading Cleveland citizens. Probably no event in his adult life stirred Rockefeller more profoundly than this show of regard by members of his community. He was a sixteen-year old boy on that memorable September day when he climbed onto the high bookkeeper stool at Hewitt and Tuttle. Now he was sixty-six, officially retired and reputedly a billionaire.

> 29. Two Empires

With Rockefeller’s business obligations lightened, he and the family could indulge in travel. There were several jaunts to American historic and scenic sites, as well as to Europe. Dr. Biggar usually went along. As a souvenir of a stay in France in the summer of 1906, the doctor prepared a scrapbook diary which has been carefully preserved. The Rockefellers’ purpose this time was to see their daughter, Mrs. Charles Strong (Bessie) who was ill, suffering from a nervous malady in which she was obsessed with the fear of dying in poverty. She was staying in Compeignie at the summer place of her husband’s parents, the Rockefellers’ old friends, Dr. and Mrs. Augustus Strong. The Rockefeller party, according to the Biggar journal, left Cleveland by private railroad car for New York where they boarded the Deutschland. In addition to Mr. and Mrs. Rockefeller and the doctor, the travelers were: Miss Spelman and maid; Alta Rockefeller Prentice and maid; a niece (not named); Miss Turner, a

Standard, and a storm was brewing against the Standard Oil of Texas. Regretfully leaving his afflicted daughter, Rockefeller departed at once. He evidently had taken to heart the advice tendered him in France by Reporter Hoster; on reaching New York, to the amazement of the Fourth Estate, he consented to an interview. He hurried on to Cleveland to face the Ohio bombardment, and immediately entered a plea of “not guilty.” He was in the news more than ever. Reporters flocked to Forest Hill. Carrying out his new policy, he received one of them, Will S. Couch, a staff member of the Plain Dealer, who for a period headed the paper’s Washington bureau. The carefully written piece that he produced was appreciated by John D., who conferred the ultimate kudos on him—Couch was invited to play golf at Forest Hill. Soon, it was conceded in Cleveland newspaper circles that Couch was the one representative of the local press sure of an entree to the Standard Oil head. But Rockefeller continued to be chary of reporters as a whole, holding that they too often failed to quote him accurately and instead put their own words in his mouth. It was the Theodore Roosevelt trust-busting era, and for Rocke- \ feller, though he had retired, it proved to be one of the most difficult of his experience. Personal attacks increased, the allegations as irresponsible as they were malicious. The Standard combination and affiliates were raked by the biggest guns that state and federal officials could muster. Rockefeller unwisely ignored the censures directed at himself, thereby strengthening their credibility. On the other hand, charges against the Standard were refuted by batteries of lawyers who presented its case before every level of tribunal, including ultimately the Supreme Court itself. Rockefeller and Standard leaders were harried by subpoena servers, called into court and cross-examined for hours. It was of small consequence that John D. was no longer the Standard’s commander-in-chief—few knew about that. In November of 1906 a shower of blows descended on him: Word came from France of Mrs. Strong’s death; a second indictment was lodged against him in Ohio; the Indiana Standard was accused of accepting railroad rebates forbidden by state law, and the United

the front page announcing the size of the fine and that Rockefeller, after learning of it, had played one of the best games of his life! The article was signed by W. S. Couch. The heavy fine won approval from some, while others condemned it as confiscatory, “vindictive and very bad law.” Rockefeller’s reaction was warranted. Two years later, following an appeal, Landis’s decision was reversed, the fine cancelled and the costs paid by the prosecution. In the meantime the government had instituted seven suits against Standard companies and subsidiaries with still others scheduled under state officials. Rockefeller’s legal force included his Cleveland counsel, the law firm headed by Kline with William F. Carr, Sheldon Tolles and Frederick H. Goff as partners. Kline and Goff were particularly active in the Standard litigation, Kline continuing as Rockefeller’s attorney for years, and Goff until 1908 when he left the firm to become president of the Cleveland Trust Company. In 1914 Goff created the Cleveland Foundation, first of the community trusts in the Western world. In his law practice he had become well acquainted with Rockefeller. It was at the time when Rockefeller was launching his philanthropic agencies, then unique in concept. This contrast may have influenced Goff to some extent. At any rate, he shared Rockefeller’s conviction that property should be managed by a living and not a dead hand. Following Goffs death in 1923 a public memorial meeting was held in Cleveland presided over by Ambrose Swasey. A warm tribute was read from John D. Rockefeller to “my friend and counselor.” The federal case against Jersey Standard was filed in November 1906. It was not to be concluded until 1911. The defendants, beside the Jersey company itself, were seven Standard officials, four of them the familiar personalities of the early Cleveland era, namely: John D. and William Rockefeller, Flagler and Oliver Payne; the others, Archbold, Pratt, and Rogers. They had conspired to control the oil business through restraint of trade, it was alleged. Top ranking Standard men, one by one, were called to testify. Some were not always articulate witnesses.

During the interminable trial publicity of a more offensive s broke over Rockefeller. Joseph Pulitzer, editor of the sensati¢ bent New York World, seized upon Ida Tarbell’s statement in ] McClure’s Magazine article that the oilman’s father had dis peared. For years John D. had been a victim of the World’s sl and shell. Here was a ready-made chance to renew the barra Pulitzer announced a reward of $8,000 for information as to 1 whereabouts of the elusive William Avery Rockefeller. It wa: goodly sum, especially in those days. The offer stirred a flurry reader interest, but no definite lead was forthcoming. Impatient at the lack of information, Pulitzer assigned a report James W. Slaght, to find the missing parent. Posing as a doordoor salesman of safety razors, and equipped with numerous pho graphs of the botanic physician, Slaght followed illusive clues fr New York to Alaska and put in two years on the chase. Carefu maintaining his incognito, the newsman seems to have tarried so time in the Cleveland-Strongsville area, picking up what he cot from the Doctor’s old cronies or their families. Working his way the tedious quest, vending his razors in likely places and holdi doorstep colloquies with householders, he struck what seemed him was pay dirt in Freeport, Illinois. Pulitzer was satisfied with the “evidence” collected. On Febru: 2, 1908, he published a long and lurid article: “Secret Double L of Rockefeller’s Father Revealed by the World.” William Av Rockefeller, the newspaper proclaimed, deserted his wife E] shortly after the Civil War when they were living in Clevela Adopting the name Dr. William Levingston (sometimes spel “Livingston” by the World), he concluded a bigamist marri:

<!-- page_id: 0130-right | printed: 207 | viewer_token: 206 | viewer_sequence: 262/340 -->
## Page 207

with a Miss Margaret L. Allen in Ontario. Later they moved to Freeport, and also lived part of the time on the “bridegroom's” 400acre ranch on the Park River in North Dakota. By the time the reporter had sleuthed out this trail, the object of his search was dead. He died May 11, 1906, when ninety-six years old, Slaght stated. The old man was buried in Freeport and, conforming to accepted detective book fiction, was lying in an unmarked grave. Thus, the plot was nicely compounded. Slaght visited at length with the “widow.” When shown photographs of the elder Rockefeller she “verified” them at once as those of Dr. Levingston, the man she had believed was her legal husband —until shortly before his death. Then, according to the newspaper, he confessed who he really was. She pointed to a large photograph conspicuously displayed in her home. That, she declared, was Dr. Levingston. According to the World writer, it was a duplicate of the likeness of Rockefeller’s father that he had with him. Asked if she ever had approached John D. for help, she replied indignantly that she had not. Dr. Levingston had been a good husband. They had had years of happiness. She wanted to have no contact with the Rockefellers. The World’s secret agent discovered a Dr. Charles H. Johnston, a medical doctor, who claimed he had been in partnership with Levingston and knew his identity. He proceeded to recite incidents in the elder Rockefeller’s early life, which seemingly, he could have learned only from the man himself. However, such information had been in one of the McClure’s Magazine articles in which Miss Tarbell thoroughly winnowed that same field. Johnston could have obtained his stories from that publication. A Freeport newspaper got into the act. The North Dakota property which Levingston had acquired through government grant, it stated, had been deeded by William Avery Rockefeller to Pierson Briggs, his son-in-law. The day following the New York World’s bombshell, Cleveland newspapers retold the tale. Frank Rockefeller, ever ready to talk, denied that his father was dead. He was living in retirement in the West, he said. William Rockefeller’s son Percy agreed with him.

Years later Frank’s daughters stated that the old man had died his ranch in 1909 aged one hundred. But they had no real proof this. There were never any other comments by the family, not wh the story was first published, or later. Briggs said nothing. John was tight-lipped. Pulitzer printed more on the “scandal” from ti: to time. Readers followed the story avidly at first, but inter palled. The bits and pieces of the puzzle seemed to fall almost 1 neatly into place. People sensed Rockefeller’s chagrin at all this, and a kindly fe ing for him developed. Whether or not he knew the truth of t story, they admired his dignified silence. Many years later Slaght recounted to a friend that he h watched as his article was rolling off the presses of the Wor Standing there he wondered if Levingston really was Rockefelle father. So, whatever knowledge the family had of William Ave Rockefeller, to the public he remains a man of mystery. While the dust was settling on Pulitzer’s “revelation,” anoth furor that affected Rockefeller was stirred up by William Ra dolph Hearst. The publisher brought to light letters Archbold h sent to leading political figures. The contents divulged the write efforts to influence legislation and his donations to campaign func Such conduct, in complete violation of Rockefeller’s principles, d turbed him greatly. Though he had known nothing about any this, the opprobrium that was generated splashed over him. T public, uninformed of his retirement, looked on him as president the Standard and therefore responsible for actions of its officers. In the meantime the government case against the Standard whi had opened in 1906 in the federal Circuit Court of Missouri w grinding on under Frank B. Kellogg as special prosecutor, w! later was Secretary of State under Presidents Coolidge and Hoov | It was November 1909 before the Court's verdict came: a comple | victory for the government and for Kellogg. Taft, by now in tl White House, personally congratulated him. Jersey Standard w ordered to divest itself of all subsidiaries and was given thirty da in which to comply.

<!-- page_id: 0131-right | printed: 209 | viewer_token: 208 | viewer_sequence: 264/340 -->
## Page 209

Standard’s lawyers immediately appealed, argued before the United States Supreme Court in March 1910 and reargued in January 1911. They had to wait until the following May for the decision: The Standard Oil Company (New Jersey) had violated the Sherman Anti-Trust Law ... there was “an intent to set up a monopoly . . . to drive others from the field. . . .” Chief Justice Edward D. White, described as “heavy of mind and body,” used 20,000 words to detail ponderously how the holding company had interfered with competition in the market place and now, within six months the thirty-eight companies which it controlled must be separated. The strength and soundness of the colossus became apparent during the court proceedings, winning admiration even from those not friendly to Rockefeller. He had brought order to the oil business—as had been his aim—and in so doing had demonstrated methods that were to be adopted by other industries. Following the separation, individual companies pursued their operations with renewed initiative, the Standard of New Jersey forging ahead of them all. Standard Oil stock appreciated in value, profits the year after the trust breakup being higher than ever before. The empire, which John D. had begun to create in Cleveland in 1870, was not destroyed, but divided in 1911 into its component parts. And that year Rockefeller retired in name as well as in fact. ; More time now could be given to his other vocation, the stewardship of his wealth. He had already made a start, founding the Rockefeller Institute for Medical Research in 1901. Its purpose was to discover the causes, prevention and treatment of disease. The Institute is now Rockefeller University devoted to graduate education and research in medicine. The General Education Board began functioning in 1902 “for promotion of education within the United States without distinction as to race, sex or creed.” Up to 1965 the Board distributed $325,000,000 for improvement of education on all levels. Two more agencies were set up later. The Rockefeller income was growing, keeping pace with rising consumption of petroleum products. As the dollars flowed in, so did

### 30. Forest Hill Nostalgia

Rockefeller, rapidly becoming a hazy legend in Cleveland where his wealth was rooted, comes alive in the reminiscences of a number of the city’s prominent men who as boys worked for him at Forest Hill. Theirs was the uninhibited closeup view of critical youth, a behind-the-scenes look at a world figure off guard, pursuing dayby-day routine. From their composite recollections there is flashed on the screen of history a vivid picture of the Oil King at home—a picture of him seldom seen. During the summer months the permanent staff at the Cleveland estate was augmented, bringing the count to fifty or more. With his interest in youth and in education, Rockefeller preferred that the extras were high school and college students who used the vacation to earn needed money for school expenses. Cyrus Eaton, prominent Cleveland financier, internationally known for his connections with Russian political leaders and his efforts to bring about world peace, was a Forest Hill summer employee when he was seventeen. In 1901 his uncle, Charles Aubrey Eaton, like him a Canadian, was called to the “Rockefeller Church” on Euclid Avenue. The minister, his wife and children first made their home in the Euclid Hotel at Huron and Brownell, within easy walking distance of the church. Cyrus joined them shortly for the summer vacation. Minister and family, including Cyrus, were invited for dinner at Forest Hill. Rockefeller asked the boy what he was going to do during his stay and learned that he already had taken the job of night clerk at the hotel. Mrs. Rockefeller felt this “was not a proper position for a boy like you.” She queried her husband: “John, can’t

| Fe Fp

<!-- page_id: 0137-left | printed: 212 | viewer_token: 212 | viewer_sequence: 276/340 -->
## Page 212

you find a place for Cyrus right here this summer?” Her husbar thought he could. “Although I worried as to how the hotel manager would g along without me,” Mr. Eaton chuckled, “I went to work the ne day at the estate. I did everything from milking cows to helping | tree moving operations that were much in evidence then.” The Eatons soon were installed in a house on Lee Road, the proy erty adjoining Forest Hill and probably belonging to Rockefelle The role of young Cy became a double one: week days he was a employee while on Sundays he rode to church with the Rockefe lers. The two-seated conveyance with a top was unostentatiot but the horses were the finest anywhere. Coachman Blakemore s: on the high front seat with the footman, Tim Concannon, h brother-in-law beside him, “They were an impressive pair,” Eato recalled, picturing them in smart dark tan liveries. Toward the end of his first summer at Forest Hill, the boy w: looking forward to a great treat, a trip to the Pan-American Exp sition at Buffalo as guest of the Rockefellers. They expected to lea Cleveland in a private car on the Lake Shore & Michigan Souther Railroad. The day before this big event, Cyrus at work in flower bec around the house, was summoned by Rockefeller’s secretary, Mr S. Louise Patteson, who obviously was much excited. She preside over the private telegraph instrument installed in the office of tk Big House. Moments before, the clicking had carried tragic new “President McKinley has been shot at the fair!” she exclaime “Take this message out to Mr. Rockefeller on the golf links. Hurry “I jumped on a bicycle and rode as fast as I could,” the indu: trialist said. “Mr. Rockefeller took the slip of paper I handed hin read it without comment, and then said calmly, ‘Please ask Mr Patteson to cancel the arrangements for the private car and te Mrs. Rockefeller we will not be going to Buffalo tomorrow.’ Wit that he resumed his golf game. It was a typical performance. M Rockefeller never permitted himself to become agitated or wrougl up over anything.” One of the dividends accruing to the youthful Cyrus from h

<!-- page_id: 0137-right | printed: 213 | viewer_token: 212 | viewer_sequence: 276/340 -->
## Page 213

> “vacation” at Forest Hill was the opportunity to meet illustrious guests. One who came frequently for stays of a week or two was Dr. Harper. He made a lasting impression on the boy, as he did on everyone who ever saw him. At this time the attention of the world was on him as President of the University of Chicago, which had been reestablished only recently with the Rockefeller millions. As described by his admirer, Dr. Harper was a “short, stout man, who was a hearty eater, as his figure indicated. He had a large head and gazed at you through thick glasses. Warm and friendly, he was a person one could not forget. Mr. Rockefeller was very fond of him.” He recalled that at times the university president used to hold forth at great length on some erudite subject. To lighten his boredom on such occasions Rockefeller would repeat one of his jokes. “If I was within earshot,” he declared, “I was the butt of one Mr. Rockefeller had improvised from a currently popular ditty: She sighed Cy sighed They sighed Side by side By the river side.

At the conclusion of course we all laughed dutifully. “One subject Dr. Harper talked about, Russia, we listened to with great attention. He had been there in 1899-1900 and again in 1902.” Both times he made the trip with the wealthy Chicagoan, Charles R. Crane, an authority on the country. Through Crane’s many contacts, he was enabled to see the real Russia. Cyrus was fascinated with accounts of the people the two men had met—Emperor Nicholas, whom Harper described as gracious and cordial; eminent intellectuals like Tolstoy; skilled craftsmen, musicians, stars of opera and ballet. “When in Russia, Dr. Harper, as a good Baptist, made a practice of looking up members of this denomination.” He declared, the industrialist recounted, “that at that period Russia had more Baptists than any other country, save the United States.”

Having a Baptist minister for a father was a great help in getting coveted summer employment at Forest Hill, as Charles and Clifford Stilwell discovered. The Rev. H. F. Stilwell, their father and a good friend of Rockefeller, was the pastor of the First Baptist Church. The boys working in small gangs under a foreman, trimmed trees to keep the vistas open. They absorbed a good deal about tree management. Some of the time they caddied for Rockefeller, both often pressed into service as a team. Rockefeller bicycled from the house to and over the golf course. At the same time as he often explained, he wanted to save his strength for the game, and so had his caddie, frequently one of the Stilwells, push the bicycle. The other brother was likely to come along holding a big black umbrella over Rockefeller if the sun was hot, or if a shower came up. “It took a good deal to keep him from golf,” Mr. Charles Stilwell said in recalling those days. “We worked a ten and a half hour day with a half hour off for lunch which we brought from home. Our pay was twelve and a half cents an hour, which seemed all right—it was, too, for our age in those days. It was a long day, but there was a fine crowd of young fellows working there, too. We knew them all.”

### 31. Finale

It was the Rockefellers’ custom to leave Forest Hill in the fall fc their New York home. However, as preparations for departure were under way in October 1913, Miss Spelman was taken sick Before she had recovered fully, Mrs. Rockefeller became serious] ill and rapidly grew worse. Plans for travel had to be cancelled. Rockefeller remained at th Homestead until his wife could be moved. By this time it was lat February. He had stayed beyond what was called tax listing day February first, according to Ohio law. Cuyahoga County Tax Commissioners John D. Fackler and Wi liam Agnew were quick to act. They notified Rockefeller that h must file an intangible personal property tax return. He ignored th matter, holding he was not liable for such an Ohio tax, since lega’ ly he was a citizen of New York State and had been for nearl twenty years. He paid this tax in that state. Nevertheless, the commissioners assessed him $1,500,000 on a estimated evaluation of $311,000,000 on all his intangible propert not only in Cleveland but in New York as well. They warned hir there would be a delinquency penalty of fifty percent. In othe words, delay in payment would bring the tax to $2,250,000. Rockefeller refused to pay and the commissioners were dete mined to collect. With such a bounty, Fackler announced with relish that East Cleveland taxes would be reduced twenty percen Fackler was an East Cleveland resident living on Rosemont Roac Miss Spelman was now recovered. Mrs. Rockefeller, althoug ailing, was strong enough to travel. The family with numerous ser vants left for New York late in February 1914. Mrs. Rockefelle never again was to see her much-loved home on The Hill. Rockefeller’s lawyers brought suit against P. C. O’Brien, Cuyz hoga County Treasurer, enjoining him from leveling or collectin the tax on the grounds that John D. was not legally an Ohio resi

dent. Kline was his chief counsel, Cyrus Locher the county’s prose. cuting attorney. Fackler and Agnew made an issue of a large painting, “The Dis puted Boundary,” by Erskine Nicol. Rockefeller had lent it in No. vember 1913 to the Art Loan Exposition arranged by prominent Cleveland art lovers in the Kinney and Levan Building. The well: attended show, it developed, stimulated interest in establishing the long hoped for Cleveland Art Museum. The painting had been insured by Rockefeller’s secretary, Sims for $75,000 for the show and at close of the exhibit he shipped it tc Pocantico. In court, Fackler pounced on the high insurance as indicating a valuable work of art, which as such was taxable prop: erty. Rockefeller’s lawyers claimed it had been insured beyond it worth; Nicol’s rating as an artist had dropped of late. Affidavits tc prove the point were produced from a New York dealer who statec the painting’s value was under $3,000. In January 1914, trustees of three separate legacies left to found an art museum in Cleveland reached an agreement clearing the way for such an institution. Work on the project went forward Shortly, it was found that one sizeable bequest, funds left by H. B. Hurlbut, had shrunken in the years, thus curtailing plans fot the museum. At this juncture Rockefeller was appealed to to make up the deficit, but he declined. His reason as generally stated was that he felt the proposed building was larger than Cleveland needed. More likely it was the timing of the appeal that influenced him to refuse assistance. When the museum problem was presented to him he was fighting local tax officials’ efforts to collect a $2,500,000 assessment he did not owe. He was heard to exclaim during this period that Clevelanders were an ungrateful lot; with one hand they were trying to extract money from him unlawfully, and with the other begging for a gift. In 1925, some years after the Art Museum was built, Mrs. John D. Rockefeller, Jr., who was especially fond of the “Disputed Boundary,” suggested donating it to the Cleveland institution. Her father-in-law was willing although he feared it “might be too trivial

## Page 227

Ohio’s governor, Democrat James M. Cox, had endorsed the tax assessment against Rockefeller, who faced a subpoena and other harassments if he stepped foot in the state. It was, therefore, not safe for him to try to bring his wife’s body to Cleveland for burial. She was given a temporary resting place in the Archbold mausoleum in Sleepy Hollow Cemetery at Tarrytown. World War I was escalating in Europe. Life in the United States, not yet involved, was proceeding more or less as usual. Cleveland newspapers printed news of Rockefeller’s tax fight side by side with reports of bitter fighting at Ypres. At the first hearing of the tax suit, the decision was in Rockefeller’s favor. Fackler and Agnew appealed. The extended litigation meant further delay in moving Mrs. Rockefeller’s body from Tarrytown to Cleveland. At this juncture it appears that her family, particularly John D., Jr., advocated selection of another grave site, presumably one outside of Ohio. Her husband, however, felt otherwise as is shown by the following letter dated June 15, 1915: Dear Son: Much as I regret under all the circumstances, my wish would be to continue Lake View Cemetery as the burial place for Mother and me. gta) lee ye nes

John D. Rockefeller

The tax case reached the United States District Court, presided over by the respected Judge John H. Clarke, former Cleveland attorney. He granted the plea of the Standard Oil head to enjoin Cuyahoga County from collecting the tax, rendering the opinion that Rockefeller was not a citizen of Ohio, but had been detained in the State in the winter of 1913-1914 by his wife’s illness. Further, in handing down his decision, the jurist added that he was aware there might be criticism of his ruling. Nevertheless, Clarke said, it is as incumbent on the judiciary to protect the rights of the rich as of the poor. It was at last possible to bring Cettie “home.” On August 10th, 1915, five months after her death, she was buried in Lake View Cemetery next to Eliza Rockefeller’s grave with one space left between them. The graves of Mrs. Rockefeller’s parents were in the next lot.

box. The flames leaped from floor to floor and out the roof, crackling and snapping and throwing a red glare over nearby homes. Little Lizzie Rhodes was awakened to find her bedroom filled with a queer dancing light. Her father dashed into his clothes and rushed off to the Hill. This time Lizzie was not allowed to go along. Dr. Rhodes joined a crowd of neighbors huddled on the lawn. Led by Pat with tears streaming down his face, efforts were made to rescue some of the furnishings like the grand piano on which John and Laura had played duets. But nothing was saved. Even the firemen were helpless. The cause of the conflagration was never determined. With the charred heap on the high slope, there was much speculation about the future of Forest Hill. As we have seen, the hope of its becoming an expanded campus for Western Reserve University was presented by university officials in 1922 to the Rockefellers, father and son, and rejected by them. Soon word got about that Rockefeller had signed over the estate to John D., Jr. Under the latter’s custody in the mid-1920s the storied realm was broken up. Portions provided sites for a new Huron Road Hospital, a junior high school, and a Masonic hall. A considerable section was laid out as an allotment. Handsome homes were built with full regard for the magnificent trees and scenic sites. Stores and business blocks took advantage of the Euclid Avenue frontage. A broad highway, Forest Hill Boulevard (often erroneously spelled Forest Hills), was cut through the estate, invading it where the big gate and Pat Lynch’s stone Lodge had stood. Management of the project was in the hands of a locally organized real estate company, the two Rockefellers staying in the background. In keeping with John D., Jr.’s, concern for preservation of natural beauty spots, he set aside 266 acres, a little less than a third of the whole, to become a public park. After the death of the senior Rockefeller, the son donated it in his memory to East Cleveland and Cleveland Heights within which municipalities the land lay. Speaking at the dedication ceremonies, John D., Jr., noted that this area to be called Forest Hill Park was the heart of the original

Complications and delays plagued the enterprise. Costs constant ly outran expectations. Requests for funds went regularly to Nev York. Records, while incomplete and confused, show that Rocke feller, Sr., answered the continuing SOS with remittances o $20,000, $30,000, $1,000, and $300. John D., Jr., came from Nev York on several occasions to “see how his money was being spent and no doubt also to check up on his building operations at Fores Hill. In the early spring of 1926, demolition got underway for the ven. erable “Rockefeller Church,” famed Cleveland landmark and re pository of so many memories for John D., Jr., and his father. By May of that year the first services were held in the chapel of the new building. Work in the main body of the church had been haltec by lack of money. With a $75,000 loan from a Cleveland bank, the banquet hall with accommodations for 1,000 was finished, and the first services of worship were held in this room. At a discouraging point in church finances, the Rev. John Snape, who had accepted the difficult charge, went to New York to consult with the Rockefellers. Shortly, he was able to announce that $250,000 would be forthcoming from Rockefeller, Sr., and his son, in part in memory of Laura Rockefeller. The money made possible the completion of both the main Sunday School room that was big enough to take care of 3,000, and the auditorium with a seating capacity of 2,300. Further, it was learned, there would be an additional $100,000 from Rockefeller if the congregation raised $250,000. The membership accepted this challenge, and eventually pledges for this amount were given. The new Euclid Avenue Baptist Church of polychrome terra cotta, with Biblical symbols carved on its facade and heroic figures flanking the high entrance arch, was a massive, handsome edifice. Mrs. Woodin could not have imagined her beloved Erie Street Church flowering into anything as elegant as this! Yet the structure, impressive though it appeared, never was finished, due to lack of funds. One example was the tower. Intended to soar aloft carrying five floors of Sunday School rooms topped by a carillon, its construction was halted when only partially completed.

The years were piling up for Rockefeller. He had outlived by de cades his four associates, young men who in the pioneer days o Cleveland had pooled their dreams and their hard work to stan by him as he brought into being the Standard Oil Company. Harkness, warmhearted and astute, whose money had quickene the enterprise into life, died in 1888, at age seventy. Flagler, secon: in command, who above all the others had won John D.’s affectio and respect, pushed his $40,000,000 railroad across Florida’s island to Key West in 1912 and died the next year. His passing was a blov to Rockefeller. William Rockefeller’s death in 1922 profoundly grieved John L for the brothers had been close since earliest childhood. After chilling canter in Central Park, William developed pneumonia t

<!-- page_id: 0147-right | printed: 233 | viewer_token: 232 | viewer_sequence: 296/340 -->

which he succumbed. The blustering Sam Andrews, the skilled re finer of the group, had died years before. Rockefeller’s rules for living paid off. His health remained excel lent, his mind as alert as ever. His eyesight and hearing were un impaired, and even his teeth were sound. As he grew older, he be came more affable—really very friendly—showing a sociability tha probably was inborn but had been submerged during his bus; early years. Contrary to persistent reports, he had no digestive curb: but ate abstemiously through choice. There were more guests ai the dinner table—Standard Oil executives, directors of his philan thropies, ministers, world figures, and the not-so-great, all of who he enjoyed. Rockefeller admired achievement, but rank or socia standing never made an impression on him. Belgium’s Cardinal Mercier, and, at another time, King Alber! came to thank him for contributions to Belgium Relief during World War I. Rockefeller had hoped America would stay out o} the conflict, feeling that Germany could be controlled by effective economic sanctions. But with the United States once in, he wholly backed the cause. In 1917 when he and Inglis came to Cleveland Rockefeller’s subscriptions to the Red Cross, YMCA, K of C, liberty loans and the like for that year equalled $70,000,000. At Forest Hill as his leisure increased, he frequently had had his chauffeur stop during afternoon automobile rides so that he might chat with farmers, tradesmen, and anyone who caught his atten. tion. He kept up the practice in drives about Pocantico and Ormond. He liked all kinds of people and often invited young folk to accompany him on his outings. News spread about his support of the war effort. The far-flung benefits of his heavily financed philanthropic programs were gaining attention. The public, heretofore unable to see anything but menacing evil in John D. Rockefeller, became equally extreme in praising him. While this swing of the opinion pendulum undoubtedly surprised him, it nonetheless must have been a pleasant change. John D. Rockefeller lived long enough to see in operation his wise and basic principles of giving: His gifts, rather than being turned

## Page 234

to specific needs were allocations aimed at inspiring others | cooperate and carry on; there were no rigid provisions for futur utilization to be outmoded in a changed era; nothing was set up 1 perpetuity. “Perpetuity,” he once remarked dryly, “is a prett long time.” Years before, while his wife was still alive, Rockefeller ha settled large inheritances on his children, his son receiving the bul of the fortune left from the millions channeled into the phila thropic agencies. The real satisfaction of his last years was the complete unde standing between him and his son. John D., Jr., joined Gates in tt task of administering distribution of Rockefeller wealth, and | times represented his father in business matters. Rockefeller, S: keeping well informed, was content to look on, his pride never en¢ ing in “Mr. John’s” management of family obligations. With lively interest, he followed events of his world and ; ninety-six could write clear headed letters of advice on investment But he was frail. He took short walks for exercise and went f only brief automobile rides. Gradually he grew weaker, relying good deal on a wheelchair and requiring constant attendance. At 4 o’clock Sunday morning, May 23, 1937, John D. Rockefell died at his Florida home. Funeral services were held at Pocantic with the renowned Baptist and family friend, Dr. Harry Emersc Fosdick, delivering a brief and moving eulogy. While the funer was in progress, the beautiful carillon of his Riverside Church New York played “Lead Kindly Light.” In tribute to Rockefelle as the service began, work ceased for five minutes in every sho every plant and office of the Standard Oil Company in Ameri and in those abroad. In Cleveland where the company started, it was indeed an ir pressive silence; the Standard Oil Trust that had been dissolve by court decree thirty years before was revived and again in opé« ation during those five minutes. His industrial empire still stoo On May 27th his body was brought to Cleveland where he hi begun his venture. In the accompanying party were Mr. and Mi (Alta) Prentice and children; Mr. and Mrs. John D. Rockefell:

<!-- page_id: 0148-right | printed: 235 | viewer_token: 234 | viewer_sequence: 298/340 -->
## Page 235

Jr., their five sons and son-in-law, and the Rey. Willard Richard son, a Brown University classmate of John D., Jr., and a Baptis minister. They traveled by private car to the New York Central’ East Cleveland station. Harold McCormick, the former husbanc of Edith Rockefeller, came from Chicago. The entourage was joine: by Cleveland relatives and close friends, among them Frank Rocke feller's daughters, Mrs. Walter S. Bowler, Mrs. William F. Nash and Miss Alice Rockefeller; Frank H. Rudd and Mrs. Edward A Roberts, the children of William and Mary Ann Rudd; the Brook Emenys—Mrs. Emeny (Winifred Rockefeller), Percy’s daughterand Mr. and Mrs. Harry D. Sims. The motor cavalcade proceeded east along Euclid Avenue t« Forest Hill Boulevard, following it past the lake and the forme golf links to Lake View Cemetery, via Lee and Mayfield Roads Burial was at the foot of the towering Rockefeller shaft in the space the oil magnate had reserved for himself between his wife and hi: mother. The brief graveside services were conducted by the Rev Mr. Richardson, and by Rockefeller’s friend, Dr. Dore Sharpe, ex. ecutive director of the Cleveland Baptist Association. John D. was the last of his father’s family. His sisters Lucy anc Mary Ann were lying nearby in this plot. Frank was also interred in this cemetery—but not here. William rested in a mausoleum in Sleepy Hollow Cemetery. Where their father’s grave was, no one knew. A roll call among the headstones and monuments in Lake View would have alerted a host of those John D. had known best, friends and enemies alike. Here was his first partner, Maurice Clark; Truman P. Handy, from whom he had obtained his first loan, the $2,000 seed money of his career; Dan P. Eells; Oliver H. Payne; Amasa Stone, at peace at last; Harkness, with his wife Anna who had survived him many years; the faithful Ambrose McGregor, and Mark Hanna, Mrs. Backus, William Scofield and Jim Corrigan, too. Dr. Biggar and Levi Scofield were together again, and here, also, was Sam Andrews. Henry Chisholm, Rockefeller’s friend since the days of the Erie Street Church, was memorialized by a life size statue near the entrance erected by his workers in the rolling mills.

## Page 236

When death came, Rockefeller was within two months of h ninety-eighth birthday. He had not realized his goal of reachir his one-hundredth. It was one objective in his carefully planne life he did not achieve. Two children survived him, John D., Jr., and Alta. The latter husband, Parmalee Prentice, was a brilliant corporation lawyer | New York. Later he turned to scientific agriculture, making h farm near Williamstown, Massachusetts, renowned for his exper ments. Bessie, the Rockefellers’ first born, had died in 1906. Edith died 1932, having presided over the magnificent McCormick mansic in Chicago. She also had spent considerable time abroad. One her major interests was the teachings of Carl Jung who was a clo friend. Rockefeller had heartily concurred with his contemporary, A) drew Carnegie, who declared that “the man who dies rich di disgraced.” Nonetheless, when the oilman’s will was probated was clear that he had not died poor. Despite his efforts otherwis he left an estate that came to $26,410,837. Of this, $16,630,000 w: absorbed by state and federal taxes. Chief heir of the residue was his granddaughter, Margar Strong De Cuevas. She was the daughter of Bessie, who had die before Rockefeller made his distributions to his children. As a chil Margaret was often with her grandparents after her mother’s dea’ and made long visits at Forest Hill. She married George De Cueva a Chilean, scion of a titled Spanish family, and well-known for h ballet troupe which toured world capitals. _ Rockefeller had kept to his getting and giving convictions. / | his death the monies he had given away through the years totale $530,000,000. Of this $446,000,000 was allocated to the philanthro; ic agencies he had established. When John D., Jr. died in 1961 wh he had dispensed added up to $561,000,000. Of this $41,000,0( was transmitted to the family philanthropic boards, the remaind distributed to various other projects such as restoration of William burg, the Cathedral at Rheims, and like interests. Thus the Rockefeller philanthropic organizations received

<!-- page_id: 0149-right | printed: 237 | viewer_token: 236 | viewer_sequence: 300/340 -->
## Page 237

various times a combined $487,000,000 from father and son. Ye these agencies by 1970 had expended $1,500,000,000 in appropria tions throughout the world. In other words, the four philanthropi boards spent over a billion dollars more than they had received The endowments of Rockefeller Foundation and Rockefeller Uni versity still exceed the original grants. Accounting for the amazing showing is the fact that gifts fo the most part were made in stocks, largely those of Standard Oi companies, and were estimated in dollars at their market pric when donated. In the passing years, indicative of the nation’s con tinuous growth, the stocks and dividends have increased in worth This, plus astute management of capital structure, explains the fis cal magic that produced more income and permitted more expendi ture from the initial sum donated than the sum itself. Benefactions of John D. Rockefeller Sr. reached into eighty-eigh countries. His passing brought messages by the thousands fron far and near: condolence for his death and gratitude for this life Communications poured into New York headquarters of his phil. anthropic boards. The avalanche of letters, telegrams, and cable: came in every language, from important and humble recipient: engaged in every kind of work—from the internationally knowr head of a hospital in China, from a lone Austrian scientist pursuing individual research in vaccine. Against scorching criticism in his lifetime, whether from muck. rakers, courts or individuals, he had refused to defend himself He was content to await the appraisal of time. Now the verdict was coming in... .

Frank Rockefeller: An interview with his nephew, Charles E. Dangler Cleveland, whose mother, the former Effie Scofield, was a sister of Fra wife. Edward Dan; Mr. Dangler’s father, founded the Dangler Stove Company in Clevelan 1885. Harkness: The family had a large estate on the Lake Shore at Willough Following Harkness’s death and that of his daughter Florence, Mrs. H: ness established the Florence Harkness Fresh Air Camp for Children o section of the property, deeding it to the Day Nursery Association of Cle land. This was an early Cleveland charity of which Mrs. Rockefeller \ one of the founders. The Harkness estate was purchased later by the Cle land Electric Illuminating Company as site for its Eastlake plant, and camp moyed to its present location on SOM Center Road, in Willough Ohio. Florence Harkness: Her marriage to Louis Severance took place in Willough a private train bringing guests from Cleveland for the wedding, accord to a memorial pamphlet by The Rev. Hiram C. Haydn, December 11, 18 Mrs. Severance left a $100,000 bequest to the Day Nursery Association found the Mary Whittlesey Day Nursery in memory of her beloved “Gra ma (Mrs. Charles) Whittlesey.” The nursery was established on Cedar A nue in the seventy-seven hundred block. ## Unnumbered Page

The more imposing houses were across the street. Also, at the time it was a little too far out to be quite haut-monde. Neither fact was of the slightest consequence to the Rockefellers. They became sentimentally attached to the place, especially Mrs. Rockefeller. She thought of it as her “Mother House,” for all her children, except Bessie, were born here. The Euclid Avenue residence was the first of several homes the Rockefellers eventually owned, and they were very happy in it. Rockefeller never could bear to part with the property, even after he left Cleveland and had no further need for a pied a terre in the city. The house has been described as “modified French,” probably because of the mansard roof with its rounded dormer window inserts. A columned portico at the entrance lent a classic touch, while window groupings reflected the Victorian mode. The architect managed to blend it all into a pleasing, substantial whole. Inside, large sunny rooms with high ceilings and open fireplaces were arranged around a center hall to make a commodious, very livable home. Now financially able to have good horses, Rockefeller built a handsome stone stable for them at the rear of his lot which extended a full block to Prospect Street. The building, far more elaborate than his home, was pine paneled inside with well designed | stalls. It was a worthy headquarters for his trotters with intriguing names like Midnight, Flash Eye, and Truffle. | Influential residents had succeeded in having the horsecars, and _ later the electric trolley cars, detoured off the Avenue from Case to 4 Perry Street. This section of about twenty blocks constituted prac- | tically a private right-of-way for the wealthy landowners. The pave- “., ment, free of car tracks, was as smooth as a table top, perfect for showing off fine horses. Friendly races took place in which Rockefeller participated with his neighbors. None of them had better horses or was more skilled in handling them. No one enjoyed the sport more than he, though he gave less time to it than some. : One of the tales about Rockefeller that went the rounds in Cleve- | Jand concerned a race he had with his longtime friend Eyears. Now a successful merchant tailor and horseman, too, Eyears and his 83
